Cyclic Higgs bundles and minimal surfaces in pseudo-hyperbolic spaces
Xin Nie

Abstract




Abstract.

We introduce a type of minimal surface in the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{n,n}
(with
n
even) or
\mathbb{H}^{n+1,n-1}
(with
n
odd) associated to cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higg bundles. By establishing the infinitesimal rigidity of these surfaces, we get a new proof, for
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
, of Labourie’s theorem that the holonomy map restricts to an immersion on the cyclic locus of Hitchin base, and extend it to Collier’s components. This implies Labourie’s former conjecture in the case of the exceptional group
G_{2}^{\prime}
, for which we also show that these minimal surfaces are
\boldsymbol{J}
-holomorphic curves of a particular type in the almost complex
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
.

1.
Introduction

Let
S_{g}
be the orientable closed surface of genus
g\geq 2
and
G
be a noncompact real semisimple Lie group of rank
\geq 2
.
Higher Teichmüller Theory (see
[
60
]
for a survey) studies fundamental group representations
\rho:\pi_{1}(S_{g})\to G
, especially the
\rho
’s in the
Hitchin components

[
42
]
, the
maximal components

[
14
]
and more recently the
\Theta
-positive components

[
37
,
38
]
.
An important object in the theory are
\rho
-equivariant minimal mappings
f:\widetilde{S}_{g}\to X_{G}
, where
\widetilde{S}_{g}
is the universal cover of
S_{g}

and
X_{G}
is the Riemannian symmetric space associated to
G
. Such an
f
is known to exist as long as
\rho
is
Anosov
(see
[
47
]
), a property shared by all those components.

The uniqueness of
f
is a difficult issue and undergoes extensive studies. Labourie
[
47
]
conjectured that
f
is unique when
\rho
is Hitchin
and proved it when
G
has rank
2
in
[
48
]
. Markovic’s recent result
[
52
]
(see also
[
53
]
) implies that the conjecture fails for hermitian
G
of rank
\geq 3
. The rank
2
case has also been studied by Labourie
[
46
]
, Loftin
[
51
]
, Collier
[
18
]
, Alessandrini-Collier
[
1
]
and Collier-Toulisse-Tholozan
[
21
]
. Most of the existing works are based on investigating certain “incarnation” of
f
as a special surface in other spaces, namely

•

(
[
46
,
51
]
)
hyperbolic affine sphere
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
when
\rho
is an
\mathrm{SL}(3,\mathbb{R})
-Hitchin representation;

•

(
[
21
]
)
maximal surface
in the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
when
\rho
is a maximal
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(2,q+1)
-representation (see also
[
10
,
11
]
for the
q=1
case);

•

(
[
48
]
)
cyclic surface
in a homogeneous space of the complexified group
G^{\mathbb{C}}
when
\rho
is in the cyclic locus of the
G
-Hitchin component (for any real split
G
; see also
[
1
,
18
]
for
\mathrm{Sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
).

In this paper, we define and study yet another incarnation when
G
is
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
or the exceptional simple Lie group
G_{2}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)
: a special type of spacelike (i.e. Riemannian) minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{n,n}
(if
n
is even) or
\mathbb{H}^{n+1,n-1}
(if
n
is odd) with a suitably defined Gauss map that recovers
f
. This generalizes the
n=2
case contained in
[
21
]
, although when
n\geq 3
, like Labourie’s cyclic surfaces, our construction does not apply to all
\rho
in a component of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-representations, but only to those in the cyclic locus. The definition of these minimal surfaces, which we proceed to explain, is related to Chern’s Frenet frame
[
16
]
for minimal spheres and Bryant’s superminimal surfaces
[
12
]
.

1.1.
A-surfaces

For a smooth curve
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{n}
, a classical fact due to Frenet, Serret and Jordan is that if the derivatives
\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime},\cdots,\gamma^{(n-1)}
are linearly independent for all
t
, then there is a unique orthonormal moving frame
t\mapsto(e_{1}(t),\cdots,e_{n}(t))
along
\gamma
such that
e_{1}=\gamma^{\prime}/|\gamma^{\prime}|
is the unit tangent vector and the derivative of the frame takes the form

(1.1)

(e_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,e_{n}^{\prime})=(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})\,\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}0&-\kappa_{2}&&&\\[2.84544pt]
\kappa_{2}&0&-\kappa_{3}&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&\kappa_{3}&0&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&-\kappa_{n}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&\kappa_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}$}

for nowhere vanishing functions
\kappa_{2},\cdots,\kappa_{n}
, which capture the extrinsic geometry of
\gamma
.

Chern
[
16
]
gave an analogue of this theory for minimal surfaces and essentially showed that if
\Sigma
is a minimal sphere in a Riemannian manifold
M
of constant curvature and is not contained in a totally geodesic hypersurface (this forces
M
to be even dimensional), then there is an orthogonal splitting

(1.2)

\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}

with the following properties:

•

each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is a rank
2
vector bundle on
\Sigma
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is the tangent bundle
\mathsf{T}\Sigma\subset\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
;

•

the decomposition of the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
on
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
under (
1.2
) has a similar form as the matrix in (
1.1
), only with the
i
th diagonal zero replaced by a connection on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and the function
\kappa_{j}
replaced by a
1
-form

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{j}))
;

•

every
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}(X):{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
(
j=2,\cdots,n
,
X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma)
) is
conformal
in the sense that it maps any circle in a fiber of
{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}
to a circle in
{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
(possibly reduced to a point).

The proof of Chern makes use of the fact that some naturally defined holomorphic
k
-differentials on
\Sigma
vanish because
\Sigma
is a sphere (a famous idea of Hopf). For
\Sigma
of general topological type, which we consider from now on, the existence of a splitting (
1.2
) with these properties defines a highly special class of minimal surfaces, namely the
superminimal
ones (c.f. Remark
4.5
).

We may generalize this definition of superminimality to the case where
M
is pseudo-Riemannian by further requiring every
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
to be (positive or negative) definite. Relaxing the definition, we may call
\Sigma

quasi-superminimal
if the condition in the last bullet point is only imposed for
j\leq n-1
and not for
j=n
(see Definition
4.1
). In this case, if furthermore
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is positive and negative definite for all odd and even
i
respectively, then we call
\Sigma
an
A-surface
(“A” for the Alternating space-/time-likeness of the
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
’s).
Note that this forces the signature
(p,q)
of
M
to be

(1.3)

(p,q)=\Bigg\{\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{array}[]{ll}(n,n)&\text{if $n$ is even},\\
(n+1,n-1)&\text{if $n$ is odd}.\\
\end{array}

In this paper, we always assume (
1.3
) whenever an A-surface is being considered. We first show:

Theorem A
.

Suppose
M
is orientable and is locally modeled on the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
. Then any oriented A-surface
\Sigma\subset M
satisfies (only rough statements are given here):

(1)

(Theorem
4.6
; see also Remark
4.17
) Each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
in (
1.2
) can be interpreted as a hermitian holomorphic line bundle on
\Sigma
, such that every
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
with
2\leq j\leq n-1
is an
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{j})
-valued holomorphic
1
-form, while

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}
is the sum of holomorphic
1
-forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
with values in
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{n})
and
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}_{n})\cong\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1})
, respectively.

(2)

(Theorem
4.10
) The Gauss-Codazzi equations of
\Sigma
boil down to the following affine Toda system involving the above
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
and the hermitian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}h_{1}-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\tfrac{h_{k}}{h_{k-1}}|\alpha_{k}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{k+1}}{h_{k}}|\alpha_{k+1}|^{2}\quad(2\leq k\leq n-2),\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n-1}=\tfrac{h_{n-1}}{h_{n-2}}|\alpha_{n-1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}\penalty\ ,\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}=\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}.\end{cases}

(3)

(Theorem
4.13
) The Gauss map from
\Sigma
to the symmetric space

X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)}=\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic $q$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{p,q}$}\big\},

defined by assigning to every
z\in\Sigma
the
q
-sphere passing through
z
and tangent to the timelike part
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}:={\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\oplus\cdots
of the normal bundle, is a conformal minimal immersion.

Remark 1.1
.

We need
M
to be oriented in order to endow
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
with an orientation and hence view it as a holomorphic line bundle (but not for
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}
). Therefore, we work with the hyperboloid model of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
in this paper rather than the projective model, as the latter is not orientable in even dimensions. If the orientation of
M
is inverted, so is the one on
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
. This interchanges
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
with
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}_{n}\cong{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}
and interchanges
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
.

The data
{\mathcal{L}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
in
(1)
and the equations in
(2)
resemble those appearing in cyclic Higgs bundles (see
[
5
,
6
,
20
,
24
,
25
]
). We establish next a link between them.

1.2.
Cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles

On a closed Riemann surface
\Sigma
, a
Higgs bundle
is a pair
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
, where
{\mathcal{E}}
is a holomorphic vector bundle and
\Phi
an
\End({\mathcal{E}})
-valued holomorphic
1
-form.
Given a real reductive group
G
, one may define a special class of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
’s called
G
-Higgs bundles
, whose Hitchin connections yield representations of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
in
G
, and the
non-abelian Hodge correspondence
claims that this roughly gives a bijection between the moduli spaces of such Higgs bundles and representations (see
[
41
,
42
,
33
,
31
]
). In this paper, we consider
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
’s of the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n},

\displaystyle\Phi=\scalebox{0.85}{
$\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc|c|ccccc}0&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}&0&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\\
&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&\ddots&&&&&&&&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&\\
&&&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&0&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&&&&\\
&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\ddots&&&\\
&&&&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&&&&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&0&\\
&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}&0\end{array}\right)$
},

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
are holomorphic line bundles with
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
.
The
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
can be brought to
1
by a gauge transformation, but this exact constant is convenient for our purpose (see Remark
1.2
). We further assume that none of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
is identically zero. In this case,
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is stable if and only if either
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\not\equiv 0
or
\deg({\mathcal{L}}_{n})<0
(see Prop./Def.
6.2
).

Such an
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is a
cyclic

\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle, so its harmonic metric splits into hermitian metrics on the
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
’s (see
[
20
, Cor. 2.11]
). Much of our work also relies on the following extra technical assumption on the divisors of the holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
:

(1.4)

({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})\prec\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\big\},

where we write “
D_{1}\prec D_{2}
” if the value of the divisor
D_{1}
is strictly less than that of
D_{2}
at every point where the former is nonzero (which always holds when
D_{1}=\emptyset
). In fact, by a generalization of Dai and Li’s work
[
24
]
, the harmonic metrics on
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}
have semi-negative Chern forms under this assumption (see §
5.1
), and some of our results can be viewed as new geometric applications of this property in addition to those in
[
24
]
.

We show that such
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
’s yield A-surfaces, where (
1.4
)
is needed in our proof of uniqueness:

Theorem B

(Theorem
6.3
)
.

Given a stable
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
as above, with the
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
’s satisfying (
1.4
), let
f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)}
be an equivariant conformal minimal mapping given by the harmonic metric. Then there is a unique antipodal pair
\pm F
of A-surface immersions of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
into
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
such that
f
is given by post-composing
\pm F
with their Gauss map.

Remark 1.2
.

It is also shown that the structural data of
F
in Theorem
A

(1)
(after possibly swapping
\pm F
) coincides with the data used to construct
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
, as the notation already suggests. One may justify this by checking that the equations in Theorem
A

(2)
are exactly the Hitchin equation of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
. The constant
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
in
\Phi
is required for this to be an exact match: otherwise the holomorphic data behind
F
and
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
will differ by constants. Meanwhile, since the antipodal map inverts the orientation of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, by Remark
1.1
, the structural data of
-F
is the same except for a flip of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm 1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
. This is related to a hidden symmetry in the definition of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
: if we interchange
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
with
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}
and interchange
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
, the resulting
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is equivalent to the original one.

The representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma)\to\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
given by the Hitchin connection of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
belongs to the
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Hitchin component when
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
are nowhere zero (we have
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}^{-i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}^{2n})
in this case).
For the slightly broader class of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
’s with only
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
nowhere zero,
\rho
belongs to the component
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
studied by Collier
[
19
]
with
d=|\deg({\mathcal{L}}_{n})|\in[0,(2g-2)n]
(after possibly interchanging
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm 1}
as in Remark
1.2
when
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\not\equiv 0
, we may assume
d=\deg({\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1})
).
As the core results of this paper, we show next that the A-surfaces in Theorem
B
are infinitesimally rigid as minimal surfaces and draw consequences for
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
.

1.3.
Infinitesimal rigidity

It is well known that in a negatively curved pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
, any spacelike minimal submanifold
\Sigma
with timelike normal bundle is locally volume maximizing (see Proposition
3.2
). This fact underlies the works
[
10
,
21
,
49
,
50
]
on minimal surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
and explains the terminology “maximal surface” used in this setting.

When
\Sigma
is an A-surfaces and
M
is modeled on a pseudo-hyperbolic space of dimension
2n\geq 6
, we generalize this fact by showing that if
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}
have semi-negative Chern forms, then
\Sigma
is a saddle type critical point for the area functional: its variations along the spacelike and timelike parts of the normal bundle strictly increase and decrease the area, respectively (Theorem
5.5
). As a consequence,
\Sigma
does not admit any nonzero compactly supported Jacobi field (Corollary
5.8
). Thus, when
\Sigma
is closed, we obtain:

Theorem C

(Corollary
5.9
)
.

In the setting of Theorem
A
, further assume that
\Sigma
is closed and the holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
satisfy (
1.4
). Then
\Sigma
does not admit any nonzero Jacobi field.

Since the Jacobi fields of a minimal surface are the infinitesimal deformations (see §
3.3
), this theorem signifies that
\Sigma
is infinitesimally rigid as a minimal surface.
Therefore, in the same way as how Labourie
[
48
, §7]
uses the infinitesimal rigidity of cyclic surfaces to show that the holonomy map restricts to an immersion on the cyclic locus of the Hitchin base (a vector bundle over the Teichmüller space), we may deduce from Theorem
C
a generalization of Labourie’s theorem to Collier’s component
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
for
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
:

Theorem D

(Theorem
6.6
)
.

Fix
g\geq 2
and
n\geq 3
. Given
1\leq d\leq(2g-2)n
, consider the natural mapping-class-group-equivariant smooth map

\Psi:{\mathcal{H}}_{4}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{6}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{d}\to{\mathcal{X}}_{d}

assigning to each point of the domain fiber bundle the holonomy of the cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle constructed from that point
(see §
6.2
). Then
\Psi
restricts to an immersion on the subbundle
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
.

Remark 1.3
.

For simplicity, we only treat in this paper the case
d>0
, as
\Psi
is a smooth map between smooth manifolds in this case. When
d=0
, the domain is more complicated and has both orbifold and non-orbifold singularities (see
[
1
,
19
]
), but Theorem
D
and its proof still hold on the smooth locus.

The following cases of Theorem
D
are already known:

•

The
d=(2g-2)n
case is the original theorem of Labourie
[
48
]
. In this case,
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
is just the Hitchin component
{\mathcal{X}}_{\scalebox{0.5}{Hitchin}}(\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1))
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}={\mathcal{H}}_{2n}
is the cyclic locus of the Hitchin base.

•

When
n=2
, Theorem
D
still holds by virtue of the maximality (or the global uniqueness result
[
21
, Thm. 3.13]
), but the statement and proof are already contained in
[
1
,
18
,
21
]
. In this case, the domain of
\Psi
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
alone, and by an argument of Labourie, we can use a differential-topological result
[
48
, Thm. 8.1.1]
, in combination with the relation between
\Psi
and the energy functional
[
47
]
, to infer that
\Psi
is a diffeomorphism.

•

The first case above with
n=3
is special: in this case,
\Psi
sends
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
into the Hitchin component
{\mathcal{X}}_{\scalebox{0.5}{Hitchin}}(G_{2}^{\prime})
of the exceptional group
G_{2}^{\prime}
(see below), which is contained in
{\mathcal{X}}_{\scalebox{0.5}{Hitchin}}(\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4))
. The same argument as the last case shows that
\Psi
is a diffeomorphism from
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
to
{\mathcal{X}}_{\scalebox{0.5}{Hitchin}}(G_{2}^{\prime})
.

The diffeomorphisms in the latter two cases prove Labourie’s conjecture for the rank
2
groups
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(2,3)
and
G_{2}^{\prime}
, while it remains elusive whether
\Psi
is a diffeomorphism in general. Our final result is about the nature of the A-surfaces occurring in the last case.

1.4.
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representations

The rank
2
exceptional complex simple Lie group
G_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
has two real forms: the compact one
G_{2}
and the split one
G_{2}^{\prime}
. The latter identifies with the group of isometries of
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
preserving a specific non-integrable almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
(see §
2.6
).

An interesting subclass of A-surfaces in this almost complex
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
are the
spacelike
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves with nowhere vanishing second fundamental form and timelike osculation lines
(where
osculation line
refers to the
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-complex line in a normal space formed by the images of second fundamental form; see §
3.4
). The Gauss map of such an A-surface takes values in

X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}=\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic ${\boldsymbol{J}}$-holomorphic $2$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{4,2}$}\big\}\subset X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(4,3)}

(see §
4.5
).
We show that the A-surfaces from Theorem
B
belong to this subclass when
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is in the
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin component (Proposition
6.8
). The infinitesimal rigidity of such A-surfaces imply Labourie’s conjecture for
G_{2}^{\prime}
, as explained in the last subsection.
The outcome can be summarized as:

Corollary E
.

For any Hitchin representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(S_{g})\to G_{2}^{\prime}
, there exist

•

a unique orientation-preserving
\rho
-equivariant immersion of
\widetilde{S}_{g}
into
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
as a
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve with nowhere vanishing second fundamental form and timelike osculation lines; and

•

a unique
\rho
-equivariant minimal immersion of
\widetilde{S}_{g}
into the symmetric space
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}
.

The latter is given by post-composing the former with its Gauss map, which assigns to every point of the
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve the
2
-sphere generated by the osculation line at that point.

It was Baraglia
[
5
, §3.6]
who first observed that Higgs bundles in the
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin component yield
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves in
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
. The above statement gives a more complete picture.

Figure 1.1.
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic A-surface in
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}

Figure
1.1
is a cartoon of the theorem, where we use the familiar drawing for the anti-de Sitter space
\mathbb{H}^{2,1}
to depict
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
schematically and show the A-surface
\Sigma
as a “Frenet curve”.
The ideal boundary
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
carries, besides the conformal metric of signature
(3,2)
induced by the metric of
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, a non-integrable
2
-plane distribution induced by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
(see
[
4
,
55
]
). Therefore, a natural question is how the frontier of
\Sigma
in
\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
(depicted as two dots in Figure
1.1
, but should actually be a Jordan curve) behaves with respect to these structures. Another intriguing problem is to geometrize
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin representations by using
\Sigma
in a similar way as what
[
21
]
did with maximal surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
.

Finally, we note that the recent work of Collier and Toulisse
[
22
]
, independent of the current paper, uses cyclic surfaces to study spacelike
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves in
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
coming from
G_{2}^{\prime}
-representations that are not necessarily Hitchin.

Organization of the paper

In §
2
and §
3
, we review backgrounds on pseudo-hyperbolic spaces, the Lie group
G_{2}^{\prime}
and minimal submanifolds. In §
4
, we discuss the definition of A-surfaces and establish their fundamental properties, namely Theorem
A
. In §
5
, we prove the infinitesimal rigidity result, Theorem
C
, with a crucial ingredient (Theorem
5.2
) postponed to the appendix. In §
6
, we relate Higgs bundles to A-surfaces by proving Theorem
B
, and finally use the infinitesimal rigidity to deduce Theorem
D
and show that the
G_{2}^{\prime}
case yields
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves.

2.
Pseudo-hyperbolic spaces and
G_{2}^{\prime}

In this section, we review the necessary backgrounds on the pseduo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, the Lie group
G_{2}^{\prime}
and the almost complex structure on
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
.

2.1.
Pseudo-hyperbolic spaces

Let
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
denote
\mathbb{R}^{p+q+1}
endowed with the quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
given by

\langle x,x\rangle:=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{p}^{2}-x_{p+1}^{2}-\cdots-x_{p+q+1}^{2}.

The
pseudo-hyperbolic space

\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
is defined as the hypersurface

\mathbb{H}^{p,q}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}\mid\langle x,x\rangle=-1\big\}

endowed with the metric restricted from
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, which is a pseudo-Riemannian metric of signature
(p,q)
with constant sectional curvature
-1
. In particular,
\mathbb{H}^{p,0}
is the two-sheeted hyperboloid, namely two copies of the usual hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{p}
, while
\mathbb{H}^{0,q}
is the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{q}
with metric multiplied by
-1
. Topologically,
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
identifies with the product of the
p
-dimensional ball with the
q
-sphere through the diffeomorphism

\mathbb{H}^{p,q}\cong\mathbb{B}^{p}\times\mathbb{S}^{q},\quad x\mapsto\Bigg(\frac{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{p})}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\sqrt{x_{p+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{p+q+1}^{2}}$}}\,,\ \frac{(x_{p+1},\cdots,x_{p+q+1})}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\sqrt{x_{p+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{p+q+1}^{2}}$}}\Bigg).

Remark 2.1
.

More commonly used in the recent literature (e.g.
[
10
,
21
,
49
,
50
]
) is the
projective model
of the pseudo-hyperbolic space. It identifies with the quotient of our hyperboloid model
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
by the antipodal map
x\mapsto-x
. The crucial advantage of the
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
here is that it is orientable, while the quotient
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}/(x\sim-x)
with
q\geq 1
is orientable only when
p+q
is odd.

A linear subspace
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
is said to be
nondegnerate
or
positive/negative definite
if the quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{L}
is.
The intersection of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
with some nondegenerate
L
is called a
pseudo-hyperbolic subspace
, which is a totally geodesic copy of
\mathbb{H}^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}
in
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
(
(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}+1)
being the signature of
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{L}
). We refer to the copies of
\mathbb{H}^{0,n}
in
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
as
timelike totally geodesic
n
-spheres
(when
n=1
, these are the complete timelike geodesics).
In particular, all the timelike totally geodesic
q
-spheres are homotopic to
\{0\}\times\mathbb{S}^{q}\subset\mathbb{B}^{p}\times\mathbb{S}^{q}\cong\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, hence can be oriented in a uniform way (corresponding to a choice of generator for the
q
th homology
H_{q}(\mathbb{H}^{p,q},\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}
).

The automorphism group
\mathrm{O}(p,q+1)
of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
acts on
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
as the isometry group. It has
4
connected components. The index
2
subgroup
\mathrm{SO}(p,q+1)
consists of the orientation-preserving isometries, whereas the identity component
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
consists of the isometries preserving the above orientation of timelike totally geodesic
q
-spheres (or equivalently, acting trivially on homology).

Let
e_{1}=(1,0,\cdots,0),\cdots,e_{p+q+1}=(0,\cdots,0,1)
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
and put

V:=\mathbb{R}e_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}e_{p}\,,\quad W:=\mathbb{R}e_{p+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}e_{p+q+1}.

These are maximal positive and negative definite subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
orthogonal to each other. The stabilizer of either
V
or
W
in
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
, namely

\mathrm{SO}(V)\times\mathrm{SO}(W)=\mathrm{SO}(p)\times\mathrm{SO}(q+1)\subset\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1),

is a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
. Meanwhile, the
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
-action on the space of maximal (i.e.
(q+1)
-dimensional) negative definite subspaces is transitive. Thus, the Riemannian symmetric space of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
can be described as

\displaystyle X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)}:=

\displaystyle\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)\big/\big(\mathrm{SO}(p)\times\mathrm{SO}(q+1)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\text{$(q+1)$-dimensional negative definite subspaces of $\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}$}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic $q$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{p,q}$}\big\}.

The
pseudosphere

\mathbb{S}^{q,p}
is the counter part of pseudo-hyperbolic spaces defined by

\mathbb{S}^{q,p}:=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{q+1,p}\mid\langle x,x\rangle=1\big\}.

It has curvature
+1
and is clearly
anti-isometric
to
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
: there is an obvious diffeomorphism
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}\cong\mathbb{S}^{q,p}
identifying the metric of the former with
-1
times that of the latter. We sometimes use
\mathbb{S}^{q,p}
with metric multiplied by
-1
as an alternative model for
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
.

2.2.
Split octonions

The unit
6
-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{6}
is known to carry a natural almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
coming from the algebra
\mathbf{O}
of octonions. The group
G_{2}\subset\mathrm{SO}(7)
of the isometries of
\mathbb{S}^{6}
preserving
{\boldsymbol{J}}
, which is also the automorphism group of
\mathbf{O}
, is a maximal compact subgroup of the
14
-dimensional complex simple Lie group
G_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
.
A close analogue holds for the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
: it carries an almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
coming from the
split octonions

\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
, whose automorphism group

G_{2}^{\prime}=\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{O}^{\prime})=\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{H}^{4,2},{\boldsymbol{J}})\subset\mathrm{SO}_{0}(4,3)

is the split real form of
G_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. We now briefly review the constructions.

The split octonions
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
is the (noncommutative and nonassociative) algebra over
\mathbb{R}
generated as a vector space by the symbols
1,\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{l},\boldsymbol{i}\boldsymbol{l},\boldsymbol{j}\boldsymbol{l},\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{l}
, such that
1
is the identity and the products of the other basis vectors are given by Table
1
.

Table 1.
Multiplication table of
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
.

Thus,
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
is an extension of the algebras of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\boldsymbol{i}
and quaternions
\mathbf{H}=\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\boldsymbol{i}\oplus\mathbb{R}\boldsymbol{j}\oplus\mathbb{R}\boldsymbol{k}
. The conjugation
x\mapsto\overline{x}
(i.e. the linear involution fixing
1
and sending any other basis vector to the opposite) and the notion of real and imaginary parts
\re(x):=\frac{x+\overline{x}}{2},\im(x):=\frac{x-\overline{x}}{2}

naturally extend from
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
, and so does the quadratic form

\langle x,y\rangle:=\re(x\overline{y})=\tfrac{1}{2}(x\overline{y}+y\overline{x}).

The conjugation and the quadratic form are still compatible with the multiplication in the sense that

(2.1)

\overline{xy}=\overline{y}\ \overline{x},\quad\langle xy,xy\rangle=\langle x,x\rangle\langle y,y\rangle.

However,
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
is not positive definite on
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
: we have

\langle x,x\rangle=x_{0}^{2}+x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}-x_{4}^{2}-x_{5}^{2}-x_{6}^{2}-x_{7}^{2}.

for
x=x_{0}+x_{1}\boldsymbol{i}+x_{2}\boldsymbol{j}+x_{3}\boldsymbol{k}+x_{4}\boldsymbol{l}+x_{5}\boldsymbol{i}\boldsymbol{l}+x_{6}\boldsymbol{j}\boldsymbol{l}+x_{7}\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{l}\in\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
. It follows that the imaginary split octonions

\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}:=\big\{x\in\mathbf{O}^{\prime}\mid\re(x)=0\big\}=1^{\perp},

endowed with the restriction of the quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
,
identifies with
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
.

2.3.
The cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}

The Lie group
G_{2}^{\prime}
can be defined as the group
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{O}^{\prime})
of algebra automorphisms of
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
. However, instead of working with
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
, we shall rather focus on
\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
and the cross product “
\times
” defined on it by

x\times y:=\im(xy)=\tfrac{1}{2}(xy-yx)\quad\text{ for all }x,y\in\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}

(the last equality is because
\overline{x}=-x
for any
x\in\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
). This is a skew-symmetric bilinear operation extending the familiar cross product on the Euclidean
3
-space and satisfying the same identities as the latter with respect to the quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
(see Lemma
2.2
below).

It is useful to notice that for any
x,y\in\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

\langle x,y\rangle=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ xy=-yx\ \Leftrightarrow\ \re(xy)=0\ \Leftrightarrow\ x\times y=xy.

As a consequence, we get from Table
1
the table
below for the cross product of the basis vectors
\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j},\cdots,\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{l}\in\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
, re-denoted here by

e_{1},\cdots,e_{7}
.

Table 2.
The cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}

We may take this table as the definition of the cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
without involving the algebra
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
. The main properties of this cross product are:

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\langle x\times y,x\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\langle x\times y,z\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle y\times z,x\rangle,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\langle x\times y,x\times y\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle x,x\rangle\langle y,y\rangle-\langle x,y\rangle^{2},

(2.5)

\displaystyle x\times(x\times y)

\displaystyle=-\langle x,x\rangle y+\langle x,y\rangle x.

Moreover, if
x
is orthogonal to both
y
and
z
, then
we further have

(2.6)

x\times(y\times z)=-y\times(x\times z).

Proof.

These actually hold in a more general setting: Given an algebra
\mathbb{A}
(with identity) over
\mathbb{R}
endowed with a nondegenerate quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
satisfying (
2.1
), one may consider the cross product
\times
on
\im\mathbb{A}:=1^{\perp}
defined in the same way as here. In the survey
[
44
]
, it is shown in Lemma 3.39, Prop. 3.33 (see also Def. 3.34 and Eq.(3.45)) and Cor. 3.49, respectively, that (
2.2
), (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) hold in this setting (although
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
is assumed to be positive define in
[
44
]
, nondegeneracy is adequate for these identities). Also,
[
44
, Cor. 3.49]
shows that for any
x,y,z\in\im\mathbb{A}
we have

x\times(y\times z)=-\langle x,y\rangle z+\langle x,z\rangle y-\tfrac{1}{2}[x,y,z],

where the
associator

[x,y,z]:=(xy)z-x(yz)
is known to be skew-symmetric in
x
,
y
and
z
(this means that
\mathbb{A}
is an
alternative
algebra; see
[
44
, Prop. 3.33]
). This implies (
2.5
) and (
2.6
).
∎

Finally, note that the cross product is encoded in the
3
-form
\varphi\in\bigwedge^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3,4})^{*}
defined by

\varphi(x,y,z):=\langle x\times y,z\rangle

(it is skew-symmetric in
x
,
y
and
z
by (
2.3
)). Using Table
2
and noting that
\langle e_{i},e_{i}\rangle=1
(resp.
-1
) for
i=1,2,3
(resp.
4,5,6,7
) and
\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle=0
for
i\neq j
, we get the explicit expression

(2.7)

\varphi=e^{123}-e^{145}+e^{167}-e^{246}-e^{257}-e^{347}+e^{356},

where
(e^{1},\cdots,e^{7})
is the basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{3,4})^{*}
dual to
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7})
and we write
e^{ijk}:=e^{i}\wedge e^{j}\wedge e^{k}
.

2.4.
The Lie group
G_{2}^{\prime}

It is known that the quadratic form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and the cross product
\times
on
\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
completely capture the algebraic structure of
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
in the sense that
the natural homomorphism from
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbf{O}^{\prime})
to the group of linear transformations of
\im\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
preserving
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and
\times
is an isomorphism. Therefore, we may henceforth forget about
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
and use the following alternative definition of
G_{2}^{\prime}
:

Definition 2.3
.

G_{2}^{\prime}
is the group of all
A\in\mathrm{O}(3,4)
whose action on
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
preserves the cross product
\times
(or equivalently, preserves the
3
-form
\varphi
; see also Remark
2.7
below). Vectors
\widetilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{e}_{7}\in\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
are said to form a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-basis
if they are successively the columns of such an
A
(or in other words,
\widetilde{e}_{i}=A(e_{i})
for the standard basis
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7})
of
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
).

The following proposition and corollary give more concrete descriptions of
G_{2}^{\prime}
and its Lie algebra:

Proposition 2.4
.

Vectors
\widetilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{e}_{7}\in\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
are a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-basis if and only if they satisfy:

(a)

\widetilde{e}_{1}
,
\widetilde{e}_{2}
and
\widetilde{e}_{4}
are orthogonal to each other and moreover

\langle\widetilde{e}_{1},\widetilde{e}_{1}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{e}_{2},\widetilde{e}_{2}\rangle=1,\quad\langle\widetilde{e}_{4},\widetilde{e}_{4}\rangle=-1,\quad\langle\widetilde{e}_{1}\times\widetilde{e}_{2},\widetilde{e}_{4}\rangle=0;

(b)

the other
\widetilde{e}_{i}
’s are determined from
\widetilde{e}_{1}
,
\widetilde{e}_{2}
and
\widetilde{e}_{4}
by

\widetilde{e}_{3}=\widetilde{e}_{1}\times\widetilde{e}_{2},\quad\widetilde{e}_{5}=\widetilde{e}_{1}\times\widetilde{e}_{4},\quad\widetilde{e}_{6}=\widetilde{e}_{2}\times\widetilde{e}_{4},\quad\widetilde{e}_{7}=\widetilde{e}_{3}\times\widetilde{e}_{4}.

Proof.

Being a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-basis means that the endomorphism
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
with
A(e_{i})=\widetilde{e}_{i}
satisfies:

•

\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
-preserving
\Leftrightarrow

\langle\widetilde{e}_{i},\widetilde{e}_{i}\rangle=1
(resp.
-1
) for
i=1,2,3
(resp.
4,5,6,7
) and
\langle\widetilde{e}_{i},\widetilde{e}_{j}\rangle=0
for
i\neq j
;

•

\times
-preserving
\Leftrightarrow
the cross product
\widetilde{e}_{i}\times\widetilde{e}_{j}
matches Table
2
(with each
e_{i}
replaced by
\widetilde{e}_{i}
).

These properties clearly imply conditions
(a)
and
(b)
. Conversely, if
(a)
and
(b)
hold, it is straightforward to verify the first bullet point and then the second by using the identities in Lemma
2.2
.
∎

Corollary 2.5
.

G_{2}^{\prime}
is connected (hence contained in the identity component
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)
of
\mathrm{O}(3,4)
) and its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{so}(2,3)
consists of matrices of the following form, with
a_{1},\cdots,a_{6},b_{1},\cdots,b_{6},c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
:

\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc|cccc}0&a_{1}&a_{2}&b_{4}&c_{1}&b_{1}&b_{2}\\[2.84544pt]
-a_{1}&0&a_{3}&b_{5}&b_{1}-b_{6}&c_{2}&b_{3}\\[2.84544pt]
-a_{2}&-a_{3}&0&b_{6}&b_{2}+b_{5}&b_{3}-b_{4}&-c_{1}-c_{2}\\
\hline\cr b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}&0&a_{4}&a_{5}&a_{6}\\[5.69046pt]
c_{1}&b_{1}-b_{6}&b_{2}+b_{5}&-a_{4}&0&a_{1}+a_{6}&a_{2}-a_{5}\\[2.84544pt]
b_{1}&c_{2}&b_{3}-b_{4}&-a_{5}&-a_{1}-a_{6}&0&a_{3}+a_{4}\\[2.84544pt]
b_{2}&b_{3}&-c_{1}-c_{2}&-a_{6}&-a_{2}+a_{5}&-a_{3}-a_{4}&0\end{array}\right)$}.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.4
,
G_{2}^{\prime}
is diffeomorphic to the space of triples
(\widetilde{e}_{1},\widetilde{e}_{2},\widetilde{e}_{4})
in
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
satisfying Condition
(a)
, which is a sphere bundle over the space of orthonormal pairs
(\widetilde{e}_{1},\widetilde{e}_{2})
. The latter space is in turn an
\mathbb{S}^{1,4}
-bundle over
\mathbb{S}^{2,4}
, hence connected. The description of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
can be deduced from Proposition
2.4
by differentiating the relations therein at the identity
(\widetilde{e}_{1},\cdots,\widetilde{e}_{7})=(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7})
. We omit the detailed calculations.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

The compact group
G_{2}
is given by an almost identical construction as above except for some changes of signs. Namely, to get
G_{2}
instead of
G_{2}^{\prime}
, it suffices to replace the split octonions
\mathbf{O}^{\prime}
by the octonions
\mathbf{O}
, whose multiplication is given by Table
1
with the signs in the lower-right
4\times 4
block inverted; or we may consider the corresponding cross product on the Euclidean space
\mathbb{E}^{7}\cong\im\mathbf{O}
.

Remark 2.7
.

Although we defined
G_{2}^{\prime}
as the group of linear transformations
A\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{R}^{3,4})
preserving both the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and the
3
-form
\varphi
, it is a nontrivial fact that if
A
preserves
\varphi
, then it automatically preserves
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
. This can be proved by showing directly, with cumbersome computations (best done with a computer algebra system), that if a matrix
X\in\mathfrak{gl}_{7}\mathbb{R}
preserves
\varphi
infinitesimally, then it has the form given in Corollary
2.5
. Alternatively, a conceptual proof of the analogous fact for
G_{2}
is given in
[
13
]
(see also
[
44
, Theorem 4.4]
), which can be adapted to
G_{2}^{\prime}
. This fact is of fundamental importance in
G_{2}
-geometry (see
[
44
, Remark 4.10]
).

Remark 2.8
.

By the last remark,
G_{2}
and
G_{2}^{\prime}
are the subgroups of
\mathrm{GL}(7,\mathbb{R})
preserving two specific
3
-forms on
\mathbb{R}^{7}
respectively. This is related to the fact that the
\mathrm{GL}(7,\mathbb{R})
-action on
\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{R}^{7*}
has exactly two open orbits (see
[
55
, p.332]
): those two
3
-forms belong to the two orbits respectively.

2.5.
Maximal compact subgroup

It is known that
G_{2}^{\prime}
is a simple Lie group, and
if we consider the orthogonal splitting

\mathbb{R}^{3,4}=V\oplus W
from §
2.1
,
then the intersection (between subgroups of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)
)

K:=G_{2}^{\prime}\cap\big(\mathrm{SO}(V)\times\mathrm{SO}(W)\big)

is a maximal compact subgroup.
In order to describe
K
more concretely, we use the
3
-form
\varphi
from §
2.3
to identify every
v\in V
as a
2
-form on
W
via the map

(2.8)

V\to\textstyle{\bigwedge^{2}}W^{*},\quad v\mapsto(v\lrcorner\varphi)|_{W}.

In particular, the basis vectors
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
of
V
correspond to the
2
-forms

(2.9)

\left\{\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{array}[]{l}e_{1}\mapsto-e^{45}+e^{67},\\
e_{2}\mapsto-e^{46}-e^{57},\\
e_{3}\mapsto-e^{47}+e^{56},\end{array}\right.

where we view
(e^{4},e^{5},e^{6},e^{7})
as a basis of
W^{*}
and set
e^{ij}:=e^{i}\wedge e^{j}
. By a well known linear-algebraic construction, the Hodge star
\star:\bigwedge^{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}W^{*}\to\bigwedge^{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}W^{*}
(defined under the Euclidean metric
-\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{W}
) is an involution on
\bigwedge^{2}W^{*}
and the
2
-forms in (
2.9
) are an orthonormal basis for the
(-1)
-eigenspace
\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*}\subset\bigwedge^{2}W^{*}
of
\star
(whose elements are known as
anti-self-dual

2
-forms).
Therefore, the map (
2.8
) induces a natural identification

V\cong\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*}
. It enables us to describe
K
as follows:

Proposition 2.9
.

Consider the homomorphism
\varrho:\mathrm{SO}(W)\to\mathrm{SO}(V)
given by the natural action of
\mathrm{SO}(W)
on
V\cong\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*}
. Then we have

K=\big\{(\varrho(A_{W}),A_{W})\mid A_{W}\in\mathrm{SO}(W)\big\}\cong\mathrm{SO}(4).

Proof.

By definition,
A\in\mathrm{O}(3,4)
is in
G_{2}^{\prime}
if and only if

(2.10)

\varphi(A(e_{i}),A(e_{j}),A(e_{k}))=\varphi(e_{i},e_{j},e_{k})\ \text{ for all }1\leq i<j<k\leq 7.

Now assume
A=(A_{V},A_{W})\in\mathrm{SO}(V)\times\mathrm{SO}(W)
. By the expression (
2.7
) of
\varphi
, we infer that

•

if
(i,j,k)=(1,2,3)
, then both sides of (
2.10
) are equal to
1
;

•

if
i\leq 3
and
j,k\geq 4
, then the left-hand side of (
2.10
) can be rewritten as
\varphi(A_{V}(e_{i}),A_{W}(e_{j}),A_{W}(e_{k}))
;

•

if
(i,j,k)
is in neither of the above cases, then both sides of (
2.10
) vanish.

As a consequence, such an
A
belongs to
K
if and only if

(2.11)

\varphi(A_{V}(v),A_{W}(w_{1}),A_{W}(w_{2}))=\varphi(v,w_{1},w_{2})\ \text{ for all }v\in V,\ w_{1},w_{2}\in W.

On the other hand, by the definition (
2.8
) of the identification
V\cong\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*}
, we have
A_{V}=\varrho(A_{W})
if and only if

\big(A_{V}(v)\lrcorner\varphi\big)\big|_{W}=A_{W}^{*}(v\lrcorner\varphi)|_{W}
for all
v\in V
,
or equivalently,

\varphi(A_{V}(v),w_{1},w_{2})=\varphi(v,A_{W}^{-1}(w_{1}),A_{W}^{-1}(w_{2}))\ \text{ for all }v\in V,\ w_{1},w_{2}\in W.

This is equivalent to (
2.11
), so the required statement follows.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

There are well known exceptional isomorphisms

\mathrm{SO}(4)\cong\big(\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2)\big)\big/\{\pm I\}\cong\mathrm{SU}(2)\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(3).

The first one results from quaternions, while the second is given by the diagonal
\mathrm{SO}(3)\cong\mathrm{SU}(2)/\{\pm I\}
in
(\mathrm{SU}(2)\times\mathrm{SU}(2))/\{\pm I\}
and the first component
\mathrm{SU}(2)
. The homomorphism
\varrho
in Proposition
2.9
is essentially the projection
\mathrm{SO}(4)\cong\mathrm{SU}(2)\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(3)\to\mathrm{SO}(3)
whose kernel is
\mathrm{SU}(2)
.

Remark 2.11
.

By using the matrix representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
in Corollary
2.5
, it can be checked that
the subspace
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
in the Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
(where
\mathfrak{k}\cong\mathfrak{so}(4)
is the Lie algebra of
K
) is isomorphic, as a
K
-module, to the subspace
\mathrm{Hom}_{0}(W,V):=\big\{T\in\mathrm{Hom}(W,V)\,\big|\,\Tr_{12}(T)=0\big\}
of

\mathrm{Hom}(W,V)\cong\mathrm{Hom}(W,\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*})=W^{*}\otimes\bigwedge^{2}_{-}W^{*}\subset W^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes W^{*}
, where
\Tr_{12}
is defined by

\Tr_{12}:W^{*}\otimes W^{*}\otimes W^{*}\to W^{*},\quad T({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\mapsto\sum_{i=4}^{7}T(e_{i},e_{i},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})

(the contraction of the first two slots of any
3
-tensor on
W
with the metric
-\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{W}
).

2.6.
The almost complex
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
and the symmetric space
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}

The cross product on
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
induces an almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
on the pseudosphere
\mathbb{S}^{2,4}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3,4}\mid\langle x,x\rangle=1\}
by

{\boldsymbol{J}}(X):=x\times X\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{S}^{2,4},\ X\in\mathsf{T}_{x}\mathbb{S}^{2,4}\cong x^{\perp}.

Using equations (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) in Lemma
2.2
, one may check that
{\boldsymbol{J}}
is indeed an almost complex structure (i.e.
{\boldsymbol{J}}
preserves
x^{\perp}
and satisfies
{\boldsymbol{J}}^{2}=-\mathit{id}
) and is
orthogonal
with respect to the pseudo-Riemannian metric in the sense that it is an isometry of each
\mathsf{T}_{x}\mathbb{S}^{2,4}
. Moreover, it is clear that the antipodal map
\tau(x)=-x
of
\mathbb{S}^{4,2}
is
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-anti-holomorphic, namely
\mathsf{d}\tau\circ{\boldsymbol{J}}=-{\boldsymbol{J}}\circ\mathsf{d}\tau
.

It is known that
{\boldsymbol{J}}
is non-integrable and has the property (c.f. §
3.4
below)

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y=-(\nabla_{Y}{\boldsymbol{J}})X\quad\text{ for all }X,Y\in\mathsf{T}\mathbb{S}^{2,4},

where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbb{S}^{2,4}
. In fact, it can be shown by computations that

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y=(X\times Y)^{x^{\perp}}\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{S}^{2,4}\text{ and }X,Y\in\mathsf{T}_{x}\mathbb{S}^{4,2}\cong x^{\perp},

where
(y)^{x^{\perp}}:=y-\langle x,y\rangle x
is the
x^{\perp}
-component of
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
under the splitting
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}=x\mathbb{R}\oplus x^{\perp}
.

We henceforth consider
(\mathbb{S}^{2,4},{\boldsymbol{J}})
with metric multiplied by
-1
and call it
the almost complex
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
(see the last paragraph of §
2.1
).
In this model of
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, timelike totally geodesic
2
-spheres
S\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
correspond to
3
-dimensional positive definite subspaces
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
via the relation
S=V^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
. The almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
singles out a subclass of such spheres, namely the
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic ones:

Lemma 2.12
.

For any timelike totally geodesic
2
-sphere
S=V^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, where
V^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
is a
3
-dimensional positive definite subspace, the following conditions are equivalent to each other:

(i)

\mathsf{T}_{x}S\subset\mathsf{T}_{x}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
is preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
for some
x\in S
;

(ii)

S
is a
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve (i.e.
\mathsf{T}_{x}S
is preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
for all
x\in S
);

(iii)

V^{\prime}
is closed under the cross product operation.

Proof.

The implication “
(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)
” is trivial and “
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
” follows easily from the definition of
{\boldsymbol{J}}
. To show “
(i)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
”, we assume
(i)
and take an orthonormal basis
(y,z)
of
\mathsf{T}_{x}S\cong V^{\prime}\cap x^{\perp}
such that
z={\boldsymbol{J}}y=x\times y
and
y=-{\boldsymbol{J}}z=-x\times z
. Then
V^{\prime}
is spanned by
(x,y,z)
, whereas
x\times y=z,x\times z=-y
and
y\times z=-(x\times z)\times z=x
(see (
2.5
) in Lemma
2.2
) are all in
V^{\prime}
, hence
(iii)
holds.
∎

The most obvious instance of
V^{\prime}
satisfying
(iii)
is the above
V=\mathbb{R}e_{1}\oplus\mathbb{R}e_{2}\oplus\mathbb{R}e_{3}
, whose stabilizer in
G_{2}^{\prime}
is the maximal compact subgroup
K\cong\mathrm{SO}(4)
. Meanwhile, we have:

Proposition 2.13
.

G_{2}^{\prime}
acts transitively on the space of
3
-dimensional positive definite subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
that are closed under the cross product.

Proof.

Let
V^{\prime}
be any such subspace. Pick an orthonormal pair of vectors
(\widetilde{e}_{1},\widetilde{e}_{2})
in
V^{\prime}
and a vector
\widetilde{e}_{4}\in(V^{\prime})^{\perp}
with
\langle\widetilde{e}_{4},\widetilde{e}_{4}\rangle=-1
. Then
\widetilde{e}_{1}
,
\widetilde{e}_{2}
and
\widetilde{e}_{4}
satisfy Condition
(a)
in Proposition
2.4
, hence there exists
A\in G_{2}^{\prime}
sending the basis
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
of
V
to the basis
\widetilde{e}_{1},\widetilde{e}_{2},\widetilde{e}_{3}:=\widetilde{e}_{1}\times\widetilde{e}_{2}
of
V^{\prime}
. Therefore,
V^{\prime}
is conjugate to
V
by the
G_{2}^{\prime}
-action.
∎

Thus, we obtain the following description for the symmetric space of
G_{2}^{\prime}
:

Corollary 2.14
.

The Riemannian symmetric space
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}:=G_{2}^{\prime}/K
is naturally identified as

\displaystyle X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\big\{\text{3d positive definite subspaces of $\mathbb{R}^{3,4}$ closed under cross product}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic ${\boldsymbol{J}}$-holomorphic $2$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{4,2}$}\big\}

and is a totally geodesic submanifold of
X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)}=\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic $2$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{4,2}$}\big\}
.

Here, the claim that
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}
is totally geodesic in
X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)}
can be shown by using the criterion
[
40
, Chapt. IV, Thm. 7.2]
for totally geodesic submanifolds in symmetric spaces.

2.7.
A matrix representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}

Let us turn to the complex Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.
In view of the matrix representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
in Corollary
2.5
, we may identify
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
as the algebra of complex matrices of the same form. However, with this form, it is difficult to single out a Cartan subalgebra and a principal
3
-dimensional subalgebra, which are important for Higgs bundles. We now exhibit another matrix representation which makes these subalgebras explicit (at the cost of making the real form
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
implicit and dependent on a hermitian metric):

Proposition 2.15
.

Consider the complex
3
-form
\varpi
on
\mathbb{C}^{7}
given by

\varpi:=\boldsymbol{i}\big(\varepsilon^{147}+\varepsilon^{246}-\varepsilon^{345}-\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,\varepsilon^{156}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon^{237}\big)\in\textstyle{\bigwedge^{3}}\mathbb{C}^{7*}

(the factor
\boldsymbol{i}
is inessential here but needed for Proposition
2.16
), where we let
(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{7})
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
,
(\varepsilon^{1},\cdots,\varepsilon^{7})
be the dual basis of
\mathbb{C}^{7*}
, and write
\varepsilon^{ijk}:=\varepsilon^{i}\wedge\varepsilon^{j}\wedge\varepsilon^{k}
. Then
an endomorphism
X\in\mathfrak{gl}_{7}\mathbb{C}
of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
preserves
\varpi
infinitesimally if and only if it has the form

(2.12)

X=\scalebox{0.9}{$\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc|c|ccc}t_{1}+t_{2}&x_{1}&x_{2}&x_{3}&x_{4}&x_{5}&0\\[7.11317pt]
y_{1}&t_{1}&x_{6}&\scalebox{0.8}{$-2\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{2}&\scalebox{0.8}{$-\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{3}&0&x_{5}\\[7.11317pt]
y_{2}&y_{6}&t_{2}&\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{1}&0&\scalebox{0.8}{$-\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{3}&-x_{4}\\[2.84544pt]
\hline\cr y_{3}&-\tfrac{y_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}&\tfrac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{1}&\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,x_{2}&x_{3}\\
\hline\cr y_{4}&-\tfrac{y_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&0&\tfrac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{2}}&-t_{2}&x_{6}&-x_{2}\\[7.11317pt]
y_{5}&0&-\tfrac{y_{3}}{2\sqrt{2}}&\tfrac{y_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}&y_{6}&-t_{1}&x_{1}\\[7.11317pt]
0&y_{5}&-y_{4}&y_{3}&-y_{2}&y_{1}&-t_{1}-t_{2}\end{array}\right)$}

with
t_{1},t_{2},x_{1},\cdots,x_{6},y_{1},\cdots,y_{6}\in\mathbb{C}
.
The Lie algebra formed by all such
X
’s is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and is contained in the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(Q)\cong\mathfrak{so}(7,\mathbb{C})
infinitesimally preserving the quadratic form

Q:=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&&&&&&-1\\
&&&&&1&\\
&&&&-1&&\\
&&&1&&&\\
&&-1&&&&\\
&1&&&&&\\
-1&&&&&&\end{pmatrix}$}.

Proof.

The first statement can be checked by direct computations with a computer algebra software. Alternatively, we may use the explicit conjugation in the proof of Proposition
2.16
below to bring the
3
-form
\varphi
considered earlier to
\varpi
and check that the conjugation brings the matrices in Corollary
2.5
to the ones here. We omit the details. The fact that
X\in\mathfrak{so}(Q)
is checked by a simple computation. Finally, the statement that the Lie algebra formed by these
X
’s is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{C}
also follows from Proposition
2.16
below, where we exhibit explicit real forms isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
.
∎

In this matrix representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, the subspace
\mathfrak{h}
of diagonal elements is a Cartan subalgebra. It is easy to find out explicitly the corresponding root system and Chevalley basis, which we do not specify here. An
\mathfrak{h}
-principal
3
-dimensional subalgebra
\mathfrak{s}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
(see
[
42
,
48
]
) is spanned by

\widetilde{e}=\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{pmatrix}0&&&&&&\\
1&0&&&&&\\
&1&0&&&&\\
&&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&&&\\
&&&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&0&&\\
&&&&1&0&\\
&&&&&1&0\end{pmatrix}$},\quad a=\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{pmatrix}3&&&&&&\\
&2&&&&&\\
&&1&&&&\\
&&&0&&&\\
&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt-1&&\\
&&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt-2&\\
&&&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt-3\end{pmatrix}$},\quad e=\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{pmatrix}0&3&&&&&\\
&0&5&&&&\\
&&0&\hskip-4.26773pt\scalebox{0.8}{$6\sqrt{2}$}&&&\\
&&&0&\hskip-4.26773pt\scalebox{0.8}{$6\sqrt{2}$}&&\\
&&&&0&5&\\
&&&&&0&3\\
&&&&&&0\end{pmatrix}$}.

As fundamental facts in the construction of Hitchin components, to any principal
3
-dimensional subalgebra is associated an involution
\sigma
of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
, and every Cartan involution
\rho
of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
which commutes with
\sigma
determines an anti-linear involution
\lambda=\sigma\circ\rho
whose fixed point set is a split real form (see
[
42
,
48
]
). Furthermore, those
\rho
arising from
cyclic
Higgs bundles preserve
\mathfrak{h}
, and such a
\rho
is called an
\mathfrak{h}
-Cartan involution
. For the above
\mathfrak{s}
, the associated
\sigma
is the usual one
\sigma(x)=-\widehat{x}
, where
\widehat{x}
denotes the transpose of
x
with respect to the diagonal from lower-left to upper-right. The following proposition characterizes all
\mathfrak{h}
-Cartan involutions commuting with
\sigma
and identifies the resulting split real forms with
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
in a concrete way:

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
H
be a hermitian metric on
\mathbb{C}^{7}
and
\rho(x)=-x^{*}
be the Cartan involution of
\mathfrak{sl}_{7}\mathbb{C}
induced by
H
(which is also the Cartan involution of the
\mathfrak{so}(Q)
in Proposition
2.15
), where
x^{*}=\overline{H}{}^{-1}{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\overline{x}\overline{H}
denotes the
H
-adjoint of
x
. Then
\rho
preserves both the above
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
and its Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}
, and furthermore commutes with
\sigma
, if and only if
H
has the form

H=\mathrm{diag}\big(\tfrac{1}{h_{3}}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{h_{2}}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\,,\ 1\,,\ h_{1}\,,\ h_{2}\,,\ h_{3}\big)\ \text{ with }h_{3}=\tfrac{h_{1}h_{2}}{4}.

Moreover, given such an
H
, consider the anti-linear involution
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
defined by

\Lambda(z_{1},\cdots,z_{7})=\big(h_{3}\overline{z}_{7}\,,\ h_{2}\overline{z}_{6}\,,\ h_{1}\overline{z}_{5}\,,\ \overline{z}_{4}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\overline{z}_{3}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{h_{2}}\overline{z}_{2}\,,\ \tfrac{1}{h_{3}}\overline{z}_{1}\big).

Then
Q
and
\varpi
from Proposition
2.15
restrict to a real quadratic form and a real
3
-form on
\mathrm{Fix}(\Lambda)\cong\mathbb{R}^{7}
, respectively, and
(\mathrm{Fix}(\Lambda),Q,\varpi)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
endowed with the
3
-form
\varphi
(see §
2.3
). In particular,
\big\{X\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}\mid\text{$X$ preserves $\mathrm{Fix}(\Lambda)$}\big\}
is a real form of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
isomorphic to
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that if
\sigma
preserves
\mathfrak{h}
then
H
is diagonal. The “if and only if” statement then follows from a simple computation. For the “moreover” statement, we consider the basis
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7})
of
\mathbb{C}^{7}
given by the columns of the matrix

B=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc|cc}&&&&&\boldsymbol{i}h_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}&h_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
&h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\[5.69046pt]
&&&h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69046pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\sqrt{2}$}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\\[5.69046pt]
&h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\[5.69046pt]
&&&&&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{3}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&h_{3}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}$}\right),

or in other words,
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7}):=(\varepsilon_{1},\cdots,\varepsilon_{7})B
. Clearly, every
e_{i}
is fixed by
\Lambda
, and we have
Q(e_{i},e_{i})=1
(resp.
-1
) for
i=1,2,3
(resp.
4,5,6,7
) and
Q(e_{i},e_{j})=0
for
i\neq j
. Also, noting that the basis
(e^{1},\cdots,e^{7})
of
\mathbb{C}^{7*}
dual to
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{7})
satisfies
(\varepsilon^{1},\cdots,\varepsilon^{7})=(e^{1},\cdots,e^{7})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!B
, we obtain by computations that

\varpi:=\boldsymbol{i}\big(\varepsilon^{147}+\varepsilon^{246}-\varepsilon^{345}-\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,\varepsilon^{156}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon^{237}\big)
is equal to
e^{123}-e^{145}+e^{167}-e^{246}-e^{257}-e^{347}+e^{356}
, which is exactly the expression of
\varphi
in the standard coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
. This implies the “moreover” statement.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

The matrix representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
considered in this subsection is adapted for the Higgs bundles of the form in the introduction, and is not symmetric in the sense that
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
does not imply
{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!X\in\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
. This is the reason why the coefficients of the
3
-form
\varpi
lack symmetry.
Conjugating this
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
by a matrix of the form
\mathrm{diag}\big(c,c,c,1,\frac{1}{c},\frac{1}{c},\frac{1}{c}\big)
(which preserves
Q
), one may bring it to a symmetric representation as the one in
[
5
, p.89]
, for which the condition
h_{3}=\tfrac{h_{1}h_{2}}{4}
on
H
becomes
h_{3}=h_{1}h_{2}
. One may also use another conjugation of this type to change the
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
in
\widetilde{e}
into
1
, so that the corresponding Higgs bundles have a more familiar form.

3.
Minimal submanifolds

In this section, we collect some preliminary definitions and results about minimal submanifolds.

3.1.
Submanifolds of pseudo-Riemannian manifold

Let
(M,{\boldsymbol{h}})
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold. Given a smooth submanifold
\Sigma\subset M
, we consider the restricted tangent bundle
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
, which is a vector bundle on
\Sigma
endowed with the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and connection
\nabla
induced by
{\boldsymbol{h}}
and its Levi-Civita connection. Also let
{{\mathcal{T}}}:=\mathsf{T}\Sigma\subset\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
be the tangent bundle of
\Sigma
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}:=\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{{\mathcal{T}}}}
be the first fundamental form.
\Sigma
is called a
spacelike
(or
Riemannian
) submanifold if
{\boldsymbol{g}}
is positive definite, which in particular implies that we have an orthogonal splitting
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={{\mathcal{T}}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}
, where
{\mathcal{N}}
is the normal bundle, namely the
orthogonal complement of
{{\mathcal{T}}}
in
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
.

Since
\nabla
preseves
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, it decomposes as follows under the splitting
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{T}}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}
:

(3.1)

\nabla=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}&-A^{\dagger}\\
A&\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}\end{pmatrix}.

\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}
is just the Levi-Civita connection of
{\boldsymbol{g}}
, while
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
is called the
normal connection
. The
1
-forms
A\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}({{\mathcal{T}}},{\mathcal{N}}))
and
A^{\dagger}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{N}},{{\mathcal{T}}}))
are related by

\langle A(X)Y,\xi\rangle=\langle Y,A^{\dagger}(X)\xi\rangle
(here and below, we let
X,Y
and
\xi
be arbitrary sections of
{{\mathcal{T}}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
, respectively). We recall the following familiar names for variants of
A
and
A^{\dagger}
:

•

The
second fundamental form
of
\Sigma
is the
{\mathcal{N}}
-valued
2
-tensor
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
defined by
1
1

1

Throughout this paper, given a decomposition
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\oplus\cdots
(of a vector space or vector bundle), we put a superscript such as
V_{1}
after
v\in V
to denote the corresponding component of
v
.

{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y):=A(X)Y:=(\nabla_{X}Y)^{\mathcal{N}}
. An basic fact is that it is a
symmetric
tensor. In analogy with the theory of Frenet curves, we call every normal vector in the image of
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
an
osculation
vector.

•

The
shape operator
of
\Sigma
associated to a given
\xi
is the
(1,1)
-tensor
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
defined by
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(X):=A^{\dagger}(X)\xi=-(\nabla_{X}\xi)^{{\mathcal{T}}}
. By the relation between
A
and
A^{\dagger}
, we have
\langle{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y),\xi\rangle=\langle Y,{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(X)\rangle
.

The section
\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(e_{i},e_{i})
of
{\mathcal{N}}
(where
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is any orthonormal local frame of
{{\mathcal{T}}}
) is called the
mean curvature field
of
\Sigma
, and
\Sigma
is said to be a
minimal
submanifold if
\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\equiv 0
.

3.2.
Jacobi operator

Given a Riemannian manifold
(\Sigma,{\boldsymbol{g}})
and a real vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on
\Sigma
endowed with a metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and a metric-preserving connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
, we have a Laplacian operator
\Delta^{\mathcal{E}}:C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})\to C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})
defined by

\Delta^{\mathcal{E}}\xi:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{\mathcal{E}}\nabla_{e_{i}}^{\mathcal{E}}\xi-\nabla_{\nabla^{\mathsf{T}\Sigma}_{e_{i}}e_{i}}^{\mathcal{E}}\xi\big),

where
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
is an orthonormal local frame of
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
and
\nabla^{\mathsf{T}\Sigma}
is the Levi-Civita connection of
{\boldsymbol{g}}
. For any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})
with
\xi_{1}
compactly supported, we have (see e.g.
[
8
, §2.1]
):

(3.2)

\int_{\Sigma}\langle\Delta^{\mathcal{E}}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\xi_{1},\Delta^{\mathcal{E}}\xi_{2}\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}=-\int_{\Sigma}\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\xi_{1},\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\xi_{2}\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}},

where the first equality signifies that
\Delta^{\mathcal{E}}
is a self-adjoint operator.

When
\Sigma
is a spacelike minimal submanifold in a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
as considered in §
3.1
, the
Jacobi operator
(or
stability operator
)
L_{\Sigma}:C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}})\to C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}})
is defined by

L_{\Sigma}\xi:=\Delta^{\!{\mathcal{N}}}\xi+\widetilde{A}(\xi)+\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big(R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\big),

where
\Delta^{\!{\mathcal{N}}}
is the Laplacian given by the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}
and the normal connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
,
R_{M}
is the curvature tensor
2
2

2

As in
[
2
,
17
]
, we follow do Carmo’s sign convention of curvature tensor:
R(X,Y)Z:=\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}Z-\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}Z+\nabla_{[X,Y]}Z
.
of
M
, and
\widetilde{A}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\End({\mathcal{N}}))
is the
Simons operator
of
\Sigma
given by

\widetilde{A}(\xi):=\sum_{i,j}^{n}\langle{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(e_{i},e_{j}),\xi\rangle{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(e_{i},e_{j}).

The sections of
{\mathcal{N}}
annihilated by
L_{\Sigma}
are called
Jacobi fields
.

The self-adjointness of
\Delta^{\mathcal{N}}
impplies that
L_{\Sigma}
is a self-adjoint operator as well:
\int_{\Sigma}\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\xi_{1},L_{\Sigma}\xi_{2}\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}
if
\xi_{1}
has compact support.
Also, since it follows from the relation between
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}
and
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}
that
\langle\widetilde{A}(\xi),\xi\rangle=\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle
, by (
3.2
), we have

(3.3)

-\int_{\Sigma}\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi,\xi\rangle\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}=\int_{\Sigma}\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\Big(\langle\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi,\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi\rangle-\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle-\langle R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi\rangle\Big)\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}

for any compactly supported section
\xi
of
{\mathcal{N}}
.

3.3.
Variations of minimal submanifolds

L_{\Sigma}
is related to variations of the minimal submanifold
\Sigma
. By definition, a
variation
of
\Sigma
is a smooth one-parameter family
(F_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
of embeddings
\Sigma\to M
such that
F_{0}
is the inclusion. We use the following standard terminologies concerning such a
(F_{t})
:

•

The derivative
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}:=\tfrac{\mathsf{d}}{\mathsf{d}t}F_{t}\big|_{t=0}
, which is a section of
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
, is called the
variational vector field
.

•

(F_{t})
is said to be
normal
if
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is a section of
{\mathcal{N}}\subset\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
, and is said to be
compactly supported
if
F_{t}=F_{0}
for all
t
outside of a compact subset of
\Sigma
.

•

Let
\Sigma_{t}
denote the image of
F_{t}
. When
(F_{t})
is compactly supported,
\Sigma_{t}
only differs from
\Sigma
within a compact set
C\subset M
, so the volume
\mathit{vol}(\Sigma_{t}\cap C)
is well defined and its derivatives in
t
are independent of the choice of
C
. The second derivative
\tfrac{\mathsf{d}^{2}}{\mathsf{d}t^{2}}\mathit{vol}(\Sigma_{t}\cap K)\big|_{t=0}
is called the
second variation of volume
associated to the variation
(F_{t})
.

The following theorem summarizes the fundamental facts about variations that we will need. See e.g.
[
2
,
17
,
57
,
61
]
for proofs. Note that although most of the literature only treats the case where the ambient space
M
is Riemannian, the results and proofs actually hold in the pseudo-Riemannian setting without much modification, and one may even assume that
\Sigma
is a pseudo-Riemannian submanifold instead of a Riemannian one (see
[
2
]
).

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a spacelike minimal submanifold in a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
and
(F_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
be a variation of
\Sigma
.

(1)

If
(F_{t})
is a compactly supported normal variation, then the associated second variation of volume is equal to the integral (
3.3
) with
\xi=\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
.

(2)

If every
\Sigma_{t}
is a minimal submanifold, then the normal component
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}
of
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is a Jacobi field.

Part
(1)
is known as the
second variation formula
. A simple consequence is:

Proposition 3.2
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.1

(1)
, if furthermore the signature of
M
is
(p,q)
with
p=\dim\Sigma
(or equivalently, the normal bundle of
\Sigma
is negative definite) and
M
has negative sectional curvature, then the second variation of volume is negative whenever
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is not identically zero.

In particular, every spacelike minimal surface in the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
locally maximizes the area. This explains why they are called
maximal surfaces
in the literature.

Proof.

Put
\xi:=\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
and let
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n})
be an orthonormal local frame of
\Sigma
with respect to the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}=\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{{\mathcal{T}}}}
. Since
\nabla_{e_{i}}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi
and
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(e_{i})
are sections of
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{{\mathcal{T}}}
, respectively, we have

(3.4)

\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi,\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}_{e_{i}}\xi,\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}_{e_{i}}\xi\rangle\leq 0,\quad\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(e_{i}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(e_{i})\rangle\geq 0.

On the other hand, at any point
z\in\Sigma
with
\xi(z)\neq 0
, the sectional curvature of
M
along the tangent
2
-plane spanned by
e_{i}
and
\xi
is

K(e_{i}\wedge\xi):=\frac{\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle}{\langle e_{i},e_{i}\rangle\langle\xi,\xi\rangle-\langle e_{i},\xi\rangle^{2}}=\frac{\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi,\xi\rangle}.

We have
K(e_{i}\wedge\xi)<0
and
\langle\xi,\xi\rangle<0
at
z
by assumption. It follows that
\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle>0
, and hence

(3.5)

\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle>0\ \ \text{at $z$}.

By (
3.4
) and (
3.5
), the right-hand side of (
3.3
) is negative when
\xi
is not identically zero. In view of Theorem
3.1

(1)
, this implies the required statement.
∎

For an application later on, we also recall the following formula for the first variation of the connection
\nabla
on
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
. Here
\Sigma
does not need to be minimal.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a spacelike submanifold in a pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
and
(F_{t})_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
be a variation of
\Sigma
. Let
\nabla_{t}
denote the connection on
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma_{t}}\cong\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
, where the identification is given by parallel transportation along the paths
(F_{t}(x))_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
,
x\in\Sigma
. Then the derivative
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{\nabla}_{0}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\End(\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}))
of
\nabla_{t}
at
t=0
is given by

(3.6)

(\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{\nabla}_{0})_{X}\zeta=R_{M}(X,\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0})\zeta\quad\text{ for any }X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma),\ \zeta\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}).

In particular, if
M
has constant sectional curvature and
(F_{t})
is a tangential variation (i.e.
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is a section of
\mathsf{T}\Sigma\subset\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
), then every component of the decomposition (
3.1
) is stationary at
t=0
except for the
\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}
-component.

Proof.

Let
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})
be local coordinates of
\Sigma
. It suffices to prove (
3.6
) at
x=0
for
X=\partial_{x_{1}}
. To this end, for each fixed
x_{1}
, we parallel-translate
\zeta_{0}(x_{1}):=\zeta(x_{1},0,\cdots,0)\in\mathsf{T}_{(x_{1},0,\cdots,0)}M
along the path
(F_{t}(x_{1},0,\cdots,0))_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
and get a vector
\zeta_{t}(x_{1})\in\mathsf{T}_{F_{t}(x_{1},0,\cdots,0)}M
for every
t
. If we fix
t
and let
x_{1}
vary instead, these vectors form a vector field along
(F_{t}(x_{1},0,\cdots,0))_{x_{1}\in(-\delta,\delta)}
, whose covariant derivative in
x_{1}
at
x_{1}=0
can be denoted by
\nabla_{\partial_{1}F_{t}(0)}\zeta_{t}(x_{1})\in\mathsf{T}_{F_{t}(0)}M
. The left-hand side of (
3.6
) at
x=0
is just the covariant derivative of the last vector in
t
at
t=0
:

(\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{\nabla}_{0})_{\partial_{x_{1}}}\zeta=\nabla_{\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(0)}\nabla_{\partial_{1}F_{t}(0)}\zeta_{t}(x_{1}).

On the other hand, by definition of the curvature tensor
R_{M}
and the fact that the vector fields
\partial_{1}F_{t}(x_{1},0)
and
\partial_{t}F_{t}(x_{1},0)
commute, we have

R_{M}(\partial_{x_{1}},\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(0))=\nabla_{\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(0)}\nabla_{\partial_{1}F_{t}(0)}\zeta_{t}(x_{1})-\nabla_{\partial_{x_{1}}}\nabla_{\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(x_{1},0)}\zeta_{t}(x_{1}),

and the last term vanishes by the construction of
\zeta_{t}(x_{1})
. This proves (
3.6
). We deduce the “In particular” statement by noting that having constant curvature
\kappa
means
R_{M}(u,v)w=\kappa(\langle u,w\rangle v-\langle v,w\rangle u)
.
∎

3.4.
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves as minimal surfaces

In an almost complex manifold
(M,{\boldsymbol{J}})
, a
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve
is a surface
\Sigma
whose tangent bundle
\mathsf{T}\Sigma\subset\mathsf{T}M
is preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
. Such a
\Sigma
is a minimal surface for a pseudo-Riemannian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}
satisfying some compatibility condition with
{\boldsymbol{J}}
:

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(M,{\boldsymbol{h}})
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold endowed with an almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
which is orthogonal (i.e.
{\boldsymbol{h}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})={\boldsymbol{h}}({\boldsymbol{J}}{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\boldsymbol{J}}{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
),
\nabla
be the Levi-Civita connection, and
\Sigma\subset M
be a spacelike
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve. Then

(1)

A sufficient condition for
\Sigma
to be a minimal surface is

(3.7)

\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{J}}X}{\boldsymbol{J}}=-{\boldsymbol{J}}\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}}\ \ \text{ for all }X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}M).

(2)

The second fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
of
\Sigma
is complex bilinear (i.e.
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\boldsymbol{J}}{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})={\boldsymbol{J}}\circ{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
) if and only if

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y=0
for any
X,Y
tangent to
\Sigma
. A sufficient condition for this is

(3.8)

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})X=0\ \ \text{ for all }X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}M).

As a consequence, under condition (
3.8
), for any point
z\in\Sigma
where
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is nonzero, the space of osculation vectors
\big\{{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)\mid X,Y\in\mathsf{T}_{z}\Sigma\big\}
is a complex line in
{\mathcal{N}}_{z}
. We call it the
osculation line
of
\Sigma
at
z
.
This proposition will mainly be applied to the almost complex
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, which fulfills (
3.8
) (see §
2.6
).

Remark 3.5
.

Condition (
3.8
) is equivalent to the condition that

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y
is anti-symmetric in
X
and
Y
, which is
stronger than (
3.7
) because it implies

(\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{J}}X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y=-(\nabla_{Y}{\boldsymbol{J}}){\boldsymbol{J}}X={\boldsymbol{J}}\nabla_{Y}{\boldsymbol{J}})X=-{\boldsymbol{J}}(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y

(note that
{\boldsymbol{J}}\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}}=-(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}}){\boldsymbol{J}}
because
{\boldsymbol{J}}^{2}=-\mathit{id}
). Correspondingly, it is easy to check that the complex linearity of
