{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
also implies the minimality condition
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})=0
.

Remark 3.6
.

When
{\boldsymbol{h}}
is Riemannian,
(M,{\boldsymbol{h}},{\boldsymbol{J}})
satisfying (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) are known as
quasi-Kähler
and
nearly Kähler
manifolds, respectively. Along with the notion of
almost-Kähler
manifolds, they were introduced by Gray
[
34
,
35
]
, whose also proved a higher-dimensional generalization of Part
(1)
(
[
34
, Theorem 5.7]
).

Proof of Proposition
3.4
.

(1)

We abuse the notation to let
\nabla
and
{\boldsymbol{J}}
also standard for the connection and complex structure induced on the vector bundle
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
(see §
3.1
). By assumption,
{\boldsymbol{J}}
preserves the subbundles
{{\mathcal{T}}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!
and
{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!
denote the restriction of
{\boldsymbol{J}}
to these subbundles. In view of the decompositions

\nabla=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}&-A^{\dagger}\\
A&\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}\end{pmatrix},\quad{\boldsymbol{J}}=\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!&\\
&{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!\end{pmatrix},

we have the following expression for
\nabla{\boldsymbol{J}}
as an
\End(\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma})
-valued
1
-form on
\Sigma
:

(3.9)

\nabla{\boldsymbol{J}}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!&{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!A^{\dagger}-A^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!\\
A{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!A&\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}
is the Levi-Civita connection of the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}
, while
{\boldsymbol{g}}
is a metric conformal to the complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!
, we
have
\nabla^{{\mathcal{T}}}{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!=0
. Therefore, the first column of (
3.9
) gives

(3.10)

(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y=A(X){\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!Y-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!A(X)Y={\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!Y)-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)

for any tangent vector fields
X
and
Y
of
\Sigma
.

Assuming (
3.7
), we have
(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})X+(\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{J}}X}{\boldsymbol{J}}){\boldsymbol{J}}X=0
for any tangent vector field
X
of
M
. Taking
X
to be tangent to
\Sigma
and applying (
3.10
), we get

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})X+(\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X}{\boldsymbol{J}}){\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X

\displaystyle=\big({\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X)-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,X)\big)+\big(-{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,X)-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X)\big)

\displaystyle=-{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!\big({\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,X)+{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X)\big),

This implies that
\Sigma
is minimal, because
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,X)+{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X)
is exactly the mean curvature field
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
when
X
has unit length under
{\boldsymbol{g}}
.

(2)

The “if and only if” statement follows immediately from (
3.10
). Assuming (
3.8
), we have
(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y+(\nabla_{Y}{\boldsymbol{J}})X=0
.
Taking
X
and
Y
to be tangent to
\Sigma
and applying (
3.10
) again, we get

(3.11)

0=(\nabla_{X}{\boldsymbol{J}})Y+(\nabla_{Y}{\boldsymbol{J}})X={\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!Y)+{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,Y)-2{\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y).

Since we know that
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})=0
by Part
(1)
, we have
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!Y)={\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!X,Y)
(this can be checked for
(X,Y)=(e_{1},e_{1})
,
(e_{2},e_{2})
,
(e_{1},e_{2})
and
(e_{2},e_{1})
respectively, for an orthonormal local frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
{{\mathcal{T}}}
with
e_{2}={\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!e_{1}
). Thus, (
3.11
) implies
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\boldsymbol{J}}^{{\mathcal{T}}}\!\!Y)={\boldsymbol{J}}^{\mathcal{N}}\!\!{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)
, as required.
∎

3.5.
Minimal immersions and maps to symmetric space

Although
\Sigma
is a submanifold of
(M,{\boldsymbol{h}})
in all the discussions above, it is just a matter of notation to adapt everything to the setting of an immersion
f:\Sigma\to M
. Namely, instead of
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
, we consider the vector bundle
f^{*}\mathsf{T}M
on
\Sigma
, which comes with the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and connection
\nabla
given by the ones on
\mathsf{T}M
. In addition, we view the differential
\mathsf{d}f
as a
1
-form on
\Sigma
with values in this vector bundle, which tells how
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
is embedded as a subbundle.

These objects can be used to study the more general setting where
\Sigma
is endowed with a background metric or conformal structure unrelated to the first fundamental form
f^{*}{\boldsymbol{h}}
and/or
f
is merely a smooth map rather than an immersion. In particular, when
\Sigma
is a Riemann surface, the notion of
harmonic map
is defined via the covariant derivative of the
1
-form
\mathsf{d}f
, and it is well known that
f
is harmonic and weakly conformal at the same time if and only if it is a branched conformal minimal immersion (see
[
39
]
).

In this paper, we will only encounter true immersions and do not need to worry about branching points. We will consider certain
f
satisfying a strong condition which implies conformal minimality:

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
f
be an immersion of a Riemann surface
\Sigma
into a Riemannian manifold
X
. If the vector bundle
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
admits an orthogonal, parallel, complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
under which
\mathsf{d}f
is a
(1,0)
-form, then
f
is a conformal minimal immersion.

Proof.

The conformality follows immediately from the assumption that
\mathsf{d}f
is a
(1,0)
-form and
{\boldsymbol{J}}
is orthogonal. To show the minimality, we transfer back to the setting where
\Sigma
is an embedded surface in
X
, and only need to show that if the vector bundle
\mathsf{T}X|_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
admits an orthogonal, parallel, complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
which preserves the subbundle
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
, then
\Sigma
is minimal. Here, being parallel means
\nabla{\boldsymbol{J}}=0
, but by the computation of
\nabla{\boldsymbol{J}}
in the proof of Proposition
3.4
, the condition that the
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
-component of
\nabla{\boldsymbol{J}}
(given by (
3.10
)) vanishes already implies that the second fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is complex bilinear, which in turns implies the minimality.
∎

Although the proof has some overlap with Proposition
3.4
, this lemma will be applied to a context different from that proposition, with
X
being the Riemannian symmetric space
X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)}
, which does not admit a natural almost complex structure itself.

Finally, we recall the following well known description of
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
(as a metric vector bundle with connection) and
\mathsf{d}f
(as a
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
-valued
1
-form) for a general symmetric spaces
X_{G}
:

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
G
be a noncompact semisimple Lie group with maximal compact subgroup
K
and Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
. Let
X_{G}=G/K
be the associated Riemannian symmetric space and
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(G,\mathfrak{g})
be the left Maurer-Cartan form on
G
. Given a smooth map
f
from a manifold
\Sigma
to
X_{G}
, if
f
has a lift
\widetilde{f}:\Sigma\to G
, then
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
identifies with the trivial
\mathfrak{p}
-bundle over
\Sigma
endowed with metric given by the Killing form and the connection given by the
\End(\mathfrak{p})
-valued
1
-form
\ad(\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega)^{\mathfrak{k}}
(where
\ad
is the map
\mathfrak{k}\to\End(\mathfrak{p})
corresponding to the
\mathfrak{k}
-action on
\mathfrak{p}
), while the
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
-valued
1
-form
\mathsf{d}f
identifies with the
\mathfrak{p}
-valued
1
-form
(\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega)^{\mathfrak{p}}
.

Proof.

If we view the projection
\pi:G\to X
as a principal
K
-bundle endowed with the principal connection whose horizontal distribution is given by left-translating
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}=\mathsf{T}_{e}G
to every point of
G
, then
\mathsf{T}X_{G}
identifies with the
\mathfrak{p}
-bundle
G\times_{\Ad_{K}}\mathfrak{p}
associated to this principal
K
-bundle, while the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathsf{T}X_{G}
is the associated connection. As a consequence,
\pi^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
identifies with the trivial
\mathfrak{p}
-bundle over
G
with the connection given by the
1
-form
\ad\omega^{\mathfrak{k}}\in\Omega^{1}(G,\End(\mathfrak{p}))
, while the
1
-form
\mathsf{d}\pi\in\Omega^{1}(G,\mathfrak{p})
is just
\omega^{\mathfrak{p}}
. The required statement follows.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

In Higgs bundle theory, one obtains a map
f:\Sigma\to X
from the data of a principal
G
-bundle
{\mathcal{P}}
over
\Sigma
, a flat
G
-connection
\nabla
on
{\mathcal{P}}
and a
K
-reduction of
{\mathcal{P}}
. We may write

\nabla=\nabla^{K}+\Psi
,
where
\nabla^{K}
is a
K
-connection and
\Psi
is a
1
-form with values in the
\mathfrak{p}
-bundle
{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
associated to the principal
K
-bundle resulting from the reduction. Lemma
3.8
essentially means that
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X_{G}
identifies with
{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
endowed with the connection
\nabla^{K}
and the metric given by the Killing form, while
\mathsf{d}f
is just
\Psi
.

4.
A-surfaces: definition and properties

In this section, we give a detailed discussion of A-surfaces and establish their fundamental properties in Theorems
4.6
,
4.10
and
4.13
, which are roughly stated as Theorem
A
in the introduction.

4.1.
The definition

In analogy to Frenet curves, we introduce:

Definition 4.1
.

Let
M
be a pseudo-Riemannian manifold of even dimension
2n\geq 4
and
\Sigma\subset M
be a smooth, embedded, spacelike surface. Consider the vector bundle
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma
endowed with the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and connection
\nabla
given by the metric and Levi-Civita connection of
M
.
Then a
Frenet splitting
of
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
is an orthogonal splitting

\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
into rank
2
subbundles
such that

•

{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma\subset\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
is the tangent bundle of
\Sigma
(hence
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is the normal bundle);

•

every
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is (positive or negative) definite;

•

(\nabla_{X}\xi)^{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}=0
for all
X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma)
and
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{L}}_{j})
with
|i-j|\geq 2
.

Since
\nabla
preserves
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, the last bullet point implies that
\nabla
decomposes under the splitting as

(4.1)

\nabla=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{1}&-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{\dagger}&&&\\[2.84544pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\nabla_{2}&-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\dagger}&&\\
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\nabla_{3}&\ddots&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-2.84544pt-{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\dagger}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}&\hskip-2.84544pt\nabla_{n}\end{pmatrix}$},

where
\nabla_{i}
is a connection on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
preserving the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}
, while
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{\dagger}
are
1
-forms with values in
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{j})
and
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{j},{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1})
, respectively,
related by
\langle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}(X)\xi,\eta\rangle=\langle\xi,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{\dagger}(X)\eta\rangle
for any tangent vector field
X
and sections
\xi
and
\eta
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
respectively.

Remark 4.2
.

The
1
-form
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is just the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
, as we have

{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)Y
.
In particular, every osculation vector (see §
3.1
) is in
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
.
By the relation
\langle{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y),\xi\rangle=\langle X,{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(Y)\rangle
, it follows that the shape operator
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\End(\mathsf{T}\Sigma))
assigned to a normal vector field
\xi
is identically zero whenever
\xi
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
.

A linear map
A:\mathbb{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{E}^{2}
of the Euclidean plane is said to be
conformal
if
A
sends any circle centered at the origin to a circle (possibly reduced to
\{0\}
), or equivalently, if
A
is either a scaled rotation, a scaled reflection, or the zero map.
A homomorphism between rank
2
metric vector bundles is said to be conformal if it is a conformal linear map on each fiber. Our main object of study is:

Definition 4.3
.

Given
M
and
\Sigma
as above, with
\Sigma
connected and orientable, we call
\Sigma

superminimal
if
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
has a Frenet splitting such that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}(X):{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
is conformal for all
X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma)
and
2\leq j\leq n
. More generally, we call
\Sigma

quasi-superminimal
if
\Sigma
is minimal and
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
has a Frenet splitting with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}(X)
conformal for
2\leq j\leq n-1
(the condition is removed for
j=n
here).
If
\Sigma
is quasi-superminimal and the
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
’s have alternating space-/time-likeness, namely
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}
is positive/negative definite for all odd/even
i
, then we call
\Sigma
an
A-surface
.

As mentioned in the introduction, by counting the number of the positive/negative definite
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
’s, we see that if
M
admits an A-surface, then its signature
(p,q)
is determined from
n=\tfrac{p+q}{2}
by (
1.3
), so we will always let
(p,q)
be given by that equation whenever an A-surface is being considered.

Remark 4.4
.

It is easy to show that if a symmetric bilinear map
B({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}):\mathbb{E}^{2}\times\mathbb{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{E}^{2}
has the property that
B(v,{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}):\mathbb{E}^{2}\to\mathbb{E}^{2}
is conformal for all fixed
v
, then
\Tr B({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}):=B(e_{1},e_{1})+B(e_{2},e_{2})
vanishes. Therefore, if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)={\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is conformal for all
X\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma)
, then
\Sigma
is minimal. In particular, superminimal surfaces are minimal, and when
n\geq 3
we may omit the condition “
\Sigma
is minimal” in the definition of quasi-superminimal surfaces as well. On the other hand, when
n=2
, the conformality condition in the definition of quasi-superminimal surfaces is vacuous, so an A-surface in this case is just a spacelike minimal surface with rank
2
negative definite normal bundle.

Remark 4.5
.

The notion of superminimal surfaces arose from the study of minimal
2
-spheres. Chern
[
16
]
essentially showed that any minimal
2
-sphere
S
in a Riemannian manifold
M
of constant curvature is superminimal in the sense of Definition
4.3
(after replacing
M
by the smallest totally geodesic submanifold containing
S
), while such spheres are also studied via twistor lift in
[
15
,
7
,
54
]
for
M=\mathbb{S}^{2n}
. Bryant
[
12
]
coined the term “superminimal” to describe minimal surfaces of general topological type in
\mathbb{S}^{4}
with similar properties, again via twistor lift. When
M
is a Riemannian
4
-manifold, the equivalent definition via the conformality of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{\dagger}(X)
(which is the same as the conformality of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)
) appears in
[
30
,
28
,
29
]
and is attributed to K. Kommerell
[
45
]
.

4.2.
Holomorphic interpretation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}

Given an A-surface
\Sigma\subset M
with Frenet splitting
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
, it is convenient to consider the positive definite metric

\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{i}:=(-1)^{i+1}\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}

on each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
. The adjoint
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{*}(X)
of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}(X):{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
with respect to these metrics is opposite to the adjoint
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{\dagger}(X)
in (
4.1
) with respect to the original
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, so (
4.1
) can be rewritten as

(4.2)

\nabla=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{1}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{*}&&&\\[2.84544pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\nabla_{2}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{*}&&\\
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\nabla_{3}&\ddots&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{*}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}&\hskip-2.84544pt\nabla_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}.

Now assume that
M
is the pseudo-hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
. Since this is a real analytic manifold, any minimal surface therein is analytic as well (see e.g.
[
27
, p. 117]
for a stronger result in this regard).
Our first main result is a holomorphic interpretation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
.
Let us recall some backgrounds in order to give the statement.

On a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the following two types of objects can be considered as the same thing:

•

rank
r
hermitian holomorphic vector bundle
({\mathcal{E}},{\boldsymbol{h}})
;

•

rank
2r
real vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
endowed with a (positive definite) metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, an orthogonal complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
and a connection
\nabla
preserving
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and
{\boldsymbol{J}}
.

Namely, given the former, we obtain the latter by letting
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and
\nabla
be the real part and Chern connection, respectively, of the hermitian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}
; conversely, given the latter, the
(0,1)
-part of
\nabla
is a Dolbeault operator (or “partial/pseudo connection”) that provides
{\mathcal{E}}
with a holomorphic atlas (see e.g.
[
36
]
).

When
r=1
, since the Euclidean plane admits exactly two orthogonal complex structures, corresponding to the two orientations, we conclude that a hermitian holomorphic line bundle
({\mathcal{L}},{\boldsymbol{h}})
on
\Sigma
is the same as an oriented real rank
2
vector bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
endowed with a metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and a metric-preserving connection
\nabla
. It can be shown that the orientation-reversed vector bundle
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}
(endowed with the same
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and
\nabla
) corresponds to the inverse hermitian holomorphic line bundle
({\mathcal{L}}^{-1},{\boldsymbol{h}}^{-1})
. As an example, the anti-canonical line bundle
{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
endowed with a hermitian metric identifies with the tangent bundle
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
endowed with the natural orientation (underlying the Riemann surface structure), a conformal Riemannian metric, and the Levi-Civita connection of that metric.

Returning to the context of A-surfaces, we have:

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be an A-surface in
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
with
n=\tfrac{p+q}{2}\geq 2
. Suppose
\Sigma
is not contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
. Then

(1)

The Frenet splitting
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is unique, and none of the
1
-forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
in (
4.2
) is identically zero (but
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}
can be zero, namely when
\Sigma
is in a codimension
2
subspace).

(2)

Once an orientation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
is chosen, we can endow each of
{\mathcal{L}}_{2},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
with an orientation as well, such that if
\Sigma
is endowed with the Riemann surface structure given by the first fundamental form
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{1}
and the orientation, and every
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is viewed as a hermitian holomorphic line bundle on
\Sigma
by means of the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{i}
, the connection
\nabla_{i}
and the orientation (in particular,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
gets identified with the anti-canonical bundle
{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
), then

(i)

each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
with
j\leq n-1
is a
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{j})
-valued holomorphic
(1,0)
-form;

(ii)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
are holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms with values in the line bundles
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{n})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}_{n})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1})
, respectively.

Remark 4.7
.

One of the nontrivial claims in Part
(2)
is the
existence
of an orientation on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
for
i\geq 2
. On the other hand, the
uniqueness
is relatively simple: On each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
with
2\leq i\leq n-1
, the orientation with the required properties
(2)(i)

(2)(ii)
is unique, because reversing it would violate
(2)(i)
. These orientations all get reversed if the initial orientation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is reversed. On the other hand, the orientation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is inessential for the properties
(2)(i)

(2)(ii)
, as reversing it just switches
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
and
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}_{n}
and switches
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
.

Remark 4.8
.

\Sigma
not being contained in a pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
is necessary for the uniqueness of the Frenet splitting and the nonzeroness of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
. For instance, if
\Sigma
is a totally geodesic hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, then
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}|_{\Sigma}
has uncountably many Frenet splittings with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
identically zero: each totally geodesic copy of
\mathbb{H}^{2,2}
in
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
containing
\Sigma
induces one (in this case,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\pm}
are zero as well). The nonzeroness of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
is crucial in the proof, as we will use it to “propagate” the property of being a holomorphic
(1,0)
-form from
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
to
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}
, then to
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}
, and so forth.

Before proceeding with the proof, we need to carry the above discussion of vector bundles one step further. Given a hermitian holomorphic line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
and a real vector bundle
{\mathcal{V}}
endowed with a metric and a metric-preserving connection,
the bundle of homomorphisms
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})
is a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle in a natural way: its complex structure is given by pre-composing each homomorphism
{\mathcal{L}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
with the complex structure of
{\mathcal{L}}
, and its metric and connection are induced by those of
{\mathcal{L}}
and
{\mathcal{V}}
. If furthermore
{\mathcal{V}}
is endowed with a parallel orthogonal complex structure (hence is itself a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle), then we have a holomorphic decomposition

(4.3)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})\oplus\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}},\overline{{\mathcal{V}}})

given by splitting each homomorphism into complex linear and anti-linear parts. In rank
2
, we have:

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a hermitian holomorphic line bundle and
{\mathcal{V}}
be a real rank
2
vector bundle endowed with a metric and a metric-preserving connection. Let
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
be a
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})
-valued holomorphic
(1,0)
-form which is not identically zero, such that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(X)
is conformal for all
X\in\mathsf{T}\Sigma
.
Then
{\mathcal{V}}
is orientable, and has a unique orientation such that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
takes values in the subbundle
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})
of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})
defined by the orthogonal complex structure of
{\mathcal{V}}
underlying the orientation.

Proof.

It is a basic fact that given a holomorphic vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
on a Riemann surface and an
{\mathcal{E}}
-valued holomorphic
(1,0)
-form
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
which is not identically zero, there is a unique holomorphic line subbundle
{\mathcal{A}}\subset{\mathcal{E}}
containing the image of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
. In fact, if we write locally
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\alpha(z){\mathsf{d}z}
for a holomorphic local section
\alpha(z)
of
{\mathcal{E}}
, then
{\mathcal{A}}
is just generated by
\alpha(z)
away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, and this extends over any zero
z=z_{0}
because we may write
(z-z_{0})^{d}\widehat{\alpha}(z)
around
z_{0}
for a nonzero holomorphic section
\widehat{\alpha}(z)
, which equally generates
{\mathcal{A}}
.

In the current setting with
{\mathcal{E}}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{V}})
, since
{\mathcal{V}}
admits locally defined orientations and hence complex structures, the splitting (
4.3
) exists locally, and
{\mathcal{V}}
is globally orientable if and only if the two components of the splitting does not intertwine (hence give a global splitting). But the conformality assumption on
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}(X)
means that the image of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is contain in one of the two components. Therefore, by the above fact, the splitting, and hence the orientation, is global. Also, after possibly reversing the orientation, the image of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is contained in the first component rather than the second.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

We first formulate the Gauss-Codazzi equations of
\Sigma
in a moving frame. Since
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
is formed by all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
with
\langle x,x\rangle=-1
, the tangent space
\mathsf{T}_{x}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
identifies with the orthogonal complement
x^{\perp}
of
x
in
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
. Thus, around any point of
\Sigma
, we may choose locally defined maps
\iota,u_{1},v_{1},u_{2},v_{2},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n}
from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
such that
\iota
is the inclusion, while
u_{i}
and
v_{i}
are perpendicular to
\iota
and form an orthonormal frame of subspace
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
of the tangent space of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
at
f
(with respect to the positive definite metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{i}
, which is
(-1)^{i+1}
times the metric inherited from
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
). These maps are the moving frame that we are going to use. In particular,
(u_{1},v_{1})
is an orthonormal local frame of the tangent bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
.

Consider each of
\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n}
as a column vector and
(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})
as a (locally defined) matrix-valued function on
\Sigma
. In view of (
4.2
), its differential is

(4.4)

\mathsf{d}(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})=(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})\Omega\ \text{ with }\ \Omega=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta&&&&\\[2.84544pt]
\theta&\Omega_{1}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&&&\\[2.84544pt]
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\Omega_{2}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&&\\
&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\Omega_{3}&\ddots&\\
&&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-2.84544pt{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}&\hskip-2.84544pt\Omega_{n}\end{pmatrix}$},

where
\theta={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
is the local frame of
\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma
dual to
(u_{1},v_{1})
,
\Omega_{i}
is the matrix of the connection
\nabla_{i}
under the frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
, and we abuse the notation to denote the matrix of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{j-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{j}))
still by
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
(so
\Omega_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
are
2\times 2
matrices of locally defined
1
-forms).

We observe that since
\Sigma
is assumed not to be contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
or higher, none of the
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}
’s with
k\leq n-1
is identically zero. In fact, if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}\equiv 0
, then
\Omega
is the direct sum matrix of a upper-left block
\Omega^{\prime}
of size
2k-1
and a lower-right block
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
of size
2(n-k+1)
, and by (
4.4
) we have

\mathsf{d}(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{k-1},v_{k-1})=(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{k-1},v_{k-1})\Omega^{\prime}
. This implies that
\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{k-1},v_{k-1}
span a fixed nondegenerate subspace
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
. In particular,
\Sigma
is contained in the pseudo-hyperbolic subspace
L\cap\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
of codimension
2(n-k+1)
, which violates the assumption unless
k=n
.

Under this moving frame, the Gauss-Codazzi equations of
\Sigma
are just

(4.5)

\mathsf{d}\Omega+\Omega\wedge\Omega=0.

To get more explicit equations on
\Omega_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
, note that since
(u_{i},v_{i})
is an orthonormal frame of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and
\nabla_{i}
preserves the metric,
\Omega_{i}
has the form

\Omega_{i}=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}&\omega_{i}\\
-\omega_{i}&\end{pmatrix}$}

for a
1
-form
\omega_{i}
, hence
\Omega_{i}\wedge\Omega_{i}=0
. We also clearly have
{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta\wedge\theta=0
. So we find by computation that

\Omega\wedge\Omega=\scalebox{0.95}{
$\begin{pmatrix}&\hskip 8.5359pt{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta\wedge\Omega_{1}&\hskip 8.5359pt{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&&&&\\[11.38092pt]
\Omega_{1}\wedge\theta&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{42.67912pt}{$\theta\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}$}}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{45.52458pt}{$\Omega_{1}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge\Omega_{2}$}}&\hskip 8.5359pt{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&&&\\[17.07182pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge\theta&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{42.67912pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge\Omega_{1}$\\
$+\Omega_{2}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}$}}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{42.67912pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$}}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{45.52458pt}{$\Omega_{2}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge\Omega_{3}$}}&\hskip 8.5359pt{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&&\\[17.07182pt]
&\hskip 8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{42.67912pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge\Omega_{2}$\\
$+\Omega_{3}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$}}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{42.67912pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}$}}&\ddots&\ddots&\\[14.22636pt]
&&\hskip 8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\scalebox{0.9}{${\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}\\[14.22636pt]
&&&\ddots&\ddots&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{62.59596pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{56.9055pt}{$\Omega_{n-1}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge\Omega_{n}$}}\\[17.07182pt]
&&&&\scalebox{0.9}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$}&\hskip 11.38092pt\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{59.75095pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge\Omega_{n-1}$\\
$+\Omega_{n}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}}&\scalebox{0.9}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}\end{pmatrix}$}.

Thus, the Gauss-Codazzi equations (
4.5
) can be rewritten as the following three sets of equations, which are respectively the diagonal part and the parts two and three steps away from the diagonal:

\displaystyle\left\{\hskip-4.26773pt\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{d}\Omega_{1}+\theta\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}=0,\\
\mathsf{d}\Omega_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k+1}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k+1}=0\quad(2\leq k\leq n-1),\\
\mathsf{d}\Omega_{n}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}=0.\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\hskip-4.26773pt\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{d}\theta+\Omega_{1}\wedge\theta=0,\\
\mathsf{d}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k-1}\wedge\Omega_{k}+\Omega_{k}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}=0\quad(2\leq k\leq n).\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\hskip-4.26773pt\begin{array}[]{l}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\wedge\theta=0,\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k-1}=0\quad(3\leq k\leq n).\end{array}\right.

We may now prove Part
(2)
of the theorem by showing, for
i=2,\cdots,n
successively, that
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
can be oriented and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}
has the required holomorphic description.

Let us first treat
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
. Since
(u_{1},v_{1})
is an orthonormal local frame of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
and the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
is
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)Y
(see Remark
4.2
), the conditions that
\Sigma
is a minimal surface and that
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}
is a symmetric tensor amount to the following equations, respectively:

(4.13)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(u_{1})u_{1}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(v_{1})v_{1}=0,\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(u_{1})v_{1}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(v_{1})u_{1}

(the latter is effectively the first equation in (
4.2
)). We may further assume that the frame
(u_{1},v_{1})
is compatible with the prescribed orientation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
, so that the complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}_{1}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is given by
{\boldsymbol{J}}_{1}u_{1}=v_{1}
. Then we deduce from (
4.13
) that

(4.14)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}({\boldsymbol{J}}_{1}X)Y={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X){\boldsymbol{J}}_{1}Y\ \text{ for any }X,Y\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}\Sigma).

In fact, using (
4.13
), we readily check that (
4.14
) holds when
(X,Y)
is
(u_{1},u_{1})
,
(u_{1},v_{1})
,
(v_{1},u_{1})
and
(v_{1},v_{1})
respectively, but this implies that (
4.14
) holds in general.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is a hermitian holomorphic line bundle, we can view
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
as a rank
2
hermitian holomorphic vector bundle as explained before the proof, and understand (
4.14
) as saying that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is a
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
-valued
(1,0)
-form.
On the other hand, the
k=2
case of the second equation in (
4.2
) just means that the covariant exterior derivative
\mathsf{d}^{\nabla^{\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2}))
vanishes. But it is a basic fact that a smooth
(1,0)
-form
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
with values in a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}
is holomorphic if and only if
\mathsf{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=0
(where
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is the Chern connection): in fact, being holomorphic means
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is annihilated by the Dolbeault operator
{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{E}}:\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})\to\Omega^{1,1}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})=\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})
, but
{\bar{\partial}}_{\mathcal{E}}
coincides with
\mathsf{d}^{\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}}
on
\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{E}})
because
\nabla^{\mathcal{E}}
is a connection of
(1,0)
-type and
\Sigma
does not admit
(2,0)
-forms. Therefore, we conclude that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is a holomorphic
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
-valued
(1,0)
-form. We proceed to show that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
matches the description
(2)(ii)
and
(2)(i)
when
n=2
and
n\geq 3
respectively.

Case
n=2
.
In this case we have
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
. By the additivity of the first Stiefel-Whitney class, we know that the direct sum of an orientable vector bundle with a non-orientable one is again non-orientable. So
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
is orientable because
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
are. Pick any orientation and view
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
as a hermitian holomorphic line bundle correspondingly. Since
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
is the direct sum of the holomorphic line subbundles
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}_{2})
(see the paragraph preceding Lemma
4.9
),
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is the sum of holomorphic
(1,0)
-forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{+}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{-}
with values in them respectively, as required.

Case
n\geq 3
.

In this case, the conformality condition in Definition
4.3
takes effect. In particular,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)
is conformal for any
X
. So we may use Lemma
4.9
to conclude that
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
carries a unique orientation under which
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is a nonzero
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
-valued holomorphic
(1,0)
-form, as required.

Thus, we have shown that
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
can be oriented and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
has the required description.

Next, we assume
n\geq 3
and show that
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
can be oriented and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}
has the required description. As
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
has already been oriented and hence is a hermitian holomorphic line bundle,
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{2},{\mathcal{L}}_{3})
is a rank
2
hermitian holomorphic vector bundle and we may write
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}
under the decomposition into
(1,0)
- and
(0,1)
-parts

\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{2},{\mathcal{L}}_{3}))=\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{2},{\mathcal{L}}_{3}))\oplus\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{2},{\mathcal{L}}_{3})).

Since
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime}
are
(1,0)
-forms with values in
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{2},{\mathcal{L}}_{3})
, respectively, their wedge-composition
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is a
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{3})
-valued
(2,0)
-form, which must vanish. Therefore, the
k=3
case of the second equation in (
4.2
) gives

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}=0
.
This forces
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}
to vanish on the set
\{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\neq 0\}\subset\Sigma
. But since
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
is holomorphic and is not identically zero as noticed early, this set is dense. So we conclude by continuity that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime\prime}
vanishes everywhere, or in other words,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{\prime}
is a
(1,0)
-form. Now the rest of the argument is the same as the above one for
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}
: we first use the second equation in (
4.2
) to infer that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}
is holomorphic, then show in the cases
n=3
and
n\geq 4
separately that
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
can be oriented and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}
has the required description.

This scheme of argument carries on and eventually shows that
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
can be oriented and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}
has the required holomorphic description for all
i=2,\cdots,n
. The proof of Part
(2)
is thus finished.

For Part
(1)
, we have already seen that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
are nonzero. To show the uniqueness of the Frenet splitting, we note that as a subbundle of
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}|_{\Sigma}
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
is already completely determined, whereas each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is determined from
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}
because

•

if
i=n
, then
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is just the orthogonal complement of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}
in
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}|_{\Sigma}
;

•

if
i\leq n-1
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is also determined because the image of the
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{i})
-valued holomorphic
(1,0)
-form
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}
is full in
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
except at the isolated zeros.

This implies the required uniqueness and completes the proof.
∎

In what follows, given an
oriented
A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
not contained in a codimension
4
pseudo-hyperbolic subspace, we will always view
\Sigma
as a Riemann surface as in Theorem
4.6

(2)
and refer to the collection

({\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm})

of hermitian holomorphic line bundles and holomorphic forms as the
structural data
of
\Sigma
. Moreover, we let
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
denote the hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
. Note that
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is the anti-canonical line bundle
{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}:=\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}=\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{1}
is the first fundamental form.

4.3.
Affine Toda system from Gauss-Codazzi equations

In the above proof, we wrote the Gauss-Codazzi equations of an A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
as the three sets of equations (
4.2
), (
4.2
) and (
4.2
), and basically deduced Theorem
4.6
from the latter two. We show next that the first set of equations (
4.2
) is an
affine Toda system
, namely the same type of equation as the Hitchin equation of cyclic Higgs bundles (see
[
5
,
6
,
20
]
). This is an expected result, as A-surfaces will be linked directly with cyclic Higgs bundles in §
6
below.

Theorem 4.10
.

For any oriented A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
(with
n=\frac{p+q}{2}\geq 2
) not contained in a pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
, the structural data satisfies

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$:}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}h_{1}-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}^{+}|^{2}-\frac{1}{h_{1}h_{2}}|\alpha_{2}^{-}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}=\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}^{+}|^{2}-\frac{1}{h_{1}h_{2}}|\alpha_{2}^{-}|^{2};\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if $n\geq 3$:}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}h_{1}-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\tfrac{h_{k}}{h_{k-1}}|\alpha_{k}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{k+1}}{h_{k}}|\alpha_{k+1}|^{2}\quad\text{for $2\leq k\leq n-2$},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n-1}=\tfrac{h_{n-1}}{h_{n-2}}|\alpha_{n-1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}=\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2};\end{cases}

where notations are as follows: we view
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
(
2\leq j\leq n-1
) and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
as holomorphic sections of
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm 1}
, respectively, then pick a conformal coordinate
z
of
\Sigma
and a holomorphic local frame
\ell_{i}(z)
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
to write locally

h_{i}={\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}(\ell_{i},\ell_{i})
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}=\alpha_{j}\ell_{j-1}^{-1}\ell_{j}{\mathsf{d}z}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}=\alpha_{n}^{\pm}\ell_{n-1}^{-1}\ell_{n}^{\pm 1}{\mathsf{d}z}
.

Remark 4.11
.

Despite having been exhibited in a local form, these equations make sense globally. In fact, after being divided by
h_{1}
, every term is a globally defined function on
\Sigma
: the left-hand side
\tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{i}
is a constant times the ratio between the curvature form
{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log h_{i}
of the Chern connection of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and the volume form
h_{1}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}
of the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}=\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
, whereas each term on the right-hand side is the (pointwise) squared norm of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
or
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
under the hermitian metric on the respective line bundle induced by the
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
’s, namely

\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\|^{2}=\tfrac{h_{j}}{h_{1}h_{j-1}}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\quad(2\leq j\leq n-1),\quad\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}=\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{1}h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2},\quad\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{h_{1}h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}.

Proof.

In the proof of Theorem
4.6
, we obtained equations (
4.2
), (
4.2
) and (
4.2
) after choosing an orthonormal local frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
for each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
. The matrices
\Omega_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
therein depend on the choice. Under the special choice

(u_{i},v_{i})=h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ell_{i},\boldsymbol{i}\ell_{i})
, we have

(4.15)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{j}}}{\sqrt{h_{j-1}}}\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}

for
2\leq j\leq n-1
and

(4.16)

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{n}}}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}}}\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$},\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}h_{n}}}\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}.

We shall show that (
4.2
) reduces to the required equations. After expanding the
1
-forms in (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) in the real coordinates
(x,y)
with
z=x+\boldsymbol{i}y
(e.g.
\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})=\re(\alpha_{j}){\mathsf{d}x}-\im(\alpha_{j}){\mathsf{d}y}
, etc.), we obtain by computations that

(4.17)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}

\displaystyle=-{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}=-\tfrac{2\,h_{j}}{h_{j-1}}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}\quad(2\leq j\leq n-1),

(4.18)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}

\displaystyle=-{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}=-\tfrac{2\,h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y},

(4.19)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y},

(4.20)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}

\displaystyle={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}=0.

Since
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
is a symmetric and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
, we deduce from (
4.18
)
\sim
(
4.20
) that

(4.21)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}

\displaystyle=({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\wedge({\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})=2\left(-\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}\right)\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y},

(4.22)

{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\wedge{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}

\displaystyle=({\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\wedge({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})=2\left(\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}\right)\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}.

Next, since
\Omega_{i}\wedge\Omega_{i}=0
(see the proof of Theorem
4.6
),
\mathsf{d}\Omega_{i}
is the matrix of the curvature
2
-form
F_{\nabla_{i}}\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma,\End_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{L}}_{i}))
of
\nabla_{i}
under the frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
. But since
\nabla_{i}
is the Chern connection of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
, its curvature is expressed in the holomorphic frame
\ell_{i}
as

{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log h_{i}=2\boldsymbol{i}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\,\log h_{i}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}
.
Changing to the real frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
, we get

(4.23)

\mathsf{d}\Omega_{i}=2\,\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{i}\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}.

Finally, since
\theta={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
is the frame of
\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma
dual to the orthonormal frame
(u_{1},v_{1})
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
,
\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}
equals the volume form
h_{1}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}
of first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}=\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
. It follows that

(4.24)

\theta\wedge{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta=-\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}=-h_{1}\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&-1\\
1&\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}.

Inserting (
4.17
) and (
4.21
)
\sim
(
4.24
) into (
4.2
), we get the required equations.
∎

4.4.
Gauss map

The classical notion of Gauss map has a nice generalization to spacelike surfaces
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
, which plays a key role in
[
10
,
21
]
. In this case, the Gauss map
f
of
\Sigma
takes values in the symmetric space

X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(2,q+1)}
(see §
2.1
) and is defined in such a way that
f(z)
is the timelike totally geodesic
q
-sphere
S\subset\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
passing through
z
with
\mathsf{T}_{z}S
being the orthogonal complement of
\mathsf{T}_{z}\Sigma
in
\mathsf{T}_{z}\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
.
For A-surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, we introduce:

Definition 4.12
.

Given an A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
not contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
, we let
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}:={\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\oplus\cdots
denote the timelike part of the normal bundle of
\Sigma
given by the Frenet splitting and define the Gauss map

f:\Sigma\to X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)}=\big\{\text{timelike totally geodesic $q$-spheres in $\mathbb{H}^{p,q}$}\big\}

in such a way that for every
z\in\Sigma
,
f(z)
is the totally geodesic
q
-sphere
S\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
passing through
z
with
\mathsf{T}_{z}S={\mathcal{N}}^{-}_{z}\subset\mathsf{T}_{z}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
.

Note that since the definition requires the uniqueness of the Frenet splitting (Theorem
4.6

(1)
), the assumption that
\Sigma
is not in a subspace of codimension
4
is indispensable (see Remark
4.8
).

Theorem 4.13
.

In the above setting,
f
is a conformal minimal immersion, with pullback metric

(4.25)

f^{*}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{X}=(8n-4)\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\|^{2}+\cdots+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2}\big){\boldsymbol{g}},

where
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{X}
is the metric on
X:=X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)}
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}=\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
is the first fundamental form of
\Sigma
.

Here, the squared norms in (
4.25
) are as define in Remark
4.11
, while the metric
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{X}
is the one induced by the Killing form
B(x,y)=(2n-1)\Tr(xy)
of
\mathfrak{so}(n,n+1)
.

Proof.

We first describe the metric and connection on
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
(see §
3.5
) by using Lemma
3.8
and the moving frame
(\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})
from the proof of Theorem
4.6
. Viewing
X
as the space of
(q+1)
-dimensional negative definite subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
, by definition of the Gauss map
f
, we can write

f(z)=\mathrm{Span}\big\{\iota(z),\,u_{2}(z),\,v_{2}(z),\,u_{4}(z),\,v_{4}(z),\,\cdots\big\}.

In particular, the matrix-valued function

\widetilde{f}:\Sigma\to G:=\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1),\quad\widetilde{f}:=(u_{1},\,v_{1},\,u_{3},\,v_{3},\,\cdots,\,\iota,\,u_{2},\,v_{2},\,u_{4},\,v_{4},\,\cdots)

is a lift of
f
. Thus, by Lemma
3.8
,
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
identifies locally with the trivial
\mathfrak{p}
-bundle endowed with the metric given by the Killing form
B
and the connection
\widehat{\nabla}
given by the
\End(\mathfrak{p})
-valued
1
-form
\ad(\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega)^{\mathfrak{k}}
, while
\mathsf{d}f\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,f^{*}\mathsf{T}X)
is locally the
\mathfrak{p}
-valued
1
-form
(\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega)^{\mathfrak{p}}
. In order to get more explicit expressions, we view
\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{so}(p,q+1)
and
G
as matrix algebra and matrix group contained in
\mathbb{R}^{(p+q+1)\times(p+q+1)}
, so that the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega
can be written as
g^{-1}\mathsf{d}g
(where
g\in G
is the matrix coordinate), and hence
\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega=\widetilde{f}^{-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f}
. By equation (
4.4
) in the proof of Theorem
4.6
, we obtain

\widetilde{f}^{*}\omega=\widetilde{f}^{-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{matrix}\Omega_{1}&&\\
&\hskip-5.69046pt\Omega_{3}&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&\hskip-4.26773pt\ddots\end{matrix}$}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma\\
\hline\cr\Gamma&\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{matrix}0&&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&\hskip-2.84544pt\Omega_{2}&&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\Omega_{4}&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-2.84544pt\ddots\end{matrix}$}\end{array}\right),

where

(4.26)

\Gamma=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta&&&&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&&&\\
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}&&\\
&&\hskip-8.5359pt\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\\
&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}\text{ if $n$ is even,}\quad\Gamma=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta&&&&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&&&\\
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}&&\\
&&\hskip-8.5359pt\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}\text{ if $n$ is odd}.

Meanwhile, since the components
\mathfrak{k}
and
\mathfrak{p}
of the Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
are

\mathfrak{k}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}&\\
&\xi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\ \Bigg|\ \parbox{56.9055pt}{$\xi_{1}\in\mathfrak{so}(p)$,\\
$\xi_{2}\in\mathfrak{so}(q+1)$}\right\},\quad\mathfrak{p}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\eta\\
\eta&\end{pmatrix}\ \Bigg|\ \eta\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}\right\},

we may identify
\mathfrak{p}
with
\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}
in such a way that
the action of
\mathrm{diag}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\mathfrak{k}
sends
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}
to
\xi_{2}\eta-\eta\xi_{1}
,
while the Killing form
B
on
\mathfrak{p}
is
(4n-2)
-times the standard metric of
\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}
.
Therefore, we conclude that
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
identifies locally with the trivial
\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}
-bundle endowed with the metric
\langle\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\rangle=(4n-2)\Tr({\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\eta_{1}\eta_{2})
and the connection
\widehat{\nabla}
defined by

\widehat{\nabla}\eta=\mathsf{d}\eta+\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}0&&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&\hskip-2.84544pt\Omega_{2}&&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\Omega_{4}&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-2.84544pt\ddots\end{pmatrix}$}\eta-\eta\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\Omega_{1}&&\\
&\hskip-5.69046pt\Omega_{3}&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&\hskip-4.26773pt\ddots\end{pmatrix}$},

while
\mathsf{d}f\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,f^{*}\mathsf{T}X)
is locally the
\mathbb{R}^{p\times(q+1)}
-valued
1
-form
\Gamma
in (
4.26
).

Next, we verify the required expression (
4.25
) of
f^{*}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{X}
. Using the above identifications, we obtain by computations that

f^{*}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{X}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})=B(\mathsf{d}f({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),\mathsf{d}f({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}))=(4n-2)\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=(4n-2)\Big\{{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\theta({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})+\sum_{j=2}^{n}\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]\Big\},

where “
\,\Tr\,
” takes the matrix trace of each matrix-valued
2
-tensor to produce a scalar
2
-tensor.
Since
\theta={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
is the frame of
\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma
dual to the orthonormal frame
(u_{1},v_{1})
of
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
, the term
{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\theta({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is just the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}
. To compute the term
\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]
, we pick a coordinate
z
of
\Sigma
and a holomorphic local frame
\ell_{i}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
as in Theorem
4.10
and let
(u_{i},v_{i})
be as in the proof of that theorem. Using the expression of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
given in that proof, we get, for
2\leq j\leq n-1
,

\displaystyle\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{h_{j}}{h_{j-1}}\Tr\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
-\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}\otimes\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2\,h_{j}}{h_{j-1}}\big(\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\otimes\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})+\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\otimes\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2\,h_{j}}{h_{j-1}}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}({\mathsf{d}x}^{2}+{\mathsf{d}y}^{2})=\tfrac{2\,h_{j}}{h_{1}h_{j-1}}|\alpha_{j}|^{2}h_{1}({\mathsf{d}x}^{2}+{\mathsf{d}y}^{2})=2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}\|^{2}{\boldsymbol{g}}.

As for
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
, we have

\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]+\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]+\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]+\Tr\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big].

By similar computations, the last two terms vanish and the first two terms equal
2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}{\boldsymbol{g}}
and
2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2}{\boldsymbol{g}}
, respectively. Putting the results together, we get (
4.25
).

This already implies that
f
is a conformal immersion. We finally show that
f
is minimal via Lemma
3.7
. Note that the above local description of
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
effectively identifies
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
globally as the homomorphism bundle
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{3}\oplus\cdots,\ \underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\oplus\cdots)
with metric and connection induced by those on the trivial line bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
and each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
.
In particular,

(4.27)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k},{\mathcal{L}}_{l}),\ \ \mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{k},\underline{\mathbb{R}})\quad(k\in\{1,3,\cdots\},\ l\in\{2,4,\cdots\})

are parallel subbundles orthogonal to each other.
Meanwhile, by the expression (
4.26
), the only nonzero components of
\mathsf{d}f=\Gamma
are
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{l}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{l-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{l}))
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}^{*}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k},{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1}))
and
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},\underline{\mathbb{R}}))=C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma\otimes\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma)
(the last one corresponds to the term
{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta
). To apply Lemma
3.7
, we need to give
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
an orthogonal, parallel, complex structure under which
\mathsf{d}f
is a
(1,0)
-form. Since each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is a hermitian holomorphic vector bundle (Theorem
4.6
), so is every subbundle in (
4.27
) (see the paragraph preceding Lemma
4.9
), and the component
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{l}
is a
(1,0)
-form by Theorem
4.6
. Also, the component
{\boldsymbol{g}}
is a
(1,0)
-form since the metric
{\boldsymbol{g}}
is conformal. On the other hand, the component
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}^{*}
is a
(0,1)
-form, but we can invert the complex structure on
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k},{\mathcal{L}}_{k-1})
to make it a
(1,0)
-from. Thus, we conclude that
f^{*}\mathsf{T}X
does admit a complex structure fulfilling the assumption of Lemma
3.7
, so we can apply the lemma to complete the proof.
∎

4.5.
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curves in
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
as A-surfaces

We now restrict to the case
n=3
and consider the interplay between A-surfaces
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
and the almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
on
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
(see §
2.6
).
By Lemma
2.12
and the description of
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}
in Corollary
2.14
, we immediately deduce from Theorem
4.13
:

Corollary 4.14
.

For any A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
which is not part of a totally geodesic
\mathbb{H}^{2}\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
, the following conditions are equivalent to each other:

•

the Gauss map
f:\Sigma\to X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)}
takes values in the totally geodesic submanifold
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}\subset X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)}
(hence is a conformal minimal immersion into
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}\,
);

•

the subbundle bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\subset\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}|_{\Sigma}
is preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
.

A particularly interesting subclass of A-surfaces satisfying these conditions are:

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
be a spacelike
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve with nowhere vanishing second fundamental form. Then
\Sigma
is an A-surface if and only if the osculation line
\{{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,Y)\mid X,Y\in\mathsf{T}_{z}\Sigma\}\subset{\mathcal{N}}_{z}
(see §
3.4
) is negative definite for all
z\in\Sigma
. In this case, the whole Frenet splitting
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
is preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
. In particular,
\Sigma
satisfies the conditions in Corollary
4.14
.

Proof.

If
\Sigma
is a
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve and an A-surface at the same time, then the fiber of
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
at any
z\in\Sigma
where
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is nonzero is exactly the osculation line at
z
, so the “only if” part is trivial. Conversely, assuming that the osculation lines are negative definite, we let
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
be the subbundle of the normal bundle
{\mathcal{N}}
formed by these complex lines and
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
be the orthogonal complement of
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
in
{\mathcal{N}}
. Since the almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
of
\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
is orthogonal, the subbundles
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
of
\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}|_{\Sigma}
form a Frenet splitting preserved by
{\boldsymbol{J}}
. Also,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}(X)={\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}(X,{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}):{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
is conformal because
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
is complex bilinear by Proposition
3.4

(2)
. Therefore,
\Sigma
is an A-surface. This proves the “if” part and the “In this case” statement.
∎

Remark 4.16
.

The A-surfaces that we construct from cyclic
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles in §
6.3
below belong to the above subclass. They also have the following extra properties by construction:

•

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{+}
is nowhere zero as well; as a consequence,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{3}
identify with
{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
,
{\mathcal{K}}^{-2}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{-3}
, respectively, as holomorphic line bundles;

•

the hermitian metrics
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}
and
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{3}
on these line bundles satisfy
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{3}={\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}/4
.

By using
G_{2}^{\prime}
-moving frames, it can be shown that these properties are not ad hoc and are local in nature. Namely, they are satisfied by every A-surface
\Sigma
as in Proposition
4.15
.

Remark 4.17
.

As the concluding remark for this section, we note that all the results till now are local in nature. Therefore, Theorems
4.6
,
4.10
and Proposition
4.15
still hold if the ambient space
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
is replaced by an orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold locally modeled on
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, or more generally if
\Sigma
is replaced by an
A-surface immersion
from the universal cover
\widetilde{\Sigma}
of an abstract surface
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
which is equivariant with respect to some representation
\rho
of
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
in
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
or
G_{2}^{\prime}
. In particular, we can treat the structural data of such an immersion as defined on
\Sigma
rather than
\widetilde{\Sigma}
by virtue of the invariance under the
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
action. The definition and results concerning the Gauss map
f
also hold in this equivariant setting, and
f
is
\rho
-equivariant as well.

5.
Infinitesimal rigidity and unique determination from Gauss map

In this section, we restrict to A-surfaces
\Sigma
satisfying an extra hypothesis related to Dai-Li’s work
[
24
]
and prove two result for them: the first is the core result of this paper that
\Sigma
is infinitesimally rigid; the second is that
\Sigma
is determined by its Gauss map up to the antipodal map of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
.

5.1.
Hypothesis (

⋆

‣
5.1
) and a sufficient condition

Fix an oriented A-surface
\Sigma
in an orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
locally modeled on
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
with
n=\frac{p+q}{2}\geq 3
. Also assume that
\Sigma
is not locally contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
, so that we have a unique Frenet splitting
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
by Theorem
4.6
and Remark
4.17
, with each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
being a hermitian holomorphc line bundle. The extra hypothesis that we consider in this section can be simply stated as
the first
n-1
line bundles
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}
have semi-negative Chern forms
. Here, the
Chern form
is
\tfrac{\boldsymbol{i}}{2\pi}
-times the curvature form of the Chern connection, which is an
\mathbb{R}
-valued
2
-form representing the first Chern class, and we call a
2
-form
\omega

semi-negative
if the open set
\{\omega\neq 0\}\subset\Sigma
is dense and
\omega
is opposite to the orientation of
\Sigma
on this set. Although this notion of negativity depends on the choice of orientation for
\Sigma
, the hypothesis as a whole does not, because reversing the orientation of
\Sigma
will also reverse the one of each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
with
2\leq i\leq n-1
(see Remark
4.7
), hence will reverse the complex structure and Chern form of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
as well.

Remark 5.1
.

It is reasonable that the semi-negativity assumption is not imposed on
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
: unlike
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
with
2\leq i\leq n-1
, whose orientation (and hence complex structure) is uniquely determined, we are free to choose the orientation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
, which just reflects the orientation of the ambient space
M
(see Remark
4.7
), and such a choice is irrelevant to what we are doing.

By virtue of Theorem
4.10
, we can reformulate the hypothesis as follows. Since the Chern form of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
is expressed locally as
\tfrac{\boldsymbol{i}}{2\pi}{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log h_{i}=-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{i}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}
, the hypothesis is equivalent to (see Theorem
4.10
and Remark
4.11
for the notations):

(
\star
)

\parbox{82.51282pt}{on an open dense subset of $\Sigma$, }\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\|^{2}>0,\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k+1}\|^{2}>0\quad(2\leq k\leq n-2),\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n-1}=\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2}>0.\end{cases}

This clearly implies some restrictions on the zeros of the holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
. For example, the last inequality in (

⋆

‣
5.1
) forces every zero of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
to be a zero of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
at the same time.
More specifically, letting
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
denote the divisor
3
3

3

When
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\not\equiv 0
, the divisor
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
is just the
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-valued function on
\Sigma
assigning to every
z\in\Sigma
the multiplicity of zero of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
at
z
. When
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\equiv 0
, we let
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
be the constant function
+\infty
by convention.

of any holomorphic section
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
, one may check that a necessary condition for (

⋆

‣
5.1
) is

(5.1)

({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})\leq\cdots\leq({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})\leq\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\big\},

where “
\leq
” and “
\min
” are pointwise inequality and minimum of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{+\infty\}
-valued functions on
\Sigma
.

When
\Sigma
is closed, we generalize the work of Dai and Li
[
24
]
to get the theorem below on the affine Toda system. It implies that a specific condition stronger than (
5.1
) is sufficient for (

⋆

‣
5.1
), although we do not know whether (
5.1
) itself is sufficient.
The condition is given by replacing every “
\leq
” in (
5.1
) by a stronger relation “
\prec
”: here “
D_{1}\prec D_{2}
” means that the divisor
D_{1}
is strictly less than that of
D_{2}
at every point where the former is nonzero (which always holds when
D_{1}=\emptyset
).

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface of genus
\geq 2
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
(
n\geq 2
) be holomorphic line bundles on
\Sigma
and
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
be a hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
(
2\leq j\leq n-1
) and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}

be holomorphic sections of the line bundles
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm 1}
, respectively, with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\not\equiv 0
. Suppose the following equations (with the same local notation as in Theorem
4.10
) hold on
\Sigma
:

(5.2)

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$:}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=h_{1}|\alpha_{1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}^{+}|^{2}-\frac{1}{h_{1}h_{2}}|\alpha_{2}^{-}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}=\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}^{+}|^{2}-\frac{1}{h_{1}h_{2}}|\alpha_{2}^{-}|^{2};\end{cases}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\text{if $n\geq 3$:}\quad\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=h_{1}|\alpha_{1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\alpha_{2}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\tfrac{h_{k}}{h_{k-1}}|\alpha_{k}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{k+1}}{h_{k}}|\alpha_{k+1}|^{2}\quad\text{for $2\leq k\leq n-2$},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n-1}=\tfrac{h_{n-1}}{h_{n-2}}|\alpha_{n-1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}=\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\alpha_{n}^{+}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}|\alpha_{n}^{-}|^{2}.\end{cases}

Then the following statements hold.

(1)

If

({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\big\}
,
then we have
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{i}\geq 0
on
\Sigma
for
i=1,\cdots,n-1
, with strict inequality away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}
.

(2)

If
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+})\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\neq\emptyset
, then
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}\geq 0
on
\Sigma
, with strict inequality away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
.

The proof is directly adapted from Dai-Li’s and is postponed to the appendix.

Remark 5.3
.

For the sake of completeness, we have given in Theorem
5.2
a statement that is stronger than required in two ways. First,
the setting is more general than the one we are working on: the line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
is not necessarily
{\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
, so an extra
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}{\mathcal{L}}_{1})
comes up correspondingly. Second, Parts
(1)
and
(2)
are about the left-hand side of the 1st to
(n-1)
th and the last equations in (
5.3
), respectively, but we only need the former. The original result of Dai-Li (see
[
24
, Lemma 5.4]
) is the two parts combined (for
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1})=\cdots=({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})=({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+})=\emptyset
), without noticing that they are unrelated.

Remark 5.4
.
