Without affecting the statement, we can add to Theorem
5.2
the extra assumption that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\not\equiv 0
. In fact, if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\not\equiv 0
but
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\equiv 0
, we can switch the roles of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}
, which interchanges
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
and does affect the statement. On the other hand, if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\equiv 0
, then the statement just reduces from
n
to the
n-1
case, with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}^{-}\equiv 0
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}^{+}
being the original
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
, whereas the
n=2
case reduces to a trivial statement.

5.2.
Infinitesimal rigidity

An A-surface in a
4
-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold is just a spacelike minimal surface with timelike normal bundle (see Remark
4.4
). In this case, by Proposition
3.2
,
\Sigma
locally maximizes the area as long as
M
is negatively curved. We show the following generalization to higher dimensions (see Theorem
4.6
, Remark
4.17
and §
3.3
for notation and background):

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\Sigma
be an A-surface in an orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
locally modeled on
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
with
n=\frac{p+q}{2}\geq 3
. Suppose
\Sigma
is not locally contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
and its structural data satisfies (

⋆

‣
5.1
). Consider the spacelike part
{\mathcal{N}}^{+}:={\mathcal{L}}_{3}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{5}\oplus\cdots
and timelike part
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}:={\mathcal{L}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{4}\oplus\cdots
of the normal bundle
{\mathcal{N}}
of
\Sigma
. Then for any compactly supported variation of
\Sigma
whose variational vector field
\xi
is a section of
{\mathcal{N}}^{+}
(resp.
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}
) and is not identically zero, the associated second variation of volume is positive (resp. negative).

Remark 5.6
.

Besides maximal surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
, minimal surfaces in hyperbolic
3
-manifolds have also been extensively studied (see e.g.
[
43
,
58
,
59
]
). For these surfaces,
we cannot expect such kind of variational property to hold in general, because the term in the second variation formula given by the second fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}
has different sign from the Laplacian term and the curvature term, which makes the sign of the whole formula indefinite.
The common feature of maximal surfaces and A-surfaces which frees us from this issue is that
all osculation vectors are timelike (see Remark
4.2
), hence the
{\boldsymbol{I\!\!I}}
-term has the correct sign.

Proof.

Recall from Theorem
3.1

(1)
that the second variation of volume is given by the integral

\int_{\Sigma}\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\Big(\langle\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi,\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi\rangle-\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle-\langle R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi\rangle\Big)\mathsf{d}\mathit{vol}_{\boldsymbol{g}}

(where the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}
is the real part of the hermitian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}=\mathsf{T}\Sigma
). To prove the theorem, we shall show that the integrand satisfies

(5.4)

\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\Big(\langle\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi,\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{\mathcal{N}}\xi\rangle-\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle-\langle R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi\rangle\Big)\ \left\{\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{array}[]{l}\geq 0\ \text{ if $\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+})$}\\
\leq 0\ \text{ if $\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-})$}\end{array}\right.

everywhere on
\Sigma
, with strict inequality on an open dense subset of
\{\xi\neq 0\}
.

To this end, suppose the connection
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
decomposes under the splitting
{\mathcal{N}}={\mathcal{N}}^{+}\oplus{\mathcal{N}}^{-}
as

(5.5)

\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}^{+}}&\Gamma_{0}^{-}\\
\Gamma_{0}^{+}&\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}^{-}}\end{pmatrix},

where
\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}}
is a connection on
{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}
and
\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}
is a
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{N}}^{\pm},{\mathcal{N}}^{\mp})
-valued
1
-form. Then the left-hand side of (
5.4
) can be rewritten as

\underbrace{\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}}\xi,\nabla_{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}}\xi\rangle}_{\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{1}}}}+\underbrace{\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle}_{\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{2}}}}\ \underbrace{-\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\rangle}_{\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{3}}}}\ \underbrace{-\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle R_{M}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi){\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},\xi\rangle}_{\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}}}\penalty\ ,

where every “
\pm
” is taken to be
+
or
-
when
\xi
is a section of
{\mathcal{N}}^{+}
or
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}
, respectively. We shall prove (
5.4
) by showing that the terms \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{1}}⃝, \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{3}}⃝ and \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}⃝ have the correct sign for trivial reason (similarly as in the proof of Proposition
3.2
), whereas the term \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{2}}⃝ is controlled by \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}⃝ by virtue of Hypothesis (

⋆

‣
5.1
).

For the term \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{1}}⃝, since
{\mathcal{N}}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}
are positive and negative definite respectively, we immediately have
\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{1}}}\geq 0
and
\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{1}}}\leq 0
for
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+})
and
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-})
, respectively.

For the term \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{3}}⃝, recall from Remark
4.2
that the shape operator
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,\End(\mathsf{T}\Sigma))
assigned to a normal vector field
\xi
is identically zero when
\xi
is orthogonal to
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
. In particular, we have
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}=0
if
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+})
. Therefore, given an orthonormal local frame
(e_{1},e_{2})
of
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
, we have

\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{3}}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(e_{i}),{\boldsymbol{S}}_{\xi}(e_{i})\rangle\ \left\{\hskip-2.84544pt\begin{array}[]{l}=0\ \text{ if $\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+})$},\\
\leq 0\ \text{ if $\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-})$}.\end{array}\right.

For the term \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}⃝, note that at any
z\in\Sigma
with
\xi(z)\neq 0
, the sectional curvature of
M
along the tangent
2
-plane spanned by
e_{i}
and
\xi
is

K(e_{i}\wedge\xi)=\frac{\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle}{\langle\xi,\xi\rangle}=-1
. Therefore, at the point
z
, we have

\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\langle R_{M}(e_{i},\xi)e_{i},\xi\rangle=2\langle\xi,\xi\rangle.

This clearly holds at the points where
\xi=0
as well, hence holds throughout
\Sigma
. In particular, we have
\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}}\geq 0
and
\textcircled{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}}\leq 0
for
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+})
and
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-})
, respectively.

In the rest of the proof, we show that the absolute value of the term \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{2}}⃝ is controlled by that of \raisebox{-0.5pt}{\small{4}}⃝:

(5.6)

|\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle|\leq 2|\langle\xi,\xi\rangle|\ \text{ for any }\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}),

and moreover the inequality is strict on an open dense subset of
\{\xi\neq 0\}
. This implies the required inequality (
5.4
) and prove the theorem.

In order to show (
5.6
), we first deduce from the decomposition (
4.2
) of the connection
\nabla
on
\mathsf{T}M|_{\Sigma}
the following more explicit expression of
\Gamma_{0}^{+}
(which is just the
\Gamma
in the proof of Theorem
4.13
with the first row and first column removed):

\Gamma_{0}^{+}=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{*}&&&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}^{*}&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&\ddots&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}^{*}\\
&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}\text{ if $n$ is even,}\quad\Gamma_{0}^{+}=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{*}&&&&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}^{*}&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&\ddots&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}^{*}&\\
&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}$}\text{ if $n$ is odd.}

We proceed to compute
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
. Pick an orthonormal local frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
of each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
as in the proof of Theorem
4.10
, so that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
can be viewed as a
2\times 2
-matrix of
1
-forms with the explicit expression given in that proof, while
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{*}
and
\Gamma_{0}^{-}
are the transposes of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
and
\Gamma_{0}^{+}
respectively. We also use the frame to view a section
\xi
of
{\mathcal{N}}^{+}
or
{\mathcal{N}}^{-}
as a column vector of functions. Then we may write

\langle\xi,\xi\rangle=\pm{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\,\xi
and
\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle=\mp{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\ {\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\ \Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi
for any
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm})
.
Thus, when
n
is even, we get by matrix computations that

\langle\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle=-{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{34.14322pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}$}}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}&&\\[8.5359pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{34.14322pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{6}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{6}$}}&\ddots&\\[8.5359pt]
&\ddots&\ddots&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
&&\hskip-14.22636pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{45.52458pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}}\end{pmatrix}$}\xi\quad\text{ for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+}),

\langle\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&&&\\[8.5359pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{34.14322pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}$}}&\ddots&&\\[8.5359pt]
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\[8.5359pt]
&&\ddots&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{54.06006pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$}}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}\xi\quad\text{ for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-}),

where
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}
is understood as the
2\times 2
-matrix-valued
2
-tensor
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})
, and similarly for the other terms in the matrices. When
n
is odd, the results become

\langle\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle=-{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{34.14322pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}$}}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}&&\\[8.5359pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&\ddots&\ddots&\\[8.5359pt]
&\ddots&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{54.06006pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$}}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}\xi\quad\text{ for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{+}),

\langle\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\xi\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}&&\\[8.5359pt]
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{34.14322pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}$}}&\ddots&\\[8.5359pt]
&\ddots&\ddots&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[8.5359pt]
&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&\scalebox{0.85}{\parbox{54.06006pt}{${\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}$\\
$+{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}$}}\end{pmatrix}$}\xi\quad\text{ for }\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}}^{-}).

The trace of each
2
-tensor in these matrices with respect to
{\boldsymbol{g}}
(not to be confused with the matrix trace considered in the proof of Theorem
4.13
) is computed as follows:

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
I
denote the
2\times 2
identity matrix. For
2\leq i,j\leq n-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=0\quad\text{(the zero $2\times 2$ matrix)},

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}\|^{2}I,

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{i}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=0,

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=2(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2})I,

\displaystyle\Tr_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}\big[{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}){\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=2\left[(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2})I+\tfrac{2}{h_{1}h_{n-1}}\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}})&\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}})\end{pmatrix}$}\right]

(see Theorem
4.10
and Remark
4.11
for the notations). Moreover, the eigenvalues of the right-hand side of the last equation are
2(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|\pm\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|)^{2}
.

Proof.

The expressions of the
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
’s given in the proof of Theorem
4.10
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{j}}}{\sqrt{h_{j-1}}}\left[\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{j})&-\im(\alpha_{j})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{j})&\re(\alpha_{j})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}+\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}-\im(\alpha_{j})&-\re(\alpha_{j})\\[2.84544pt]
\re(\alpha_{j})&-\im(\alpha_{j})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}y}\right]\quad(2\leq j\leq n-1),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-},\text{ where}

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{n}}}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}}}\left[\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{+})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{+})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{n}^{+})&\re(\alpha_{n}^{+})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}+\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}-\im(\alpha_{n}^{+})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{+})\\[2.84544pt]
\re(\alpha_{n}^{+})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{+})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}y}\right],

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}h_{n}}}\left[\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{-})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-})\\[2.84544pt]
-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{-})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}x}+\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{-})\\[2.84544pt]
-\re(\alpha_{n}^{-})&\im(\alpha_{n}^{-})\end{pmatrix}$}{\mathsf{d}y}\right].

Taking account of the fact that

\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big({\mathsf{d}x}\otimes{\mathsf{d}x}\big)=\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big({\mathsf{d}y}\otimes{\mathsf{d}y}\big)=\tfrac{1}{h_{1}}\penalty\ ,\quad\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big({\mathsf{d}x}\otimes{\mathsf{d}y}\big)=\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big({\mathsf{d}y}\otimes{\mathsf{d}x}\big)=0

(because
{\boldsymbol{g}}=\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}=h_{1}({\mathsf{d}x}^{2}+{\mathsf{d}y}^{2})
), we obtain the required equalities and the eigenvalues by direct computations.
∎

Using the expressions of
\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
and Lemma
5.7
, we get expressions for
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
, from which it is clear that at any point of
\Sigma
, both of the quadratic form
\xi\mapsto-\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{+}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
and
\xi\mapsto\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{-}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
have eigenvalues
belonging to the list

\displaystyle 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\|^{2},\quad 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}\|^{2},\quad 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{5}\|^{2},\ \cdots

\displaystyle\quad\cdots,\ 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2},\quad 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2},\quad 2(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|\pm\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|)^{2}.

But Hypothesis (

⋆

‣
5.1
) implies that the following inequalities hold on an open dense set
U\subset\Sigma
:

(5.7)

\tfrac{1}{2}(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|\pm\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|)^{2}<\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2}<\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2}<\ \cdots\ <\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\|^{2}<\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\|^{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}.

Therefore, all the eigenvalues are strictly less than
2
on
U
. This implies the required inequality (
5.6
) and completes the proof.
∎

This theorem can be roughly understood as saying that A-surfaces satisfying the assumptions are saddle type critical points for the area functional. Such critical points are nondegenerate. In fact, it is a simple linear algebraic fact that if a symmetric matrix
L
has the form

L=\begin{pmatrix}L_{+}&*\\
*&L_{-}\end{pmatrix}

such that the blocks
L_{+}
and
L_{-}
are positive definite and negative definite respectively, then
L
is nondegenerate. Generalizing this to infinite dimensions and considering the Jacobi operator
L_{\Sigma}
in place of
L
, we obtain:

Corollary 5.8
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
5.5
,
\Sigma
does not admit any nonzero compactly supported Jacobi field.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}})
be a compactly supported Jacobi field and write
\xi=\xi_{+}+\xi_{-}
, where
\xi_{\pm}
is a compactly supported section of
{\mathcal{N}}^{\pm}
. Since
L_{\Sigma}\xi=0
, we have

\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{+},\,\xi_{+}\rangle+\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{-},\,\xi_{+}\rangle=\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi,\,\xi_{+}\rangle=0,\quad\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{-},\,\xi_{-}\rangle+\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{+},\,\xi_{-}\rangle=\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi,\,\xi_{-}\rangle=0.

So the following equalities (between functions on
\Sigma
) hold:

\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{-},\,\xi_{+}\rangle=-\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{+},\,\xi_{+}\rangle,\quad\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{+},\,\xi_{-}\rangle=-\langle L_{\Sigma}\xi_{-},\,\xi_{-}\rangle.

The second variation formula (see Theorem
3.1
) says that for any compactly supported
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{N}})
, the integral of
-\langle L_{\Sigma}\eta,\eta\rangle
on
\Sigma
is exactly the second variation of volume associated to
\eta
. Therefore, by Theorem
5.5
, the integral of the first (resp. second) function above is
\geq 0
(resp.
\leq 0
), with strict inequality unless
\xi_{+}\equiv 0
(resp.
\xi_{-}\equiv 0
). But the two integrals coincide because
L_{\Sigma}
is self-adjoint (see §
3.2
). So we conclude that
\xi_{\pm}
are both identically zero.
∎

In particular, if
\Sigma
is closed, then it does not admit any nonzero Jacobi field at all. Note that we still assume Hypothesis (

⋆

‣
5.1
) here. Applying the sufficient condition for (

⋆

‣
5.1
) in terms of divisors from Theorem
5.2

(1)
(with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
), we obtain:

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed A-surface of genus
\geq 2
in an orientable pseudo-Riemannian manifold
M
locally modeled on
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
with
n=\frac{p+q}{2}\geq 3
. Suppose
\Sigma
is not locally contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
and the holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
in its structural data satisfy
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})\prec\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\big\}
. Then
\Sigma
does not admit any nonzero Jacobi field.

5.3.
Uniqueness of A-surface with given Gauss map

An A-surface
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
is not completely determined by its Gauss map
f:\Sigma\to X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)}
, as the antipodal surface
-\Sigma
has the same Gauss map
(more precisely, the latter is the composition of
f
with the antipodal map
-\Sigma\to\Sigma
; this can be easily seen by using the setup in the proof of Theorem
4.13
). Again under Hypothesis (

⋆

‣
5.1
), we may show that this is the only ambiguity:

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
be an A-surface, where
n=\frac{p+q}{2}\geq 3
. Suppose
\Sigma
is not contained in any pseudo-hyperbolic subspace of codimension
4
and satisfies (

⋆

‣
5.1
). Let
\iota^{\prime}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
be an A-surface immersion with the same Gauss map as
\Sigma
. Then
\iota^{\prime}
is the restriction of either the identity map or the antipodal map of
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
to
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let
\iota,u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n}
be locally defined maps from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
as in the proofs of Theorem
4.6
and Theorem
4.13
(where
\iota
is the inclusion), so that the matrix-valued function

\widetilde{f}:=(u_{1},\,v_{1},\,u_{3},\,v_{3},\,\cdots,\ \iota,\,u_{2},\,v_{2},\,u_{4},\,v_{4})

is a locally defined lift of the Gauss map
f
of
\Sigma
and we have

(5.8)

\widetilde{f}^{-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f}=\begin{pmatrix}*&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma\\
\Gamma&*\end{pmatrix},

where
\Gamma
has the expression (
4.26
) in terms of the structural
1
-forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}
of
\Sigma
.

For any another A-surface immersion
\iota^{\prime}
, we again have locally defined maps
u^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,u^{\prime}_{n},v^{\prime}_{n}
from
\Sigma
to
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
such that

\widetilde{f}^{\prime}:=(u^{\prime}_{1},\,v^{\prime}_{1},\,u^{\prime}_{3},\,v^{\prime}_{3},\,\cdots,\ \iota^{\prime},\,u^{\prime}_{2},\,v^{\prime}_{2},\,u^{\prime}_{4},\,v^{\prime}_{4})

lifts the Gauss map
f^{\prime}
of
\iota^{\prime}
and that

(5.9)

\widetilde{f}^{\prime-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}*&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma^{\prime}\\
\Gamma^{\prime}&*\end{pmatrix},

with
\Gamma^{\prime}
given by the same expression (
4.26
) as
\Gamma
except that each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
is replaced by the
1
-forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}^{\prime}
coming from
\iota^{\prime}
, which might be different from
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
a priori.

Now assume
f^{\prime}=f
. Then there is a locally defined map
g=(g_{1},g_{2})
from
\Sigma
to
\mathrm{SO}(p)\times\mathrm{SO}(q+1)
such that
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}g
, and it follows that
\widetilde{f}^{\prime-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f}^{\prime}=g^{-1}(\widetilde{f}^{-1}\mathsf{d}\widetilde{f})g+g^{-1}\mathsf{d}g
. Comparing with (
5.8
) and (
5.9
), we infer
that
\Gamma^{\prime}=g_{2}^{-1}\Gamma g_{1}
, and as a consequence,

(5.10)

\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big[\Gamma^{\prime}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma^{\prime}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=g_{2}^{-1}\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big[\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]g_{2},

where we take the tensor traces of matrix-valued
2
-tensors with respect to the first fundamental form
{\boldsymbol{g}}
(as in the proof of Theorem
5.5
) to produce matrix-valued functions. These traces can be calculated similarly as the calculation of
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi,\Gamma_{0}^{\pm}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\xi\rangle
in the proof of Theorem
5.5
(the
\Gamma_{0}^{+}
therein is just
\Gamma
with the first row and column removed). The result is

(5.11)

\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big[\Gamma^{\prime}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma^{\prime}({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\begin{pmatrix}2&\\
&\Lambda^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big[\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]=\begin{pmatrix}2&\\
&\Lambda\end{pmatrix},

where the matrix
\Lambda
is diagonal except for a possible
2\times 2
block and its eigenvalues belong to the list

\displaystyle 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\|^{2},\quad 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{4}\|^{2},\ \cdots

\displaystyle\quad\cdots,\ 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2},\quad 2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|^{2}+2\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|^{2},\quad 2(\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\|\pm\|{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\|)^{2}.

The matrix
\Lambda^{\prime}
is the same except that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
are replaced by
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\prime}{\!\!}{}^{\pm}
.

We may now apply condition (

⋆

‣
5.1
), which implies inequalities (
5.7
) on an open dense set
U\subset\Sigma
. It follows that at every point in
U
, the sole largest eigenvalue of
\Tr_{\boldsymbol{g}}\big[\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\,{\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\Gamma({\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,})\big]
is
2
. In view of (
5.10
) and (
5.11
), this forces the
\mathrm{SO}(q+1)
-valued function
g_{2}
to have the form

g_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}\pm 1&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}

on
U
. By continuity, it has the same form throughout
\Sigma
. Since
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}=\widetilde{f}g
, we conclude that
\iota^{\prime}=\pm\iota
.
∎

6.
Application to cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles

In this section, we review some backgrounds on cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle before showing in Theorem
6.3
(
\approx
Theorem
B
) that such Higgs bundles yield A-surfaces, then we use the infinitesimal rigidity result to prove Theorem
D
(restated as Theorem
6.6
) and finally show in Proposition
6.8
that in the
G_{2}^{\prime}
case these A-surfaces belong to the type from Proposition
4.15
.

6.1.
Cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles and A-surfaces

We refer to
[
3
,
19
,
21
,
31
]
for the definition of
G
-Higgs bundles for a general real reductive group
G
and the special case of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q)
-Higgs bundles. We only consider here the following particular type of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles:

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface. Given holomorphic line bundles
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
(
n\geq 2
) on
\Sigma
and holomorphic sections

{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
(
2\leq j\leq n-1
) and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
of the line bundles
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm 1}
, respectively, with
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}\not\equiv 0
, we definite the
cyclic
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle
associated to these data as the
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle
({\mathcal{V}},Q_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{W}},Q_{\mathcal{W}},\eta)
given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{-n+1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{-n+3}\oplus\ \cdots\ \oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n-3}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1},\quad Q_{\mathcal{V}}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\[-2.84544pt]
&\iddots&\\[-2.84544pt]
1&&\end{pmatrix}$},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}_{-n}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{-n+2}\oplus\ \cdots\ \oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\quad Q_{\mathcal{W}}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\[-2.84544pt]
&\iddots&\\[-2.84544pt]
1&&\end{pmatrix}$},

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}*&&&*\\[-2.84544pt]
&\ddots&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&*&\end{pmatrix}$}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{V}})),

where we set
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}:={\mathcal{O}}
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{m}:={\mathcal{L}}_{-m}^{-1}
for
m<0
, and let each “
*
” in the matrix expression of
\eta
be one of the above holomorphic sections, namely the unique one which fits in that slot (for example, the first row is
({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+},0,\cdots,0,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})
, the second is
(0,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-2},0,\cdots,0)
, etc.).

Alternatively, we may define the above
({\mathcal{V}},Q_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{W}},Q_{\mathcal{W}},\eta)
as the
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle whose underlying
\mathrm{GL}(2n+1,\mathbb{C})
-Higgs bundle is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{V}}\oplus{\mathcal{W}}={\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n},

\displaystyle\hskip 7.96674pt\Phi=\scalebox{0.85}{$\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc|c|ccccc}0&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}&\\
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}&0&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\\
&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&\ddots&&&&&&&&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&\\
&&&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&0&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}&0&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}&0&&&&\\
&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\ddots&&&\\
&&&&&&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&&&&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}&0&\\
&&&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}&0\end{array}\right)$}.

We emphasize that each of the sub-diagonal holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
is assumed not to be identically zero, although
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
can be zero.
Also note that the trivial line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}
is a summand in
{\mathcal{V}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{W}}
) when
n
is odd (resp. even) and we can choose a global holomorphic section
\underline{1}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
with
Q_{\mathcal{V}}(\underline{1}\,,\,\underline{1})\equiv 1
(resp.
Q_{\mathcal{W}}(\underline{1}\,,\,\underline{1})\equiv 1
). This section is unique up to sign. We let
\underline{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathbb{R}\underline{1}
be the real line subbundle of
{\mathcal{O}}
generated by it.

In general, a polystable
G
-Higgs bundle admits a canonical
K
-reduction (
K\subset G
denotes the maximal compact subgroup), namely the one solving the Hitchin equation, as well as a canonical flat
G
-connection
D
, namely the Hitchin connection. Applying the parallel transportation of
D
to the
K
-reduction, we get a harmonic map
f
from
\widetilde{\Sigma}
to the symmetric space
X_{G}=G/K
which is equivariant with respect to the holonomy representation of
D
. For the above particular
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles,
f
is a conformal minimal immersion and these objects have a more concrete description:

Proposition/Definition 6.2
.

The above
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is stable if and only if either
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\not\equiv 0
or
\deg({\mathcal{L}}_{n})<0
. In this case, there is a unique hermitian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
on each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
such that the hermitian metric

H:={\boldsymbol{h}}_{n}^{-1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}^{-1}\oplus 1\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{n}

on
{\mathcal{E}}
(where the middle
1
denotes the hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{O}}
under which the section
\underline{1}
has unit norm) satisfies the
Hitchin equation

(6.1)

F_{\nabla^{H}}+[\Phi,\Phi^{*}]=0.

Here,
\nabla^{H}
is the Chern connection of
({\mathcal{E}},H)
and
\Phi^{*}
is the
\End({\mathcal{E}})
-valued
(0,1)
-form
H
-adjoint to
\Phi
.

Eq.(
6.1
) coincides with equations (
5.2
) or (
5.3
) in Theorem
5.2

and is equivalent to the condition that the real connection

D^{H}:=\nabla^{H}+\Phi+\Phi^{*}

on
{\mathcal{E}}
is flat. The metric
H
and the connection
D^{H}
are called the
harmonic metric
and
Hitchin connection
of
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
, respectively. We also refer to
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
as the harmonic metric on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
. Further let

•

Q_{\mathcal{E}}
be the holomorphic quadratic form
Q_{\mathcal{V}}\oplus(-Q_{\mathcal{W}})
on
{\mathcal{E}}
;

•

\lambda_{i}
be the anti-linear involution of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
given by

\lambda_{i}(\ell_{i}^{-1})=\tfrac{1}{h_{i}}\ell_{i},\quad\lambda_{i}(\ell_{i})=h_{i}\ell_{i}^{-1}

(where
\ell_{i}
is a holomorphic local frame of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
,
\ell_{i}^{-1}
is the corresponding frame of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{-1}
, and
h_{i}:={\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}(\ell_{i},\ell_{i})
; the definition of
\lambda_{i}
is independence of the choice of
\ell_{i}
);

•

\lambda
be the anti-linear involution of
{\mathcal{E}}
which is the direct sum of
\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{n}
and the anti-linear involution of
{\mathcal{O}}
fixing
\underline{\mathbb{R}}\,
;

•

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{R}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
be the real subbundles formed by the fixed points of
\lambda
in
{\mathcal{E}}
,
{\mathcal{V}}
,
{\mathcal{W}}
, respectively.

Then the connection
D^{H}
preserves both
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, whereas
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
restricts to a real quadratic form of signature
(n,n+1)
on
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, denoted by
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, under which
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
) is a positive (resp. negative) definite subbundle of rank
n
(resp.
n+1
).
Moreover, if we choose a base point
z_{0}\in\Sigma
, let

\rho:\pi_{1}(\Sigma,z_{0})\to\mathrm{SO}_{0}({\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}},\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle)\cong\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)

be the holonomy representation of
D^{H}
and

\displaystyle f:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)}

\displaystyle\cong\big\{\text{$n$-dimensional positive definite subspaces of ${\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}$}\big\}

\displaystyle\cong\big\{\text{$(n+1)$-dimensional negative definite subspaces of ${\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}$}\big\}

be the
\rho
-equivariant map given by translating the fiber of
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{R}}
or
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
at each point into
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}
via the parallel transportation of
D^{H}
, then
f
is a conformal minimal immersion.

Outline of proof.

If we assume
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
to be stable, then every assertion can be either verified directly or deduced from the general theory of Higgs bundles
[
23
,
26
,
41
,
56
]
and cyclic Higgs bundles
[
6
,
20
]
.
For the stability statement at the beginning, the “if” part can be shown by the same argument as Lemma 4.5 and Lemma 5.11 in
[
19
]
; the “only if” part is because polystablity implies solvability of the Hitchin equation, but by the form (
5.2
) or (
5.3
) of the Hitchin equation, it is clear that if
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\equiv 0
then the Chern form
\frac{\boldsymbol{i}}{2\pi}{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log h_{n}=-\tfrac{1}{\pi}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}{\mathsf{d}x}\wedge{\mathsf{d}y}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is negative away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}
, hence
\deg({\mathcal{L}}_{n})=\int_{\Sigma}\frac{\boldsymbol{i}}{2\pi}{\bar{\partial}}\partial\log h_{n}<0
(this also shows that if
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is polystable then it is stable).
∎

When
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,\mathbb{C})=H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1})
, the Hitchin equation (
5.2
) (
5.3
) are exactly the equations satisfied by the structural data of A-surfaces (see Theorem
4.10
). In this case, we show that
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
yields an A-surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
(with
(p,q)
given by
n
as before; see (
1.3
)) in a similar way as how maximal
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(2,q+1)
-Higgs bundles yield maximal surfaces in
\mathbb{H}^{2,q}
(see
[
21
]
):

Theorem 6.3
.

In the setting of Proposition/Definition
6.2
, further assume

•

{\mathcal{L}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
;

•

({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2})\prec\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}),({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-})\big\}
(see §
5.1
).

Then there exists a unique antipodal pair
\pm F
of
\rho
-equivariant A-surface immersions of
\widetilde{\Sigma}
into
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
whose Gauss map is
f
. Moreover, after possibly swapping
\pm F
, the structural data of
F
is exactly the holomorphic data
({\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm})
used to construct
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
along with the harmonic metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
on each
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
.

Proof.

In this proof, when
n
is odd, we multiply
Q_{\mathcal{E}}
by
-1
and swap the notations of
{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}
. Under this new notation,
{\mathcal{W}}
is the direct sum of all
{\mathcal{L}}_{k}
with
k\in[-n,n]
even, including
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}={\mathcal{O}}
. The signature of
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
is exactly
(p,q+1)
, while
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathbb{R}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}
) is a positive (resp. negative) definite subbundle of rank
p
(resp.
q+1
) in
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Identifying
\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
as the hypersurface in the fiber
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}
formed by all
x\in{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}
with
\langle x,x\rangle=-1
, we let
F
be the
\rho
-equivariant map from
\widetilde{\Sigma}
to
\mathbb{H}^{p,q+1}
given by translating the section
\underline{1}
of
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
at each point into
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}
(similarly as the construction of
f
).
We shall show that
F
is an A-surface immersion with Gauss map
f
and has the required structural data. Combining with the uniqueness result in Theorem
5.10
, this implies the theorem.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{i}
denote the real subbundle of
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}_{i}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
fixed by
\lambda_{i}
. Then
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{i}
is positive (resp. negative) definite with respect to
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
exactly when
i
is odd (resp. even), and we have orthogonal splittings

{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\prime},\quad{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathbb{R}}=\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}^{\prime}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{4}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots.

Choose a holomorphic local frame
\ell_{i}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
and consider the following orthonormal local frame
(u_{i},v_{i})
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}
with respect to the positive definite metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{i}:=(-1)^{i+1}\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle|_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}}
:

u_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ell_{i}^{-1}+h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ell_{i}\big),\quad v_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(-\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\ell_{i}^{-1}+\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ell_{i}\big).

We may identify
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}
with
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
through the
\sqrt{2}
-scaled projection from
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
to
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
, and view
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}
as a hermitian holomorphic line bundle in this way.
Note that this identifies
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{i}
with the real part of the hermitian metric
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
and identifies
(u_{i},v_{i})
with the frame
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ell_{i},\boldsymbol{i}\ell_{i})
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
.
In particular,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{\prime}
gets identified with
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}={\mathcal{K}}^{-1}
, which can be viewed as the tangent bundle
\mathsf{T}\Sigma
(see the paragraph preceding Theorem
4.6
), while the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle_{1}\cong\re{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}
corresponds to the conformal Riemannian metric
{\boldsymbol{g}}=h_{1}|{\mathsf{d}z}|^{2}
on
\Sigma
.

To prove the required statement about
F
, it suffices to show that the Hitchin connection
D^{H}
(restricted to
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
) is expressed under the local frame
(\underline{1},u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
as

(6.2)

D^{H}=\mathsf{d}+\Omega,\quad\text{ where }\Omega=\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta&&&\\[2.84544pt]
\theta&\Omega_{1}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&&\\[2.84544pt]
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\Omega_{2}&\ddots&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&\hskip-5.69046pt\ddots&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-8.5359pt{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}&\hskip-2.84544pt\Omega_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}.

Here,
\theta={\vphantom{\!}}^{\mathsf{t}}\!(\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
is an orthonormal local frame of
\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma
with respect to
{\boldsymbol{g}}
,
\Omega_{i}
represents the Chern connection of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}\cong{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
, and we abuse the notation to let
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
be the matrices of
1
-forms given in the proof of Theorem
4.10
, which represent the holomorphic forms
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{\pm}
used to construct
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi
).
In fact, once (
6.2
) is established, by the parallel transportation process in the construction of
f
and
F
, we may infer that there are locally defined maps
U_{1},V_{1},\cdots,U_{n},V_{n}
from
\widetilde{\Sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}^{p,q+1}
(given by parallel transporting
u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n}
) such that the matrix valued function
P:=(F,U_{1},V_{1},\cdots,U_{n},V_{n})

satisfies
P^{-1}\mathsf{d}P=\Omega
, whereas the function
(U_{1},V_{1},U_{3},V_{3},\cdots,F,U_{2},V_{2},U_{4},V_{4})
, which takes values in
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(p,q+1)
, is a lift of
f
. But this implies that
F
has the required structural data and
f
is the Gauss map of
F
(c.f. the proof of Theorems
4.6
and
4.13
).

To show (
6.2
), we start from the expression of
D^{H}
under the frame
(\ell_{n}^{-1},\cdots,\ell_{1}^{-1},\underline{1},\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n})
of
{\mathcal{E}}
:

\displaystyle D^{H}=\nabla^{H}+\Phi+\Phi^{*}=\mathsf{d}+\Omega^{\prime},\text{ where}

\displaystyle\Omega^{\prime}=\left(\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{array}[]{ccccc|c|ccccc}-\tfrac{\partial h_{n}}{h_{n}}&\tfrac{h_{n}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{+}}}{h_{n-1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&&&&&&\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z}&\\[8.5359pt]
\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z}&-\tfrac{\partial h_{n-1}}{h_{n-1}}&\tfrac{h_{n-1}\overline{\alpha_{n-1}}}{h_{n-2}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&&&&&&\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z}\\[8.5359pt]
&\alpha_{n-1}{\mathsf{d}z}&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&\\[8.5359pt]
&&\ddots&\ddots&\tfrac{h_{2}\overline{\alpha_{2}}}{h_{1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&&&&\\[8.5359pt]
&&&\alpha_{2}{\mathsf{d}z}&-\tfrac{\partial h_{1}}{h_{1}}&\tfrac{h_{1}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{\sqrt{2}}&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&\tfrac{{\mathsf{d}z}}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\tfrac{h_{1}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{\sqrt{2}}&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&\tfrac{{\mathsf{d}z}}{\sqrt{2}}&\tfrac{\partial h_{1}}{h_{1}}&\tfrac{h_{2}\overline{\alpha_{2}}}{h_{1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&\\[8.5359pt]
&&&&&&\alpha_{2}{\mathsf{d}z}&\ddots&\ddots&&\\[8.5359pt]
&&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\tfrac{h_{n-1}\overline{\alpha_{n-1}}}{h_{n-2}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}}\,{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}&&&&&&&&\alpha_{n-1}{\mathsf{d}z}&\tfrac{\partial h_{n-1}}{h_{n-1}}&\tfrac{h_{n}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{+}}}{h_{n-1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}\\[8.5359pt]
&\tfrac{\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}}\,{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}&&&&&&&&\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z}&\tfrac{\partial h_{n}}{h_{n}}\end{array}$
}\right).

The frame for (
6.2
) is
(\underline{1},u_{1},v_{1},\cdots,u_{n},v_{n})=(\ell_{n}^{-1},\cdots,\ell_{1}^{-1},\underline{1},\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n})R
with change-of-basis matrix

R=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\scalebox{0.87}{
$\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc|cc|cc}&&&&&&&h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&&&&&&\iddots&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&&&&\iddots&&&\\
&&&h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\
&h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69046pt\sqrt{2}&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&&&&&\\
&&&h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&&&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&&&&&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&&&h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}$
}\right).

So the matrix of
D^{H}
under this frame is
R^{-1}\Omega^{\prime}R+R^{-1}\mathsf{d}R
, and we only need to show that it equals
\Omega
in (
6.2
).
This is a straightforward but lengthy matrix computation. We give below the details of some key components of the computation:

•

The
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}^{\prime}
-diagonal block of
R^{-1}\Omega^{\prime}R+R^{-1}\mathsf{d}R
is

\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}$
}\begin{pmatrix}-\tfrac{\partial h_{i}}{h_{i}}&\\
&\tfrac{\partial h_{i}}{h_{i}}\end{pmatrix}\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}$
}+\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}$
}\hskip-8.5359pt\scalebox{0.85}{
$\mathsf{d}\begin{pmatrix}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}$
}=\begin{pmatrix}&\tfrac{\mathsf{d}^{\mathrm{c}}h_{i}}{2h_{i}}\\
-\tfrac{\mathsf{d}^{\mathrm{c}}h_{i}}{2h_{i}}&\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathsf{d}^{\mathrm{c}}h:=\boldsymbol{i}(\partial-{\bar{\partial}})h=\partial_{y}h{\mathsf{d}x}-\partial_{x}h{\mathsf{d}y}
. The result is exactly the matrix expression of the Chern connection of
({\mathcal{L}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i})
under the frame
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ell_{i},\boldsymbol{i}\ell_{i})
, as required.

•

The
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
-to-
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{\prime}
block of
R^{-1}\Omega^{\prime}R+R^{-1}\mathsf{d}R
is

\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}$
}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{h_{1}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{\sqrt{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
\frac{{\mathsf{d}z}}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\sqrt{2}=h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathsf{d}x}\\
{\mathsf{d}y}\end{pmatrix}

(the term
R^{-1}\mathsf{d}R
is block-diagonal and does not contribute here; similarly below). This is indeed an orthonormal local frame of
\mathsf{T}^{*}\Sigma
under
{\boldsymbol{g}}=h_{1}({\mathsf{d}x}^{2}+{\mathsf{d}y}^{2})
, as required.

•

The
{\mathcal{L}}_{j-1}^{\prime}
-to-
{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{\prime}
block (
2\leq j\leq n-1
) and the
{\mathcal{L}}_{n-1}^{\prime}
-to-
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\prime}
block are respectively

\displaystyle\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{j}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{j}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{j}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}$
}\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{h_{j}\overline{\alpha_{j}}}{h_{j-1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&\\
&\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z}\end{pmatrix}\scalebox{0.85}{
$\begin{pmatrix}h_{j-1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{j-1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{j-1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{j-1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}$
}=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{j}}}{\sqrt{h_{j-1}}}\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{j}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$},

\begin{pmatrix}h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{n}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{h_{n}\overline{\alpha_{n}^{+}}}{h_{n-1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z}\\[5.69046pt]
\tfrac{\overline{\alpha_{n}^{-}}\,{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{h_{n-1}h_{n}}&\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z}\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}h_{n-1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{n-1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
h_{n-1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{n-1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\tfrac{\sqrt{h_{n}}}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}}}\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
\im(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})&\re(\alpha_{n}^{+}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{h_{n-1}h_{n}}}\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\re(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})\\[2.84544pt]
-\im(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})&-\re(\alpha_{n}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z})\end{pmatrix}$}.

These are exactly the matrices representing
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{j}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}+{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
under the frame
h_{i}^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\ell_{i},\boldsymbol{i}\ell_{i})
(see the proof of Theorem
4.10
), as required.

In summary, we obtain the expression (
6.2
) of
D^{H}
by computations and this completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Collier’s components

We briefly review here the construction of Collier’s components
[
19
]
, which leads to the statement of Theorem
D
, and then give the proof.

We henceforth fix integers
g\geq 2
and
n\geq 3
.
Given
k\geq 1
, the Hodge bundle
{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
is the holomorphic vector bundle over the Teichmüller space
{{\mathcal{T}}}_{g}
whose fiber
{\mathcal{H}}_{k}|_{[\Sigma]}
at any
[\Sigma]\in{{\mathcal{T}}}_{g}
is the vector space
H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}^{k})
of holomorphic
k
-differentials on the Riemann surface
\Sigma
(marked by
S_{g}
). Given
0<d\leq(2g-2)n
, we also define a fiber bundle
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
over
{{\mathcal{T}}}_{g}
in such a way that the fiber
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
is the moduli space
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}\big/\sim
, where

•

\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
is the space of triples
({\mathcal{M}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})
consisting of a holomorphic line bundle
{\mathcal{M}}
of degree
d
on
\Sigma
and holomorphic sections
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}^{n}\!\!\!{\mathcal{M}}^{-1})\setminus\{0\}
,
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}^{n}\!\!\!{\mathcal{M}})
;

•

the equivalence relation
({\mathcal{M}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\sim({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime})
means that there are line bundle isomorphisms
\phi_{0}:{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+1}\to{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+1}
and
\phi_{1}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
which, along with the isomorphism
\phi_{-1}:{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}{}^{-1}
induced by
\phi_{1}
, fit into a commutative diagram

Here, an horizontal arrow such as
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\overset{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}{\to}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
means that
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
is a
\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
-valued holomorphic
1
-form, or equivalently a holomorphic section of
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
.

Remark 6.4
.

The
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
here is slightly different from the one in
[
19
]
4
4

4

Note that everything in
[
19
]
is based on a fix Riemann surface
\Sigma
, so the notation “
\,{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\,|_{[\Sigma]}
” is not included therein.
in that our
{\mathcal{M}}
is a concrete line bundle rather than a point in
\mathrm{Pic}^{d}(\Sigma)
, but the resulting quotient
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
is the same. As noticed in
[
19
]
,
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
identifies with the total space of a rank
d+(2n-1)(g-1)
holomorphic vector bundle over the symmetric product
\mathrm{Sym}^{(2g-2)n-d}\Sigma
.

The direct sum fiber bundle
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{4}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
over
{{\mathcal{T}}}_{g}
can be viewed as a data space whose each point determines an equivalence class of stable
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles.
More specifically,
from
\big({\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2n-2},({\mathcal{M}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\big)\in\big({\mathcal{H}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{4}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2n-2}\oplus\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}\big)|_{[\Sigma]}
, we get the following
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundle
({\mathcal{V}},Q_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{W}},Q_{\mathcal{W}},\eta)
on
\Sigma
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{K}}^{n-1}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{n-3}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+3}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+1},\quad Q_{\mathcal{V}}=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\[-2.84544pt]
&\iddots&\\[-2.84544pt]
1&&\end{pmatrix}$},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}={\mathcal{M}}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{n-4}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+4}\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+2}\oplus{\mathcal{M}}^{-1},\quad Q_{\mathcal{W}}=\scalebox{0.8}{$\begin{pmatrix}&&1\\[-2.84544pt]
&\iddots&\\[-2.84544pt]
1&&\end{pmatrix}$},

\displaystyle\eta=\scalebox{0.85}{$\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\mu}}&{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2}&{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{4}&\cdots&\hskip-4.26773pt{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2n-2}&{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\\
&1&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots&\\[-2.84544pt]
&&\ddots&\ddots&{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{4}&\\
&&&1&{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2}&\\
&&&&\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\end{pmatrix}$}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}({\mathcal{W}},{\mathcal{V}}));

while replacing
({\mathcal{M}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})
by an equivalent triple
({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\prime})
yields an equivalent Higgs bundle (the
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
here can be replaced by
1
as mentioned in the introduction).

Let
{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(S_{g}),\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1))/\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
be the
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-character variety. Combining the above construction with the non-abelian Hodge correspondence,
which assigns to every equivalence class of stable
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
-Higgs bundles a point of
{\mathcal{X}}
,
we obtain a map

\Psi:{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{4}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{d}\to{\mathcal{X}}.

For any fixed Riemann surface
\Sigma
, Collier
[
19
]
studied the restriction of
\Psi
to the fiber of the domain bundle at
[\Sigma]
and found that it parametrizes certain remarkable components of
{\mathcal{X}}
:

Theorem 6.5

(
[
19
]
)
.

Given
d\in(0,(2g-2)n]
and a Riemann surface
\Sigma
, the map
\Psi
restricts to a diffeomorphism from
\big({\mathcal{H}}_{2}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{4}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{2n-2}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{d}\big)|_{[\Sigma]}
to a connected component
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
of
{\mathcal{X}}
.

On the other hand, the motivation behind Labourie’s conjecture is to give certain components of character varieties
mapping-class-group-equivariant
parametrizations. For the component
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
from Theorem
6.5
, the major candidate for such a parametrization is the restriction of
\Psi
to the domain fiber bundle with the summand
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
removed. However, although the existing techniques
[
47
,
9
,
37
]
imply that this restriction is surjective, the injectivity is a difficult problem, especially in view of the new twist
[
52
,
53
]
in the study of Labourie’s conjecture for hermitian-type Lie groups. Our goal here is to provide the following partial result which extends Labourie’s work
[
48
]
for Hitchin components. The proof is closely modeled on the argument in
[
48
, §7.3.4]
, only with the infinitesimal rigidity of cyclic surfaces replaced by that of A-surfaces.

Theorem 6.6
.

Given
d\in(0,(2g-2)n]
,
\Psi
restricts to an immersion from the fiber bundle
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
to
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
.

Proof.

Fix
q=([\Sigma],{\mathcal{M}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}
and consider a smooth variation
q_{t}=([\Sigma_{t}],{\mathcal{M}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t})
with
q_{0}=q
,
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
.
Let
({\mathcal{E}}_{t},\Phi_{t})
be the Higgs bundle constructed from
q_{t}
as above, with
{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\phi}}_{2n-2}=0
. Then
({\mathcal{E}}_{t},\Phi_{t})
is the special case of the Higgs bundles studied in §
6.1
with
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}^{-i}
for
i\leq n-1
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}={\mathcal{M}}^{-1}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}=\cdots={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1}=1
,
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}
and
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}={\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t}
. By Prop./Def.
6.2
and Theorem
6.3
, we get from
({\mathcal{E}}_{t},\Phi_{t})
a holonomy representation
\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(S_{g})=\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{t})\to\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
and a
\rho_{t}
-equivariant A-surface immersion
F_{t}:\widetilde{\Sigma}_{t}\to\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
, while the map
\Psi
is given by
\Psi([q_{t}])=[\rho_{t}]\in{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
. To prove the theorem, we assume that the path
([\rho_{t}])_{t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}
in
{\mathcal{X}}_{d}
is stationary at
t=0
, and only need to show that the path
([q_{t}])
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}
is stationary at
t=0
as well.

After conjugating each
\rho_{t}
by some element of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}(n,n+1)
, we may assume that
\rho_{t}
itself (rather than the conjugacy class
[\rho_{t}]
) is stationary, namely
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{\rho}_{0}=0
. As a consequence, taking the derivative in
t
of the
\rho_{t}
-equivariance relation

F_{t}(\gamma.z)=\rho_{t}(\gamma).F_{t}(z)
(
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{t})
,
z\in\widetilde{\Sigma}
), we obtain, at
t=0
,

\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(\gamma.z)=\rho_{0}(\gamma).\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}(z)\ \ \text{ for all }\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\Sigma),\ z\in\widetilde{\Sigma}.

Namely,
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is
\rho_{0}(\Sigma)
-invariant. In particular, the normal component
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}
of
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
, which can be viewed as a Jacobi field on the A-surface
F_{0}(\widetilde{\Sigma})\subset\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
(see §
3.3
), descents to a Jacobi field on the closed A-surface
F_{0}(\widetilde{\Sigma})/\rho_{0}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma))
in the quotient
M:=\mathbb{H}^{p,q}/\rho_{0}(\pi_{1}(\Sigma))
. Therefore, we may apply Corollary
5.9
to conclude that
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}^{\mathcal{N}}\equiv 0
, or in other words,
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
is tangent to
F
.

Since the first fundamental form of the immersion
F_{t}
is conformal to the Riemann surface structure
\Sigma_{t}
, by the first variation formula of first fundamental form (see e.g.
[
57
]
), the variation of this Riemann surface structure at
t=0
is given by its Lie derivative with respect to the tangent vector field of
\Sigma
corresponding to
\accentset{\,\mbox{\large.}}{F}_{0}
. It follows that the path
([\Sigma_{t}])
in
{{\mathcal{T}}}_{g}
is stationary at
t=0
.
Therefore, we may assume from the beginning that
\Sigma_{t}
is the fixed Riemann surface
\Sigma
and only the triple
({\mathcal{M}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t})
on
\Sigma
vary.
By Lemma
3.3
, the connection
\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}_{t}}
on the normal bundle
{\mathcal{N}}_{t}\subset F_{t}^{*}\mathsf{T}\mathbb{H}^{p,q}
is stationary at
t=0
. But on the other hand, by definition of A-surfaces,
({\mathcal{N}}_{t},\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}_{t}})
is isomorphic to
{\mathcal{K}}^{-2}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-n+1}\oplus{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}_{t}
(as a real vector bundle) endowed with the connection

\nabla=\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{2}&1^{*}&&&\\[2.84544pt]
1&\nabla_{3}&\ddots&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&1^{*}&\\[5.69046pt]
&&1&\nabla_{n-1}&\hskip-2.84544pt{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}^{*}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t}^{*}\\[2.84544pt]
&&&\hskip-5.69046pt{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t}&\hskip-11.38092pt\nabla_{n}\end{pmatrix}$}

(where each
1\in\mathbb{C}\cong H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}\otimes\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}({\mathcal{K}}^{-j+1},{\mathcal{K}}^{-j}))
is viewed as a
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathcal{K}}^{-j+1},{\mathcal{K}}^{-j})
-valued
1
-form). In particular, the equivalence class of the holomorphic chain

is determined by the connection
\nabla^{{\mathcal{N}}_{t}}
. Therefore, we conclude that this equivalence class is stationary at
t=0
. By definition of the equivalence relation in
\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}
, this means that the point in
{\mathcal{F}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}=\widetilde{{\mathcal{F}}}_{d}|_{[\Sigma]}/\sim
represented by
({\mathcal{M}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{\nu}}_{t})
is stationary at
t=0
, as required.
∎

6.3.
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin component

We now turn to the special case of Def.
6.1
and Prop./Def.
6.2
with

(6.14)

n=3,\quad{\mathcal{L}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}^{-i}\ (i=1,2,3),\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}},\quad{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}={\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}^{+}=1.

The
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
’s in this case are parametrized by
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}\in H^{0}(\Sigma,{\mathcal{K}}^{6})
and are exactly the Higgs bundles in the
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Hitchin component constructed from the principal
3
-dimensional subalgebra
\mathfrak{s}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
in §
2.7
. By using the description in Remark
2.11
of the
\mathrm{SO}(4)
-module
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\prime}
, one may check that
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
is indeed a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-Higgs bundles, so the Hitchin connection
D^{H}
on
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{K}}^{3}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-3}
is a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-connection. We omit the details here, as it involves the lengthy definition of
G
-Higgs bundles for a general
G
. Alternatively, we now use Proposition
2.16
to identify
D^{H}
as a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-connection in a direct way.

Recall from Proposition
2.15
that
\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{\mathbb{C}}
can be identified as the Lie algebra of matrices infinitesimally preserving the
3
-form
\varpi:=\boldsymbol{i}\big(\varepsilon^{147}+\varepsilon^{246}-\varepsilon^{345}-\scalebox{0.8}{$2\sqrt{2}$}\,\varepsilon^{156}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\varepsilon^{237}\big)\in\bigwedge^{3}\mathbb{C}^{7*}
. But each term
\varepsilon^{ijk}
in
\varpi
(note that
i+j+k=12
) can be viewed as a holomorphic section of
\bigwedge^{3}{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
through

\varepsilon^{ijk}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}):=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{3}}\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\ \xi_{\sigma(1)}^{(i)}\otimes\xi_{\sigma(2)}^{(j)}\otimes\xi_{\sigma(3)}^{(k)}

for any holomorphic local sections
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}
of
{\mathcal{E}}
,
where
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
is the permutation group of the indices
\{1,2,3\}
and the superscript “
(i)
” means taking the
i
th component in
{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{K}}^{3}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{K}}^{-3}
, namely the
{\mathcal{K}}^{4-i}
-component; the tensor product yields a holomorphic section of
{\mathcal{K}}^{4-i}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}^{4-j}\otimes{\mathcal{K}}^{4-k}={\mathcal{O}}
, which is just a holomorphic function. Therefore, we may consider
\varpi
as a holomorphic section of
\bigwedge^{3}{\mathcal{E}}^{*}
as well. It allows us to deduce from Proposition
2.16
and Prop./Def.
6.2
:

Corollary 6.7
.

In the case (
6.14
), the following statements about the objects in Prop./Def.
6.2
hold:

•

the harmonic metric
H={\boldsymbol{h}}_{3}^{-1}\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}^{-1}\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}^{-1}\oplus 1\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}\oplus{\boldsymbol{h}}_{3}
satisfies

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{3}={\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{h}}_{2}/4
;

•

the Hitchin connection
D^{H}
preserves
\varpi\in H^{0}(\Sigma,\bigwedge^{3}{\mathcal{E}}^{*})
;

•

\varpi
restricts to a real
3
-form on each fiber of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
which, together with the metric
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
, makes the fiber isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
endowed with the
3
-form
\varphi
associated to the cross product
\times
(see §
2.3
).

As a consequence, the representation
\rho
takes values in the subgroup
G_{2}^{\prime}
of
\mathrm{SO}_{0}({\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}},\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle)\cong\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)
formed by linear transformations of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}|_{z_{0}}
preserving
\langle{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,},{\,\raisebox{0.8pt}{\scalebox{0.65}{$\bullet$}}\,}\rangle
and the
3
-form, whereas the minimal immersion
f
takes values in the corresponding submanifold
X_{G_{2}^{\prime}}
of
X_{\mathrm{SO}_{0}(3,4)}
.

Proof.

It is a general fact for Higgs bundles in the Hitchin component (see
[
42
,
46
]
) that the Cartan involution
\rho
(induced by the harmonic metric) on each fiber of the underlying Lie algebra bundle commutes with the holomorphic involution
\sigma
(induced by the principal
3
-dimensional subalgebra), and in the cyclic case
\rho
furthermore preserves
\mathfrak{h}
. Therefore, the statement on
H
follows from Proposition
2.16
. For the statement on
D^{H}
, we note that in the current setting, the expression (
6.1
) of
D^{H}
under the local frame
(\ell_{n}^{-1},\cdots,\ell_{1}^{-1},\underline{1},\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n})
of
{\mathcal{E}}
(here
n=3
and we may take
\ell_{i}={\mathsf{d}z}^{-i}
) becomes

(6.15)

D^{H}=\mathsf{d}+\left(\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{array}[]{ccc|c|ccc}-\tfrac{\partial h_{3}}{h_{3}}&\tfrac{h_{3}}{h_{2}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&&\alpha_{3}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z}&\\[8.5359pt]
{\mathsf{d}z}&-\tfrac{\partial h_{2}}{h_{2}}&\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&&&&\alpha_{3}^{-}{\mathsf{d}z}\\[8.5359pt]
&{\mathsf{d}z}&-\tfrac{\partial h_{1}}{h_{1}}&\tfrac{h_{1}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{\sqrt{2}}&&&\\[8.5359pt]
\hline\cr&&\tfrac{{\mathsf{d}z}}{\sqrt{2}}&0&\tfrac{h_{1}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{\sqrt{2}}&&\\
\hline\cr&&&\tfrac{{\mathsf{d}z}}{\sqrt{2}}&\tfrac{\partial h_{1}}{h_{1}}&\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}&\\[8.5359pt]
\tfrac{\overline{\alpha_{3}^{-}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{h_{2}h_{3}}&&&&{\mathsf{d}z}&\tfrac{\partial h_{2}}{h_{2}}&\tfrac{h_{3}}{h_{2}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}\\[8.5359pt]
&\tfrac{\overline{\alpha_{3}^{-}}{\mathsf{d}\bar{z}}}{h_{2}h_{3}}&&&&{\mathsf{d}z}&\tfrac{\partial h_{3}}{h_{3}}\end{array}$
}\right),\text{ where }h_{3}=\tfrac{h_{1}h_{2}}{4}.

This matrix has the form (
2.12
) in Proposition
2.15
, hence
D^{H}
preserves
\varpi
. The statement on the restriction of
\varpi
to
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
is again contained in Proposition
2.16
. These statements imply that
D^{H}
is a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-connection on
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
, whence the statement on
\rho
and
f
follows.
∎

The A-surfaces underlying
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
belong to the subclass discussed in Proposition
4.15
:

Proposition 6.8
.

In the above setting, the A-surface immersion
F:\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
given in Theorem
6.3
is
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic. As a consequence,
-F
is
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-anti-holomorphic and the images of
\pm F
are

{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic curve with nowhere vanishing second fundamental form and timelike osculation lines.

Proof.

Consider the holomorphic local frame
(\ell_{3}^{-1},\ell_{2}^{-1},\ell_{1}^{-1},\underline{1},\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})=({\mathsf{d}z}^{3},{\mathsf{d}z}^{2},\cdots,{\mathsf{d}z}^{-3})
of
{\mathcal{E}}
. In the proof of Theorem
6.3
, we have shown that under the frame
(\underline{1},u_{1},v_{1},u_{2},v_{2},u_{3},v_{3})=(\ell_{3}^{-1},\cdots,\ell_{3})R
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
given by

R=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\scalebox{0.9}{$\begin{array}[]{c|cc|cc|cc}&&&&&h_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.69046pt]
&&&h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&\\[5.69046pt]
&h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&-\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\
\hline\cr\hskip-5.69046pt\scalebox{0.9}{$\sqrt{2}$}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&&&\\[5.69046pt]
&&&h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{2}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&&\\[5.69046pt]
&&&&&h_{3}^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\boldsymbol{i}h_{3}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}$}\right)

(note that
h_{3}=\frac{h_{1}h_{2}}{4}
by Corollary
6.7
), the Hitchin connection is represented by the matrix

\Omega=\scalebox{0.9}{
$\begin{pmatrix}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!\theta&&\\[2.84544pt]
\theta&\Omega_{1}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\\[2.84544pt]
&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}&\Omega_{2}&{\vphantom{\!}}{}^{\mathsf{t}}\!{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}\\[2.84544pt]
&&{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{3}&\hskip-2.84544pt\Omega_{n}\end{pmatrix}$};

and as a consequence, there are maps
U_{i},V_{i}
(
i=1,2,3
) from
\widetilde{\Sigma}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3,4}
such that the matrix-valued map
P:=(F,U_{1},V_{1},U_{2},V_{2},U_{3},V_{3})
satisfies
P^{-1}\mathsf{d}P=\Omega
. In particular,
\mathsf{d}F_{z}:\mathsf{T}_{z}\widetilde{\Sigma}\to\mathsf{T}_{F(z)}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}\cong F(z)^{\perp}
sends the unit tangent vectors
h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{x},h_{1}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{y}\in\mathsf{T}_{z}\widetilde{\Sigma}
to
U_{1}
and
V_{1}
, respectively.

On the other hand, by the proof of Proposition
2.16
, the modified frame
(\underline{1},u_{2},v_{2},u_{1},v_{1},-v_{3},u_{3})
(which equals
(\ell_{3}^{-1}\,\cdots,\ell_{3})B
with
B
from that proof) is a
G_{2}^{\prime}
-frame of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\mathbb{R}}
in the sense of Definition
2.3
, so the matrix
(F,U_{2},V_{2},U_{1},V_{1},-V_{3},U_{3})
is in
G_{2}^{\prime}
. Thus, if we re-denote the column vectors of this matrix by
e_{1},\cdots,e_{7}
successively, then their cross products are given by Table
2
. In particular, we have
F\times U_{1}=e_{1}\times e_{4}=e_{5}=V_{1}
. By definition of the almost complex structure
{\boldsymbol{J}}
(see §
2.6
), this means that
{\boldsymbol{J}}
sends
U_{1}(z)\in F(z)^{\perp}=\mathsf{T}_{F(z)}\mathbb{H}^{4,2}
to
V_{1}(z)
for all
z\in\widetilde{\Sigma}
. Combining with the above description of
\mathsf{d}F_{z}
, we conclude that
\mathsf{d}F_{z}\circ{\boldsymbol{j}}={\boldsymbol{J}}\circ\mathsf{d}F_{z}
(where
{\boldsymbol{j}}
denotes the complex structure on
\widetilde{\Sigma}
), namely
F
is
{\boldsymbol{J}}
-holomorphic.
∎

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
5.2

The proof here is adapted from
[
24
, §5]
. In order to make it easier for the reader to compare with the original work, we restate the theorem as follows, where the assumption from Remark
5.4
has been added and the notation has been reset (see the remark below) so as to be consistent with
[
24
]
.

Theorem
.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed Riemann surface of genus
\geq 2
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{1},\cdots,{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
(
n\geq 2
) be holomorphic line bundles on
\Sigma
and
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i}
be a hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
(
i=1,\cdots,n
). Let
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j}
(
j=1,\cdots,n-1
),
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

be holomorphic sections of the line bundles
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{j+1}
,
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
, respectively, such that none of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}
is identically zero and the following equations hold on
\Sigma
:

(A.1)

\displaystyle\text{if $n=2$: }\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\gamma_{1}|^{2}+h_{1}h_{2}|\beta|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}=\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\gamma_{1}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{2}}|\gamma_{2}|^{2}+h_{1}h_{2}|\beta|^{2};\end{cases}

(A.2)

\displaystyle\text{if $n\geq 3$: }\begin{cases}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=-\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\gamma_{1}|^{2}+h_{1}h_{2}|\beta|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}=\tfrac{h_{2}}{h_{1}}|\gamma_{1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{3}}{h_{2}}|\gamma_{2}|^{2}+h_{1}h_{2}|\beta|^{2},\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\tfrac{h_{k}}{h_{k-1}}|\gamma_{k-1}|^{2}-\tfrac{h_{k+1}}{h_{k}}|\gamma_{k}|^{2}\quad(3\leq k\leq n-1),\\[5.69046pt]
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{n}=\tfrac{h_{n}}{h_{n-1}}|\gamma_{n-1}|^{2}-\tfrac{1}{h_{n}}|\gamma_{n}|^{2}.\end{cases}

Then the following statements hold.

(1)

If
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})\prec({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\neq\emptyset
, then
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}\leq 0
on
\Sigma
, with strict inequality away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}
.

(2)

If
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n})\prec\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}),({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\big\}
, then

\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{i}\leq 0
on
\Sigma
for
i=2,\cdots,n
, with strict inequality away from the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
.

Remark
.

The hermitian line bundle
({\mathcal{L}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{i})
here corresponds to
({\mathcal{L}}_{n+1-i}^{-1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{n+1-i}^{-1})
in Theorem
5.2
. In particular,
\log h_{1},\log h_{2},\cdots
correspond to
-\log h_{n},-\log h_{n-1},\cdots
therein, respectively. Meanwhile,
