{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
correspond respectively to
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n-1},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{+},{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{n}^{-}
. In fact, while the equations in Theorem
5.2
are the Hitchin equation for the Higgs bundle
({\mathcal{E}},\Phi)
exhibited in §
6.1
, the above (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) are the Hitchin equation for the Higgs bundle

\displaystyle\hskip 2.84544pt{\mathcal{E}}={\mathcal{L}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{2}\cdots\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\oplus{\mathcal{O}}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}\oplus\cdots{\mathcal{L}}_{2}^{-1}\oplus{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1},

\displaystyle\Phi=\scalebox{0.83}{$\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|c|cccc}0&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\beta}}&\\
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}&0&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&\\
&&\hskip-5.69046pt{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n-1}&0&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}&0&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}&0&&&\\
&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n-1}&\ddots&&\\
&&&&&&\ddots&0&\\
&&&&&&&{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}&0\end{array}\right)$}\penalty\ .

The original result in
[
24
]
deals with case
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}^{n+1-i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}=\cdots={\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}=1
.

Proof.

When
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv 0
,
(1)
is trivial and
(2)
can be proved by a simple adaptation of the argument below for
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\not\equiv 0
. So let us assume
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\not\equiv 0
from now on and only outline the adaption for
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv 0
at the end.

Pick a background conformal metric
{\boldsymbol{g}}=h_{0}|{\mathsf{d}z}|^{2}
on
\Sigma
. After being divided by
h_{0}
, each term in (
A.2
) is a globally defined function on
\Sigma
. The terms on the right-hand side are

\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{j}\|^{2}=\tfrac{h_{j+1}}{h_{0}h_{j}}|\gamma_{j}|^{2}\quad(j=1,\cdots,n-1),\quad\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n}\|^{2}=\tfrac{1}{h_{0}h_{n}}|\gamma_{n}|^{2},\quad\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}=\tfrac{h_{1}h_{2}|\beta|^{2}}{h_{0}}.

In what follows, we extend the definition of the real logarithm and exponential functions by putting
\log 0:=-\infty
and
e^{-\infty}:=0
. Consider the functions
u_{0},\cdots,u_{n}:\Sigma\to\{-\infty\}\cup\mathbb{R}
given by

u_{0}:=\log\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2},\quad u_{i}:=\log\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\|^{2}\quad(i=1,\cdots,n).

Note that
u_{0}
(resp.
u_{i}
) is smooth away from the discrete set
\{u_{0}=-\infty\}
(resp.
\{u_{i}=-\infty\}
), which is just the zero locus of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(resp.
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
).

Let
\Delta=\frac{4}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}
be the Laplacian associated to the metric
{\boldsymbol{g}}
.
Since
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log|\phi|^{2}=0
for any holomorphic function
\phi(z)
, by (
A.1
) and the first two equations in (
A.2
), away from
\{u_{0}=-\infty\}
, we have

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\big(\log|\beta|^{2}+\log h_{1}+\log h_{2}-\log h_{0}\big)=\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}+\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle=\big(-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}\big)+\big(\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}+\|\beta\|^{2}\big)+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle=2\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}=2e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}

where
\kappa=-\frac{2}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{0}
is the curvature of
{\boldsymbol{g}}
. By similar computations, we obtain

\text{if $n=3$: }\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{0}=2e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{1}=2e^{u_{1}}-e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2};\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{2}=-e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}}+e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2};\\
\end{cases}\ \text{if $n\geq 3$: }\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{0}=2e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{1}=2e^{u_{1}}-e^{u_{2}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{2}=-e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}}+2e^{u_{2}}-e^{u_{3}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\penalty\ ,\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{k}=-e^{u_{k-1}}+2e^{u_{k}}-e^{u_{k+1}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}\quad(3\leq k\leq n-1),\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta u_{n}=-e^{u_{n-1}}+e^{u_{n}}+\tfrac{\kappa}{2}.\end{cases}

Taking differences, we get equations for
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{i}-u_{i+1})
away from
\{u_{i}=-\infty\}\cup\{u_{i+1}=-\infty\}
:

(A.3)

if
n=2
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})=2e^{u_{0}}-2e^{u_{1}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})=e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-2e^{u_{2}};\end{cases}\text{if $n=3$: }\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})=2e^{u_{0}}-2e^{u_{1}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})=e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{2}-u_{3})=-e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}}+3e^{u_{2}}-2e^{u_{3}};\end{cases}

(A.4)

\displaystyle\text{if $n\geq 4$: }\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})=2e^{u_{0}}-2e^{u_{1}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})=e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{2}-u_{3})=-e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}}+3e^{u_{2}}-3e^{u_{3}}+e^{u_{4}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{k-1}-u_{k})=-e^{u_{k-2}}+3e^{u_{k-1}}-3e^{u_{k}}+e^{u_{k+1}}\quad(4\leq k\leq n-1),\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{n-1}-u_{n})=-e^{u_{n-2}}+3e^{u_{n-1}}-2e^{u_{n}}.\end{cases}

We are now ready to carry out the proof of statements
(1)
and
(2)
.

(1)

The assumption
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})\prec({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
implies that
e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}/\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}
attains its maximum
A_{1}>0
on a set
P_{1}\subset\Sigma
not containing the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(note that the condition
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})\leq({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
is sufficient for
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}/\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}
to attain maximum, but in this case a maximal point might be a common zero of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
with the same multiplicity). Since a maximal point of
e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}
is also a maximal point of
u_{0}-u_{1}
, by the first equation in (
A.3
) or (
A.4
) and Maximum Principle, we obtain the following inequality, where every function is evaluated at a given
p\in P_{1}
:

\text{at $p\in P_{1}$:}\quad 0\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})=2e^{u_{0}}-2e^{u_{1}}=2e^{u_{1}}(e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}-1)=2e^{u_{1}}(A_{1}-1).

It follows that
A_{1}\leq 1
because
e^{u_{1}}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}>0
at
p
.

We now improve this to the strict inequality
A_{1}<1
by using E. Hopf’s Strong Maximum Principle (see e.g.
[
32
, Theorem 3.5]
), which says that any function
u
on a disk in
\mathbb{C}
satisfying
\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}u\geq cu
for a constant
c>0
cannot achieve nonnegative maximum unless it is constant. Suppose by contradiction that
A_{1}=1
. Given
p\in P_{1}
, on any disk
U
centered at
p
and avoiding the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, we have

\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}(u_{0}-u_{1})=\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})=2e^{u_{1}}(e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}-1)\geq e^{u_{1}}(u_{0}-u_{1}).

Since the maximum
\log A_{1}=0
of
u_{0}-u_{1}
on
U
is achieved at
p
, we can use Strong Maximum Principle to infer that
u_{0}-u_{1}
is identically
0
on
U
. Therefore, the nonempty closed set
P_{1}
is also open, hence is the whole
\Sigma
. This is impossible because
P_{1}
does not intersect the zero locus of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
, which is nonempty by assumption. Thus, we have shown
A_{1}<1
. The required statement follows:

\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{1}=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}=0\}$},\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}\big(\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}}-1\big)\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}(A_{1}-1)<0&\text{ on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\neq 0\}$}.\end{cases}

(2)
Assuming
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n})\prec\cdots\prec({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}),({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\big\}
, we first observe that the divisors
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})
and
({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
cannot be both empty: otherwise we have
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})=\cdots=({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n})=\emptyset
as well, and hence the holomorphic line bundles
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{j}^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}_{j+1}
,
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{-1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
are all trivial; but the triviality of the first two implies that
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}={\mathcal{K}}^{n+1-i}
, so
{\mathcal{K}}\!{\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\mathcal{L}}_{2}={\mathcal{K}}^{2n}
is not trivial (as
\Sigma
has genus
\geq 2
), a contradiction. Therefore, after possibly replacing
({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1})
by
({\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{-1},{\boldsymbol{h}}_{1}^{-1})
and interchanging
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}
with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(which does not affect the equations), we may assume that
({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\neq\emptyset
.

We also infer from the assumption that
e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}=\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}}
attains its maximum
A_{2}>0
on a set
P_{2}\subset\Sigma
not containing the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}
, while
e^{u_{k-1}-u_{k}}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k-1}\|^{2}/\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\|^{2}
(
3\leq k\leq n
) attains its maximum
A_{k}>0
on
P_{k}\subset\Sigma
not containing the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}
. We proceed in the following steps:

Step 1.
Use Maximum Principle to produce a set of algebraic inequalities on
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}
.

First consider the case
n\geq 4
. Using (
A.4
) and Maximum Principle, we get

\displaystyle\text{at $p\in P_{3}$:}\quad 0

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-u_{3}}\Delta(u_{2}-u_{3})

\displaystyle=e^{-u_{3}}(-e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}}+3e^{u_{2}}-3e^{u_{3}}+e^{u_{4}})

\displaystyle=-e^{u_{2}-u_{3}}(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-1)+2(e^{u_{2}-u_{3}}-1)-(1-e^{u_{4}-u_{3}})

\displaystyle\geq-A_{3}(A_{2}-1)+2(A_{3}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{4}}\big),

where the multiplier
e^{-u_{3}}
in the first line is allowed because
P_{3}
does not contain the zeros of
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{3}
. Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\text{at $p\in P_{k}$ ($4\leq k\leq n-1$):}\quad 0

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-u_{k}}\Delta(u_{k-1}-u_{k})

\displaystyle=e^{-u_{k}}(-e^{u_{k-2}}+3e^{u_{k-1}}-3e^{u_{k}}+e^{u_{k+1}})

\displaystyle=-e^{u_{k-1}-u_{k}}(e^{u_{k-2}-u_{k-1}}-1)+2(e^{u_{k-1}-u_{k}}-1)-(1-e^{u_{k+1}-u_{k}})

\displaystyle\geq-A_{k}(A_{k-1}-1)+2(A_{k}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{k+1}}\big);

\displaystyle\text{at $p\in P_{n}$:}\quad 0

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-u_{n}}\Delta(u_{n-1}-u_{n})

\displaystyle=e^{-u_{n}}(e^{-u_{n-2}}+3e^{u_{n-1}}-2e^{u_{n}})

\displaystyle=-e^{u_{n-1}-u_{n}}(e^{u_{n-2}-u_{n-1}}-1)+2(e^{u_{n-1}-u_{n}}-1)

\displaystyle\geq-A_{n}(A_{n-1}-1)+2(A_{n}-1).

As for
P_{2}
, we use the inequality

\Delta\log(e^{f}+1)=\tfrac{e^{f}}{e^{f}+1}\Delta f+\tfrac{e^{f}}{(e^{f}+1)^{2}}\|\nabla f\|^{2}\geq\tfrac{e^{f}}{e^{f}+1}\Delta f
, which holds for any
f\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma)
, to get

(A.5)

\displaystyle\text{at $p\in P_{2}$:}\quad 0

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta\log\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta\log\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}+1\big)+\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}}{e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}+1}\cdot\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{0}-u_{1})+\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}}{e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}+1}\cdot 2(e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}})+(e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-u_{1}}(e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}})^{2}}{e^{u_{0}-u_{1}}+1}+(e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}})+(e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}})

\displaystyle\geq(e^{u_{0}}-e^{u_{1}})+(e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}})

\displaystyle=e^{u_{2}}\left[2\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-1\big)-\big(1-e^{u_{3}-u_{2}}\big)\right]

\displaystyle\geq e^{u_{2}}\left[2(A_{2}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}\big)\right].

Thus, we have obtained

(A.6)

\text{ if $n\geq 4$: }\begin{cases}2(A_{2}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}\big)\leq 0,\\
-A_{3}(A_{2}-1)+2(A_{3}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{4}}\big)\leq 0,\\
-A_{k}(A_{k-1}-1)+2(A_{k}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{k+1}}\big)\leq 0\quad(4\leq k\leq n-1),\\
-A_{n}(A_{n-1}-1)+2(A_{n}-1)\leq 0.\end{cases}

When
n=2,3
, the same argument yields

(A.7)

\displaystyle\text{ if $n=2$: }A_{2}-1\leq 0;

(A.8)

\displaystyle\text{ if $n=3$: }\begin{cases}2(A_{2}-1)-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}\big)\leq 0,\\
-A_{3}(A_{2}-1)+2(A_{3}-1)\leq 0.\end{cases}

Step 2.
Infer from these inequalities that
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}
are either all equal to
1
or all less than
1
.

If
n=2
, there is nothing to prove. If
n=3
, we can rewrite (
A.8
) as

4\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}\big)\leq 2(A_{2}-1)\leq 1-\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}
, whence
the required conclusion follows easily. Now assume that
n\geq 4
and
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}
are not all
1
. Put
B_{k}:=\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{k}}\big)-(A_{k-1}-1)
for
3\leq k\leq n
. Then (
A.6
) can be rewritten as

(A.9)

\begin{cases}A_{2}-1\leq B_{3},\\
A_{3}B_{3}\leq B_{4},\\
A_{k}B_{k}\leq B_{k+1}\quad(4\leq k\leq n-1),\\
A_{n}B_{n}\leq-(A_{n}-1).\end{cases}

Let us first show
A_{n}\leq 1
. Applying the definition of the
B_{k}
’s repeatedly, we get

\displaystyle 1-\tfrac{1}{A_{n}}

\displaystyle=B_{n}+A_{n-1}-1=B_{n}+A_{n-1}\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{n-1}}\big)

\displaystyle=B_{n}+A_{n-1}(B_{n-1}+A_{n-2}-1)=B_{n}+A_{n-1}B_{n-1}+A_{n-1}A_{n-2}\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{n-2}}\big)

\displaystyle=B_{n}+A_{n-1}B_{n-1}+A_{n-1}A_{n-2}(B_{n-2}+A_{n-3}-1)=\cdots

\displaystyle=B_{n}+A_{n-1}B_{n-1}+A_{n-1}A_{n-2}B_{n-2}+\ \cdots\ +A_{n-1}\cdots A_{3}B_{3}+A_{n-1}\cdots A_{3}(A_{2}-1)

The result has
n-1
terms and each term is no larger than
B_{n}
by (
A.9
). Therefore, we have

1-\tfrac{1}{A_{n}}\leq(n-1)B_{n}\leq-(n-1)\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{n}}\big),

which implies
A_{n}\leq 1
, as required.

Next, we show
A_{2}<1
. Suppose by contradiction that
A_{2}\geq 1
. Then the inequalities in (
A.9
) imply, successively for
3\leq k\leq n
, that
B_{k}\geq 0
; or equivalently,
1-\tfrac{1}{A_{k}}\geq A_{k-1}-1
. This in turn implies successively that
A_{k}\geq 1
for
3\leq k\leq n
. Since we already know that
A_{n}\leq 1
, it follows that
A_{n}=1
. This allows us to deduce again from (
A.9
) that
B_{k}\leq 0
for
3\leq k\leq n
(in reversed order). Therefore, we have
B_{3}=\cdots=B_{n}=0
, which implies
A_{2}=\cdots=A_{n}=1
, a contradiction.

Finally, we show that
A_{3},\cdots,A_{n}<0
in a similar way as follows. Suppose by contradiction that
A_{l-1}<1
but
A_{l}\geq 1
for some
3\leq l\leq n
. Then we have
B_{l}:=\big(1-\tfrac{1}{A_{l}}\big)-(A_{l-1}-1)>0
. Similarly as in the above proof of
A_{2}<1
, we infer that
B_{k}>0
and hence
A_{k}\geq 1
for
l\leq k\leq n
. So again we have
A_{n}=1
, which implies
B_{n}\leq 0
, a contradiction.

Step 3.
Exclude the case
A_{2}=\cdots=A_{n}=1
by using Stronger Maximum Principle.

Suppose
A_{2}=\cdots=A_{n}=1
by contradiction. Observe that all the inequalities in (
A.5
) other than the first and the last hold outside of the zeros of
({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
or
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})
(namely, they do not require the functions to be evaluated at
p\in P_{2}
). So we have the following estimates for
w:=\log\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}\big)=\log\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}}
away from the zeros of
({\boldsymbol{\beta}})
or
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})
:

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta w

\displaystyle\geq e^{u_{2}}\left[2\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-1\big)+\big(e^{-(u_{2}-u_{3})}-1\big)\right]

\displaystyle\geq 2e^{u_{2}}\big(e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-1\big)

\displaystyle=2e^{u_{2}}(e^{w}-1)\geq 2e^{u_{2}}w,

where the second inequality is because
e^{u_{2}-u_{3}}\leq A_{3}=1
.
Since
w
achieves its maximum
\log A_{2}=0
on
P_{2}
, we may use Strong Maximum Principle in the same way as in Part
(1)
to conclude that
P_{2}=\Sigma
, or in other words,
\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}}=1
throughout
\Sigma
. By the assumption
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})\prec\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}),({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\}
, it follows that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
do not have any common zero and
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}
is nowhere zero.

In order to derive a contradiction, we consider the maximum
A_{2}^{\prime}
of
e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}/\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}
. By the second equation in (
A.3
) or (
A.4
), at any point where
A_{2}^{\prime}
is achieved (which cannot be a zero of
e^{u_{2}}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}
because
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})\prec({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1})
), we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\tfrac{1}{4}e^{-u_{2}}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})=e^{-u_{2}}\big(e^{u_{0}}+3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq e^{u_{0}-u_{2}}+e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}+2e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-3+e^{-(u_{2}-u_{3})}

\displaystyle=2e^{u_{1}-u_{2}}-2+e^{-(u_{2}-u_{3})}

\displaystyle\geq 2A_{2}^{\prime}-2+\tfrac{1}{A_{3}}=2A_{2}^{\prime}-1.

It follows that
A_{2}^{\prime}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
. But this is impossible because
\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}/\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}=1
on the zero locus of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}
(which is nonempty as observed at the beginning). This contradiction shows that
A_{2}=\cdots=A_{n}=1
cannot hold. Therefore, we have
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}<1
.

Step 4.
Deduce the required statement from the inequality
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}<1
that we just obtained:
since
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{n})\leq\cdots\leq({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2})\leq\min\big\{({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}),({\boldsymbol{\beta}})\big\}
, we have

\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{2}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}\\
=\begin{cases}0&\text{on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}=0\}$},\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}\big(\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{1}\|^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}}-1\big)\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\|^{2}(A_{2}-1)<0&\text{on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{2}\neq 0\}$};\end{cases}

\tfrac{1}{h_{0}}\partial_{z\bar{z}}^{2}\log h_{k}=\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k-1}\|^{2}-\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\|^{2}=\begin{cases}0&\text{on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}=0\}$},\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\|^{2}\big(\tfrac{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k-1}\|^{2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\|^{2}}-1\big)\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\|^{2}(A_{k}-1)<0&\text{on $\{{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{k}\neq 0\}$}.\end{cases}

Thus, we have proven statement
(2)
in the case
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\not\equiv 0
. To adapt this proof to
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\equiv 0
, we basically only need to erase all the terms containing
u_{0}
or
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|^{2}
and take into account some simplications along the way. For example, when
n\geq 4
, the equations that we started with become

\begin{cases}\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{1}-u_{2})=3e^{u_{1}}-3e^{u_{2}}+e^{u_{3}}\geq 3e^{u_{1}}-3^{u_{2}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{2}-u_{3})=-e^{u_{1}}+3e^{u_{2}}-3e^{u_{3}}+e^{u_{4}},\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{k-1}-u_{k})=-e^{u_{k-2}}+3e^{u_{k-1}}-3e^{u_{k}}+e^{u_{k+1}}\quad(4\leq k\leq n-1),\\
\tfrac{1}{4}\Delta(u_{n-1}-u_{n})=-e^{u_{n-2}}+3e^{u_{n-1}}-2e^{u_{n}}.\end{cases}

Putting
A_{k}:=\max_{\Sigma}e^{u_{k-1}-u_{k}}
for
k=2,\cdots,n
(only
A_{2}
differs from the above one), we may use Maximum Principle to deduce almost the same inequalities on
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}
as (
A.6
), except that the first one is replaced by the simpler inequality
A_{2}-1\leq 0
. Then, in the same way as above, we may show that
A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}<1
, whence the required statement follows.
∎
